Polyhedral compactifications of Bruhat-Tits buildings of quasi-reductive groups
Dorian Chanfi

Abstract
Given a quasi-reductive group G G over a local field k k , using Berkovich geometry, we exhibit a family of G ⁡ ( k ) G(k) -equivariant compactifications of the Bruhat-Tits building ℬ ⁡ ( G , k ) \mathcal{B}(G,k) , constructed and investigated by Solleveld and Lourenço. The compactification procedure consists in mapping the building in the analytification G an G^{\an} of G G , then composing this map with the projections from G an G^{\an} to its (in general non-compact) pseudo-flag varieties ( G / P ) an (G/P)^{\an} , for P P ranging among the pseudo-parabolic subgroups of G G . This generalises previous constructions of Berkovich, then Rémy, Thuillier and Werner. To define the embedding, we are led to giving a partial extension to the quasi-reductive context of results due to Rousseau on the functoriality of Bruhat-Tits buildings with respect to field extensions, which are of independent interest. Finally, we conclude by investigating the geometry at infinity of these compactifications. The boundaries are shown to be stratified, each stratum being equivariantly homeomorphic to the Bruhat-Tits building of the maximal quasi-reductive quotient of a pseudo-parabolic subgroup.



Introduction

This article is devoted to the construction of equivariant compactifications of Bruhat-Tits buildings of quasi-reductive groups. Pseudo-reductive and quasi-reductive groups were introduced with the aim of studying the combinatorics of rational points of arbitrary connected linear algebraic groups over arbitrary fields. They form a class of groups that generalises that of reductive groups, defined by Borel and Tits in
[
BoT78
]
and
[
Tit13
]
, then studied extensively by Conrad, Gabber and Prasad in
[
CGP15
]
and
[
CP16
]
.

Recall that a smooth connected affine group
G
over a field
k
is said to be reductive if
G_{\overline{k}}
contains no nontrivial smooth connected unipotent normal subgroups. More generally, the group
G
is said to be
pseudo-reductive
(resp.
quasi-reductive
) if it contains no nontrivial smooth connected unipotent normal (resp.
k
-split unipotent)
k
-subgroups.

When
k
is a perfect field, the unipotent radical
\mathcal{R}_{u}(G_{\overline{k}})
of
G_{\overline{k}}
, that is the maximal smooth connected unipotent normal subgroup of
G_{\overline{k}}
, is defined over
k
and
k
-split, hence the notions of reductivity, pseudo-reductivity, and quasi-reductivity coincide in that case. Over imperfect fields, however, the classes are distinct
[
CGP15
, Example 1.1.3]
and the role of pseudo-reductive groups in the study of linear algebraic groups is analogous to that of reductive groups in the case of perfect fields, in the sense that any smooth connected affine group over a field is an extension of a pseudo-reductive group by a unipotent group. Moreover, the structure theory of
[
CGP15
]
provides a good enough understanding of pseudo-reductive groups to allow one to extend results about smooth connected affine groups previously known over perfect fields to imperfect fields. For example, it allowed Conrad in
[
Con12
]
to extend previous results of Oesterlé’s
[
Oes84
, IV, 5.6]
and Borel’s
[
Bor63
, 5.8]
on the finiteness of Tamagawa numbers to connected groups over imperfect fields.

One upshot of the structure theory of pseudo-reductive groups developed in
[
CGP15
]
, notably the fact that many are
generalised standard

[
CGP15
, Theorem 5.1.1]
, is that the rational points of quasi-reductive groups over a field
k
are close to being direct products of groups of rational points of reductive groups (over extensions of
k
). This observation makes it plausible that, when
k
is valued, the study of rational points of quasi-reductive groups over
k
should be covered by some generalised version of Bruhat-Tits theory.

Recall that, in the papers
[
BT72
]
and
[
BT84
]
, Bruhat and Tits associated to each reductive group
G
over a valued field
k
an affine
building

\mathcal{B}(G,k)
endowed with an action of
G(k)
, commonly called its
Bruhat-Tits building
. An affine building is a polysimplicial complex covered by subcomplexes called
apartments
, each of which is isomorphic to a given Euclidean tiling, the apartment system being subject to rigid incidence relations
[
Bro89
, IV.1]
. Bruhat-Tits buildings provide rich geometries that encode a lot of the structure of reductive groups over non-archimedean valued fields, analogously to symmetric spaces for real non-compact Lie groups. They are endowed with a natural non-positively curved metric and their vertices parameterise maximal compact subgroups.

In
[
Sol18
]
, Solleveld associates a building to any quasi-reductive group
G
over a local field
k
a Euclidean building endowed with an action of
G(k)
that reduces to the Bruhat-Tits building when
G
is reductive. The main observation is, as previously mentioned, the fact that the group
G(k)
is, up to extension by a compact group and modification of Cartan subgroups, a finite direct product of groups of rational points of reductive groups
\prod_{i\in I}G^{\prime}_{i}(k^{\prime}_{i})
over finite extensions
k^{\prime}_{i}
of
k
. The building of
G
over
k
is then defined as the product
\prod_{i\in I}\mathcal{B}(G^{\prime}_{i},k^{\prime}_{i})
.
In
[
Lou21
]
, Lourenço extends Solleveld’s construction to quasi-reductive groups over discretely valued fields with perfect residue fields. The article closely follows the layout of
[
BT84
]
and one of its key new features is its extension of the arithmetic theory of Bruhat-Tits integral models to the quasi-reductive setting: To each bounded subset
\Omega\subset\mathcal{B}(G,k)
, we can associate integral models of
G
whose integral points are stabilisers of
\Omega
in
G(k)
(pointwise, global, and other variants
[
Lou21
, §§3.2, 4.2]
).

This brings us to the subject matter of our article, namely compactifications of Bruhat-Tits buildings of quasi-reductive groups over local fields. A motivating factor for compactifying Bruhat-Tits buildings is the analogy between affine buildings and symmetric spaces, whose compactifications have found applications in group theory – see eg.
[
BoS76
]
for applications to finiteness properties of
S
-arithmetic groups, or Mostow’s proof of his strong rigidity theorem
[
Mos73
]
– and arithmetic geometry – where arithmetic quotients of symmetric spaces can sometimes be interpreted as moduli spaces of algebraic varieties. An example of this analogy coming to fruition is provided by
[
Pra79
]
, in which the author proves a rigidity result for lattices in semisimple
p
-adic groups by letting geodesic compactifications
[
BH99
]
of Bruhat-Tits buildings play the role that geodesic compactifications of symmetric spaces played in
[
Mos73
]
.

In the following, we shall specifically concern ourselves with polyhedral compactifications of Bruhat-Tits buildings, studied under various guises in, among others,
[
Lan96
]
,
[
Wer07
]
, and
[
RTW10
]
. These compactifications fit in an ordered family (for the domination order) indexed by conjugacy classes of pseudo-parabolic subgroups. Each space admits a polysimplicial structure and its boundary naturally decomposes as a union of Bruhat-Tits buildings of smaller rank, much like in Satake’s construction of compactifications of symmetric spaces
[
Sat60
]
. The analogy between both constructions is given a thorough treatment in
[
Wer07
]
and
[
RTW12
]
. In
[
GR06
]
, the authors establish that the vertices of the maximal compactification of
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
parameterise closed amenable subgroups of
G(k)
with Zariski-connected closure that are maximal for these properties. This suggests that a similar result should hold for an arbitrary quasi-reductive group
G
over a local field.

In this article, we take the analytic-geometric point of view on compactifications of Bruhat-Tits buildings of quasi-reductive groups over local fields, as adopted in
[
Wer04
]
and
[
RTW10
]
.

Our main goal is to establish the following theorem, which synthesises most of the results of Sections 2 to 4.

Theorem A
.

Let
G
be a quasi-reductive group over a local field
k
. Denote by
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
the Bruhat-Tits building of
G
over
k
. Let
P
be a pseudo-parabolic subgroup of
G
.

1.

There exists a continuous
G(k)
-equivariant map

\vartheta_{P}:\mathcal{B}(G,k)\to(G/P)^{\an},

where
\an
is the Berkovich analytic space associated to
G/P
.

2.

The map
\vartheta_{P}
has relatively compact image and is an embedding if and only if
P
is of non-degenerate type
1
1

1

See Definition
33
.
. The homeomorphism type of the closure of the image depends only on the conjugacy class of
P
.

3.

Whenever
P
is of non-degenerate type, the closure of the image of
\vartheta_{P}
admits a stratification by affine buildings. Precisely, the compactification
\overline{\vartheta_{P}(\mathcal{B}(G,k))}
is
G(k)
-equivariantly homeomorphic to a disjoint union

\overline{\vartheta_{P}(\mathcal{B}(G,k))}=\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\subset G\end{subarray}}\mathcal{B}(Q/\mathcal{R}_{us,k}(Q),k),

where
Q
ranges among the
J
-relevant pseudo-parabolic subgroups
2
2

2

See Definition
58
.
of
G
.
For each
J
-relevant pseudo-parabolic subgroup
Q
of
G
, the group
Q(k)
is the stabiliser of the stratum
\mathcal{B}(Q/\mathcal{R}_{us,k}(Q),k)
.

The proof loosely follows the structure of
[
RTW10
]
, but substantial new difficulties must be overcome.
The first difficulty is the construction of the map
\vartheta_{P}
, which relies on the existence of a canonical continuous
G(k)
-equivariant map

\vartheta:\mathcal{B}(G,k)\to G^{\an}.

The idea in
[
Ber90
]
and
[
RTW10
]
was to associate to each point
x\in\mathcal{B}(G,k)
an affinoid subgroup
G_{x}
of
G^{\an}
, which contains the information about the stabilisers
G(K)_{x_{K}}
of
x_{K}\in\mathcal{B}(G,K)
for each non-archimedean extension
K/k
. This makes extensive use of Rousseau’s theory of the functoriality of Bruhat-Tits buildings with respect to field extensions, developed in
[
Rou77
]
.
The Shilov boundary of
G_{x}
turns out to be a single point, which we denote by
\vartheta(x)
. In other words, each function
f\in\mathcal{O}(G_{x})
achieves its maximum at
\vartheta(x)
.
This mapping is reminiscent of the compactification in
[
GR06
]
, which associated to each special point its stabiliser in
G(k)
, viewed as a point in the Chabauty space of closed subgroups of
G
. By associating to a point the data of all stabilisers
G(K)_{x_{K}}
, for all non-archimedean extensions, the analytic construction can separate not just all special points, but in fact all points in the buildings.

In our situation, due to technical limitations, we can only carry out this construction for points that become special after a finite Galois extension of
k
, which we dub
virtually special
. Indeed, the theory of Bruhat-Tits buildings for quasi-reductive groups over non-archimedean fields only associates buildings to groups over local fields for
[
Sol18
]
and discretely valued fields with perfect residue fields for
[
Lou21
]
, which prevents the use of arbitrarily ramified extensions as in
[
Ber90
]
. These virtually special points are then shown to be dense, which allows one to extend the map
\vartheta
from the set of virtually special points to the whole building. This spans the entirety of the second section.

The second difficulty is to establish that
\vartheta_{P}
is open with relatively compact image, and injective for suitable
P
. The proofs of these various assertions form the bulk of Section
3
. The main observation is the fact that the restriction of
\vartheta_{P}
to an apartment lies in the analytification of an affine scheme, which admits an interpretation in terms of seminorms over the algebra of regular functions. An explicit description of the restriction of the map
\vartheta_{P}
to an apartment in terms of seminorms, proved in Proposition
32
, plays a central role in most arguments. Note that the relative compactness is somewhat surprising in this case as, in contrast with the reductive case, the target space
(G/P)^{\an}
of
\vartheta_{P}
is in general not compact. Indeed, by
[
Ber90
, Theorem 3.4.8 (ii)]
, the analytification
(G/P)^{\an}
is compact if and only if
G/P
is proper, that is if and only if
P
is a parabolic subgroup. However, quasi-reductive groups do not admit proper parabolic subgroups unless they are reductive
[
CGP15
, Proposition 3.4.9]
.

The last point is the description of the boundary of
\vartheta_{P}(\mathcal{B}(G,k))
. Here, we rely on previous work by Charignon on stratified compactifications of affine buildings. In
[
Cha10
]
, the author associates to each affine building
\mathcal{I}
, modeled on an affine Coxeter complex
\Sigma
, and each fan
\mathcal{F}
in the direction
\vec{\Sigma}
of
\Sigma
subject to some compatibility relations
[
Cha10
, 2.3]
a canonical compactification
\overline{I}^{\mathcal{F}}
of
\mathcal{I}
, which we call a polyhedral compactification. We establish in Proposition
50
that the compactification
\overline{\vartheta_{P}(\mathcal{B}(G,k))}
is a polyhedral compactification in that sense, with respect to a fan that can be described explicitly in terms of the type of
P
. The remainder of Section
4
is devoted to giving a group-theoretic description of the strata, which are constructed in terms of pure building geometry in
[
Cha10
]
. This culminates in Theorem
60
.

To conclude, we give a brief description of Section
1
and its relation to the rest of the article. It is devoted to the proof of the following theorem:

Theorem B
.

The construction of the Bruhat-Tits building
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
is functorial in the discretely valued field
k
with perfect residue field.

This theorem plays a central role in the article as it allows one to make sense of the notion of virtually special points, which lies at the heart of the entire construction.

The precise statement can be found in Theorem
11
and generalises, in the setting of discretely valued fields with perfect residue fields, previous results of Rousseau’s on the functoriality of Bruhat-Tits buildings of reductive groups with respect to field extensions
[
Rou77
]
. The proof relies heavily on the combinatorial theory of buildings associated to valuated root groop data developed in
[
BT72
]
.

Structure of the article

The present article splits into four sections. Section
1
, dealing with the functoriality of Bruhat-Tits buildings with respect to field extensions, may be taken as a blackbox for the remainder of the text. Section
2
constructs a continuous map

\vartheta:\mathcal{B}(G,k)\to G^{\an}.

Section
3
establishes that, for suitable pseudo-parabolic subgroups
P
, the composition

\vartheta_{P}:\mathcal{B}(G,k)\overset{\vartheta}{\to}G^{\an}\to(G/P)^{\an}

is a compactification map.
Section
4
studies the geometry at infinity of the compactification and its strata. Finally, the Appendix studies the relation between the buildings associated to centralisers of tori in
G
and the Bruhat-Tits building of
G
.

Notations and preliminaries

Unless otherwise specified, algebraic group over
k
will mean smooth affine group scheme of finite type over
k
. We will usually adhere to the following notations.

Algebraic groups

In the following, we denote by
k
a field and
G
a connected algebraic
k
-group.

•

We denote by
\mathcal{R}_{u,k}(G)
the
k
-unipotent radical of
G
, that is its maximal smooth connected
k
-unipotent normal subgroup.

•

Recall that a unipotent
k
-group is said to be
split
if it admits a composition series whose successive factors are isomorphic to
\mathbb{G}_{a,k}
. Denote by
\mathcal{R}_{us,k}(G)
the
k
-split unipotent radical of
G
, that is its maximal smooth connected
k
-split unipotent normal subgroup.

•

We say that a connected algebraic
k
-group is
pseudo-reductive
if
\mathcal{R}_{u,k}(G)=1
and
quasi-reductive
if
\mathcal{R}_{us,k}(G)=1
.

•

If
G
is endowed with an action of split torus
S
, then we denote by
\Phi(G,S)
the set of nonzero weights of the adjoint action of
S
on the Lie algebra of
G
.

In the following definitions, we assume that
G
is quasi-reductive and that
S
is a maximal split torus in
G
.

•

\Phi=\Phi(G,S)
is a root system called the (relative or
k
-) root system of
G
.

•

W(G,S)=N_{G}(S)/Z_{G}(S)
is the Weyl group of
\Phi
, a finite constant group scheme over
k
.

•

X^{*}(S)=\Hom_{\Grp}(S,\mathbb{G}_{m})
is the group of characters of
S
.

•

X_{*}(S)=\Hom_{\Grp}(\mathbb{G}_{m},S)
is the group of cocharacters of
S
.

•

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:X^{*}(S)\times X_{*}(S)\to\mathbb{Z}
is the canonical pairing: For
\alpha\in X^{*}(S),\lambda\in X_{*}(S)
, we have
\alpha\circ\lambda:t\mapsto t^{\langle\alpha,\lambda\rangle}
.

•

We set

V(G,S)=\frac{X_{*}(S)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}}{\Span_{\mathbb{R}}(\Phi(G,S))^{\perp}}\simeq\frac{X_{*}(S)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}}{X_{*}(Z(G))\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}}\simeq X_{*}(S^{\prime})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R},

where
S^{\prime}=(S\cap\mathcal{D}(G))^{0}_{\red}
.

•

We set

V^{*}(G,S)=\Span_{\mathbb{R}}(\Phi(G,S))\subset X^{*}(S)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}.

•

The canonical pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
induces a perfect pairing, which we still denote by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:V^{*}(G,S)\times V(G,S)\to\mathbb{R}.

•

Given a cocharacter
\lambda
of
G
, we let
P_{G}(\lambda)
,
U_{G}(\lambda)
, and
Z_{G}(\lambda)
be the subgroups associated with
\lambda

[
Mil17
, 13.d]
, characterised functorially by the condition that, for each
k
-algebra
A
, we have

\begin{array}[]{ccl}P_{G}(\lambda)(A)&=&\{g\in G(A),\lambda(t)g\lambda(t)^{-1}\text{ has a limit as }t\to 0\}\\
U_{G}(\lambda)(A)&=&\{g\in G(A),\lambda(t)g\lambda(t)^{-1}\underset{t\to 0}{\longrightarrow}1\}\\
Z_{G}(\lambda)(A)&=&\{g\in G(A),\forall t\in A^{\times},\lambda(t)g\lambda(t)^{-1}=g\}\end{array}.

•

A
pseudo-parabolic
subgroup of
G
is a closed subgroup
P
of the form
P=P_{G}(\lambda)\mathcal{R}_{u,k}(G)
. If
G
is quasi-reductive, then we have
P=P_{G}(\lambda)
and
\mathcal{R}_{us,k}(P)=U_{G}(\lambda)
.

Affine spaces

We denote by
A
a real affine space with direction
V
.

•

If
F
is an affine subspace of
A
, we denote by
\vec{F}
its direction.

•

If
X
is a subset of
V
, we denote by
X^{\vee}=\{\alpha\in V^{*},\forall x\in X,\langle\alpha,x\rangle\geq 0\}
its dual cone.

•

If
X
is a subset of
V
, we denote by
X^{0}=\{\alpha\in V^{*},\forall x\in X,\langle\alpha,x\rangle=0\}
its annihilator.

Buildings and Bruhat-Tits theory

•

We call a non-archimedean field any field that is complete with respect to a non-trivial non-archimedean absolute value.

•

We call a local field any non-archimedean field that is locally compact.

•

If
G
is quasi-reductive over a complete discretely valued field with a perfect residue field
k
, then we denote by
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
its (reduced) Bruhat-Tits building. If
K
is a finitely ramified extension of
k
, then we will also write
\mathcal{B}(G,K)
for
\mathcal{B}(G_{K},K)
.

•

If
S
is a maximal split torus in
G
, we denote by
A(G,S)
the apartment of
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
corresponding to
S
. It is an affine space under
V(G,S)
.

•

If
\Omega\subset A(G,S)
is a subset of an apartment, we denote by

\hat{P}_{\Omega}=\{g\in G(k),\forall x\in\Omega,gx=x\}

its pointwise stabiliser.

•

Recall that, given an affine building
X
modeled on an affine Coxeter complex
\Sigma
with direction
\vec{\Sigma}
and vectorial Weyl group
W
, any
W
-invariant inner product on
\vec{\Sigma}
extends to a unique metric on
X

[
Bro89
, §3A]
. If
X=\mathcal{B}(G,k)
is the Bruhat-Tits building of a group
G
, then the action of
G(k)
on
X
is isometric. The topology determined by this metric does not depend on the specific inner product on
\vec{\Sigma}
. All affine buildings in the text will be endowed with this topology and all metrics on buildings will be assumed to be of the type described above.

1
Functoriality for Bruhat-Tits buildings

In this section, we give an account of functoriality of Bruhat-Tits buildings of quasi-reductive groups with respect to field extensions, following the general outline of
[
Rou77
]
.

Specifically, our goal is to establish the following two results, which will be sufficient for our purposes:

1.

Given a quasi-reductive group
G
over a discretely valued field
(k,\omega)
with perfect residue field and given a discretely valued Galois extension
K/k
, there exists an action of
\Gal(K/k)
on
\mathcal{B}(G,K)
via simplicial automorphisms.

2.

There exists a system of equivariant maps

p_{L/K}:\mathcal{B}(G,K)\to\mathcal{B}(G,L)

indexed by towers of finite separable extensions
k\subset K\subset L
such that:

(i)

The restriction of
p_{L/K}
to any apartment
A(G_{K},S)\subset\mathcal{B}(G,K)
is an affine isomorphism onto an apartment
A(G_{L},T)
for some maximal split torus
T\subset G_{L}
containing
S_{L}
.

(ii)

For any
k\subset K\subset L\subset M
, we have

p_{M/K}=p_{M/L}\circ p_{L/K}.

(iii)

Whenever
L/K
is Galois, there is an inclusion

p_{L/K}(\mathcal{B}(G,K))\subset(\mathcal{B}(G,L))^{\Gal(L/K)}.

In order to prove the general theorem, we shall first need to prove some special cases and variants of it. The proofs will vary in length and complexity, but will always follow the same template.

1.

Observe that, under the assumption that
p_{L/K}
is equivariant and satisfies (i), the linear part of the restriction of
p_{L/K}
to an apartment
A(G_{K},S)
will be the map induced by base change on cocharacters.

2.

The assumptions of equivariance and affineness then reduce the proof of uniqueness to checking that a given special point
x
can only have one image.

3.

Use characteristics of the special case at hand to exhibit the image
y
of
x
. Usually, this will involve studying the action of
G(K)_{x}
on
\mathcal{B}(G,L)
and its fixed points.

4.

Prove that the affine map
A(G_{K},S)\to A(G_{L},T)
mapping
x
to
y
with the linear part exhibited above extends to a
G(K)
-equivariant map. This will sometimes be proved explicitly, and sometimes be dealt with using Bruhat and Tits’ theory of descent for valuated root data
[
BT72
, §9]
, which aims specifically at producing such statements.

We fix a quasi-reductive group
G
over a discretely valued field
(k,\omega)
with perfect residue field, normalised so that
\omega(k^{\times})=\mathbb{Z}
. We fix, once and for all, an algebraic closure
\overline{k}
of
k
which will contain all the extensions to be discussed below. Each algebraic extension
K/k
will be endowed with the unique valuation that extends that of
k
.

1.1
Galois action on the building

We start off by proving that, for any Galois extension
K/k
of discretely valued fields with perfect residue fields (eg.
K/k
is finite or unramified), the Galois group
\Gal(K/k)
acts on the building
\mathcal{B}(G,K)
.

Theorem 1
.

Let
K
and
L
be two discretely valued separable extensions of
k
with perfect residue fields and
\sigma:K\to L
be a morphism of
k
-algebras. Assume that
G_{K}
and
G_{L}
have the same relative rank. Then, there exists a unique map

p_{\sigma}:\mathcal{B}(G,K)\to\mathcal{B}(G,L)

such that:

1.

For each maximal
K
-split torus
S\subset G_{K}
, the map
p_{\sigma}
restricts to an affine isomorphism of
A(G_{K},S)
onto
A(G_{L},S^{\sigma})
.

2.

For each
x\in\mathcal{B}(G,K)
and
g\in G(K)
, we have
p_{\sigma}(gx)=\sigma(g)p_{\sigma}(x)
.

Moreover, affine roots in
A(G_{K},S)
are mapped to affine roots in
A(G_{L},S^{\sigma})
.
Lastly, if
K,L,M
are three extensions of
k
as above and
\sigma:K\to L
and
\tau:L\to M
are two
k
-morphisms, then, we have
p_{\tau\circ\sigma}=p_{\tau}\circ p_{\sigma}
.

Proof.

We begin with the proof of uniqueness. The key point is the observation that the compatibility condition on
p_{\sigma}
with respect to the
G(K)
-action together with the condition that
p_{\sigma}
be affine on apartments imply that
p_{\sigma}
is determined by the image of single special vertex in
\mathcal{B}(G,K)
. The uniqueness of the image of the latter then follows from condition 2.

(Uniqueness)
Let a map
p_{\sigma}
be given satisfying conditions 1 and 2. Fix a maximal
K
-split torus
S\subset G_{K}
. Let
x
be a special vertex in
A(G_{K},S)
. Because the Weyl group
W(G_{K},S)(K)
acts without nonzero fixed vectors on the direction
V(G_{K},S)
, the point
x
is the unique fixed point for the action of the stabiliser
N_{G_{K}}(S)(K)_{x}
on
A(G_{K},S)
. Moreover, because
N_{G_{K}}(S)(K)_{x}
acts on
A(G_{K},S)
via its bounded quotient
N_{G_{K}}(S)(K)_{x}/Z(G)(K)
, condition 2 implies that its image
\sigma(N_{G_{K}}(S)(K)_{x})
act on
A(G_{L},S^{\sigma})
via its bounded quotient
\sigma(N_{G_{K}}(S)(K)_{x})/Z(G)(L)
. Therefore, there exists
x^{\prime}\in A(G_{L},S^{\sigma})
that is fixed under
\sigma(N_{G_{K}}(S)(K)_{x})
. Lastly, because the image of the subgroup
N_{G_{K}}(S)(K)_{x}
in the linear group of
V(G_{K},S)
is the full Weyl group
W(G_{K},S)
, condition 2 implies that the image of
\sigma(N_{G_{K}}(S)(K)_{x})
in
GL(V(G_{L},S^{\sigma}))
is the full Weyl group
W(G_{L},S^{\sigma})(L)
and therefore acts on
V(G_{L},S^{\sigma})
without fixing nonzero vectors. The point
x^{\prime}
is thus a special vertex and the unique fixed point of the apartment
A(G_{L},S^{\sigma})
under the action of
\sigma(N_{G_{K}}(S)(K)_{x})
.

We now characterise the linear part of the restriction of
p_{\sigma}
to the apartment
A(G_{K},S)
. Let
\varpi
be a uniformiser of the valuation ring
k^{\circ}
. For each cocharacter
\lambda\in\Phi(G_{K},S)^{\vee}
, the element
s=\lambda(\varpi^{-1})\in S(K)
acts on
A(G_{K},S)
by translation by the vector
\nu(s)\in V(G_{K},S)
satisfying

\forall a\in\Phi(G_{K},S),\langle a,\nu(s)\rangle=-\omega(\varpi^{-\langle a,\lambda\rangle})=\langle a,\lambda\rangle.

In other words, the vector
\nu(s)
is the image of
\lambda
in
V(G_{K},S)
.
By assumption, we have

p_{\sigma}(s\cdot x)=\sigma(s)\cdot x^{\prime}=\sigma(\lambda)(\varpi)\cdot x^{\prime},

denoting by
\sigma:X_{*}(S)\overset{\sim}{\to}X_{*}(S^{\sigma})
the isomorphism given by pulling back cocharacters along the map
\sigma^{*}:\Spec L\to\Spec K.
This map extends
\mathbb{R}
-linearly and passes to the quotient to a linear isomorphism

\sigma:V(G_{K},S)\to V(G_{L},S^{\sigma}).

The same argument as before then implies that
\sigma(s)
acts on
A(G_{L},S^{\sigma})
by translation by
\sigma(\lambda)
.
In other words, for each cocharacter
\lambda\in X_{*}(S)
, we have

p_{\sigma}(x+\lambda)=x^{\prime}+\sigma(\lambda).

We deduce from condition 1 and the fact that the images of the cocharacters span
V(G_{K},S)
that, for each
v\in V(G_{K},S)
, we have

p_{\sigma}(x+v)=x^{\prime}+\sigma(v).

The linear part of
p_{\sigma}
is therefore the isomorphism
\sigma:V(G_{K},S)\to V(G_{L},S^{\sigma})
induced by pulling back cocharacters.

We deduce from the above considerations the uniqueness of the map
p_{\sigma}
. Indeed, if
p_{\sigma}
and
p^{\prime}_{\sigma}
satisfy conditions 1 and 2, then they coincide over
A(G_{K},S)
. Because
\mathcal{B}(G,K)=G(K)\cdot A(G_{K},S)
, condition 2 implies that
p_{\sigma}
and
p^{\prime}_{\sigma}
coincide everywhere.

(Existence)
We now prove that the conditions brought forth above do define a map from
\mathcal{B}(G,K)
to
\mathcal{B}(G,L)
. The idea is to check that the map

\begin{array}[]{rcl}G(K)\times A(G_{K},S)&\longrightarrow&G(L)\times A(G_{L},S^{\sigma})\\
(g,x+v)&\longmapsto&(\sigma(g),x^{\prime}+\sigma(v))\end{array}

passes to the quotient, which amounts to proving that, for each
z\in A(G_{K},S)
, the stabliser
\hat{P}_{z}
is mapped to
\hat{P}_{p_{\sigma}(z)}
by
\sigma
.
Fix an apartment
A(G_{K},S)
as before and a special vertex
x\in A(G_{K},S)
. Denote by
x^{\prime}
the unique fixed point of
A(G_{L},S^{\sigma})
under the subgroup
\sigma(N_{G_{K}}(S)(K)_{x})
. We define
p_{\sigma}
on
A(G_{K},S)
as the affine isomorphism

p_{\sigma}:\begin{array}[]{rcl}A(G_{K},S)&\longrightarrow&A(G_{L},S^{\sigma})\\
x+v&\longmapsto&x^{\prime}+\sigma(v)\end{array}.

Then, for each
n\in N_{G_{K}}(S)(K)
and each
z=x+v\in A(G_{K},S)
, we have

p_{\sigma}(nz)=\sigma(n)p_{\sigma}(z).

(1)

Indeed, by construction, if
n\in N_{G_{K}}(S)(K)_{x}
, we have

p_{\sigma}(nz)=p_{\sigma}(x+nvn^{-1})=x^{\prime}+\sigma(n)\sigma(v)\sigma(n)^{-1}=\sigma(n)\cdot(x^{\prime}+\sigma(v))=\sigma(n)p_{\sigma}(z).

Moreover, if
n\in Z_{G_{K}}(S)(K)
, then
nz=x+v+\nu(n)
, where
\nu(n)\in V(G_{K},S)
satisfies
\langle a,\nu(n)\rangle=-\omega(a(n))
for each
a\in\Phi
. Then, we have

p_{\sigma}(nz)=p_{\sigma}(x+v+\nu(n))=x^{\prime}+\sigma(v)+\sigma(\nu(n)).

Now, by a previous observation, we have

\langle\sigma(a),\sigma(\nu(n))\rangle=\langle a,\nu(n)\rangle=-\omega(a(n))=-\omega(\sigma(a)(\sigma(n)))

for each
a\in\Phi
, hence
\sigma(\nu(n))=\nu(\sigma(n))
and finally

p_{\sigma}(nz)=\sigma(n)(x^{\prime}+\sigma(v))=\sigma(n)p_{\sigma}(z).

Because
N_{G_{K}}(S)(K)
is spanned by
N_{G_{K}}(S)(K)_{x}
and
Z_{G_{K}}(S)(K)
, we get the result we claimed.

To conclude, we now only have to prove that, denoting by

\varphi^{x}=(\varphi^{x}_{a})_{a\in\Phi}\text{ (resp. }\varphi^{x^{\prime}}=(\varphi^{x^{\prime}}_{a})_{a\in\Phi^{\sigma}}\text{)}

the valuation of the root data

(Z_{G_{K}}(S)(K),(U_{a}(K))_{a\in\Phi})\text{ (resp. }(Z_{G_{K}}(S^{\sigma})(K),(U_{\sigma(a)}(K))_{a\in\Phi})\text{ )},

associated to
x
(resp.
x^{\prime}
) we have for each
a\in\Phi
and
u\in U_{a}(K)\setminus\{1\}

\varphi_{a}^{x}(u)=\varphi^{x^{\prime}}_{\sigma(a)}(\sigma(u)).

Let
a\in\Phi
and
u\in U_{a}(K)\setminus\{1\}
. Recall from
[
BT72
, 6.2.12 b)]
that, if
(u^{\prime},u^{\prime\prime})\in U_{-a}(K)^{2}
is the unique pair
[
BT72
, 6.1.2 (2)]
such that
m(u)=u^{\prime}uu^{\prime\prime}\in N_{G_{K}}(S)(K)
, then
\varphi_{a}^{x}(u)
is the only
k\in\mathbb{R}
such that the fixed locus of
m(u)
in
A(G_{K},S)
is the hyperplane

\partial\alpha_{a,k}^{x}=\{z\in A(G_{K},S),a(z-x)+k=0\}.

Now observe that

\sigma(m(u))=\sigma(u^{\prime})\sigma(u)\sigma(u^{\prime\prime})\in N_{G_{L}}(S^{\sigma})(L).

Because
\sigma(u)
lies in
U_{\sigma(a)}(L)\setminus\{1\}
whereas
\sigma(u^{\prime})
and
\sigma(u^{\prime\prime})
lie in
U_{-\sigma(a)}(L)
, the aforementioned uniqueness property implies

\sigma(m(u))=m(\sigma(u)).

Finally, recall that the equivariance property (
1
) implies that the fixed locus of
m(\sigma(u))
is the image under
p_{\sigma}
of the fixed locus of
m(u)
, that is the hyperplane

\partial\alpha_{\sigma(a),\varphi_{a}^{x}(u)}^{x^{\prime}}=\{z\in A(G_{L},S^{\sigma}),\sigma(a)(z-x^{\prime})+\varphi_{a}^{x}(u)=0\}.

In other words, we have

\varphi_{\sigma(a)}^{x^{\prime}}(\sigma(u))=\varphi_{a}^{x}(u).

Note that this proves that
p_{\sigma}
maps the affine roots of
A(G_{K},S)
to those of
A(G_{L},S^{\sigma})
.

We deduce that, for any bounded subset
\Omega\subset A(G_{K},S)
, we have

\sigma(U_{\Omega})=U_{p_{\sigma}(\Omega)}.

Moreover, by (
1
), we have

\sigma(\hat{N}_{\Omega})=\hat{N}_{\sigma(\Omega)},

where
\hat{N}_{\Omega}=\{n\in N_{G_{K}}(S)(K),\forall x\in\Omega,n\cdot x=x\}
is the pointwise stabiliser of
\Omega
. Because
\hat{P}_{\Omega}=\hat{N}_{\Omega}U_{\Omega}
, by
[
BT72
, 7.1.8, 7.4.4]
, we conclude that

\sigma(\hat{P}_{\Omega})=\hat{P}_{p_{\sigma}(\Omega)}.

Consequently, for all pairs
(g,x),(h,y)\in G(K)\times A(G_{K},S)
, there exists
n\in N_{G_{K}}(S)(K)
such that
nx=y
and
n^{-1}h^{-1}g\in\hat{P}_{x}
if and only if there exists
n^{\sigma}\in N_{G_{L}}(S^{\sigma})(L)
such that
n^{\sigma}p_{\sigma}(x)=p_{\sigma}(y)
and
(n^{\sigma})^{-1}\sigma(h^{-1})\sigma(g)\in\hat{P}_{p_{\sigma}(x)}
. In other words, the injection

\begin{array}[]{rcl}G(K)\times A(G_{K},S)&\longrightarrow&G(L)\times A(G_{L},S^{\sigma})\\
(g,x)&\longmapsto&(\sigma(g),p_{\sigma}(x))\end{array}

descends to an injection from
\mathcal{B}(G,K)
to
\mathcal{B}(G,L)
, which we still denote by
p_{\sigma}
, such that, for each
g\in G(K)
and
x\in\mathcal{B}(G,K)
, we have

p_{\sigma}(gx)=\sigma(g)p_{\sigma}(x).

∎

Note that the proof of Theorem
1
yields the following relation between valuated root data.

Proposition 2
.

Let
K
and
L
be two discretely valued separable extensions of
k
with perfect residue fields and
\sigma:K\to L
be a morphism of
k
-algebras such that
G_{K}
and
G_{L}
have the same relative rank.
Let
S\subset G_{K}
be a maximal
K
-split torus and
x
be a point in the apartment
A(G_{K},S)
. Then, for each
a\in\Phi(G_{K},S)
, we have

\varphi_{a}^{x}\circ\sigma^{-1}=\varphi_{\sigma(a)}^{p_{\sigma(x)}}.

We also deduce the following property relating the polysimplicial structures of
\mathcal{B}(G,K)
and
\mathcal{B}(G,L)
under the assumptions of Theorem
1
.

Proposition 3
.

Let
K
and
L
be two discretely valued separable extensions of
k
with perfect residue fields and
\sigma:K\to L
be a morphism of
k
-algebras such that
G_{K}
and
G_{L}
have the same relative rank (eg.
G_{K}
is pseudo-split).
Let
S\subset G_{K}
be a maximal
K
-split torus. Then, the embedding
p_{\sigma}
maps vertices (resp. special vertices) in
\mathcal{B}(G,K)
to vertices (resp. special vertices) in
\mathcal{B}(G,L)
.

Proof.

This statement follows directly from the fact that
p_{\sigma}
maps affine roots in
A(G_{K},S)
to affine roots in
A(G_{L},S^{\sigma})
and the equality between the relative ranks of
G_{K}
and
G_{L}
.
∎

Remark 4
.

Note that, in general, the image of a higher-dimensional facet in
A(G_{K},S)
will not be a facet in
A(G_{L},S^{\sigma})
.

Finally, Theorem
1
also immediately yields the existence of a Galois action on the Bruhat-Tits building.

Theorem 5
.

Let
K/k
be a Galois extension of discretely valued fields with perfect residue fields and
\sigma\in\Gal(K/k)
. Then, there exists a unique map

p_{\sigma}:\mathcal{B}(G,K)\to\mathcal{B}(G,K)

such that:

1.

For each maximal
K
-split torus
S\subset G_{K}
, the map
p_{\sigma}
restricts to an affine isomorphism of
A(G_{K},T)
onto
A(G_{K},S^{\sigma})
in such a way that affine roots in
A(G_{K},S)
are mapped to affine roots in
A(G_{K},S^{\sigma})
. In particular, the map
p_{\sigma}
is simplicial.

2.

For each
x\in\mathcal{B}(G,K)
and
g\in G(K)
, we have
p_{\sigma}(gx)=\sigma(g)p_{\sigma}(x)
.

Moreover, for each
\sigma,\tau\in\Gal(K/k)
, we have
p_{\sigma\tau}=p_{\sigma}\circ p_{\tau}
.

1.2
Functorial system of embeddings

Our second, more substantial, task is to establish the existence of a canonical system of embeddings indexed by discretely valued separable extensions of
k
with perfect residue fields.

We first prove the existence of canonical embeddings
p_{L/K}
for two classes of extensions, mirroring the two steps of the general construction of the Bruhat-Tits building. First, we treat the case where
L
is the strict Henselisation of
K
, then we treat the case where
G_{K}
is quasi-split and
G_{L}
is pseudo-split.

Both proofs rely on descent theory for valuations of root data, as exposed in
[
BT72
, §9]
, which provides a general framework for constructing equivariant embeddings of buildings.

The case of the strict henselisation

The first case that we treat is that of a strict Henselisation
K^{\nr}/K
. The situation is as nice as it could be, in the sense that the canonical embedding
\mathcal{B}(G,K)\to\mathcal{B}(G,K^{\nr})
is a simplicial map and identifies
\mathcal{B}(G,K)
with the fixed locus of
\mathcal{B}(G,K^{\nr})
under the action of
\Gal(K^{\nr}/K)
.

The proof is a direct consequence of the construction of Bruhat-Tits buildings which relies on unramified descent in the general case
[
BT84
, §5]
,
[
Lou21
, Section 4.4]
.

Proposition 6
.

Let
K
be a discretely valued separable extension of
k
with perfect residue field and
K^{\nr}
be its strict henselisation. There exists a unique map

p_{K^{\nr}/K}:\mathcal{B}(G,K)\to\mathcal{B}(G,K^{\nr})

such that

1.

For each maximal
K
-split torus
S\subset G_{K}
, there exists a maximal
K^{\nr}
-split torus
S^{\nr}\subset G_{K^{\nr}}
such that
S_{K^{\nr}}\subset S^{\nr}
and such that the map
p_{K^{\nr}/K}
restricts to an affine isomorphism of
A(G_{K},S)
onto an affine subspace of
A(G_{K^{\nr}},S^{\nr})
.

2.

The map
p_{K^{\nr}/K}
is
G(K)
-equivariant.

3.

We have the inclusion
p_{K^{\nr}/K}(\mathcal{B}(G,K))\subset\mathcal{B}(G,K^{\nr})^{\Gal(K^{\nr}/K)}
.

Moreover, for each maximal
K
-split torus
S\subset G_{K}
and each maximal
K^{\nr}
-split torus
S^{\nr}\subset G_{K^{\nr}}
such that
S_{K^{\nr}}\subset S^{\nr}
, the affine roots in
A(G_{K},S)
correspond to the restrictions of the affine roots in
A(G_{K^{\nr}},S^{\nr})
, and we have the equality
p_{{K^{\nr}}/K}(\mathcal{B}(G,K))=\mathcal{B}(G,{K^{\nr}})^{\Gal({K^{\nr}}/K)}
.

Proof.

Let
S
be a maximal
K
-split torus in
G_{K}
. Let
\Gamma
be the Galois group
\Gal(K^{\nr}/K)
. By
[
Lou21
, Lemme 4.8]
, there exists a maximal
K^{\nr}
-split torus
S^{\nr}
in
G_{K^{\nr}}
that contains
S_{K^{\nr}}
and is defined over
K
. Denote by
\Phi=\Phi(G_{K},S)
and
\Phi^{\nr}=\Phi(G_{K^{\nr}},S^{\nr})
the corresponding root systems. The apartment
A(G_{K^{\nr}},S^{\nr})
is then stable under the action of
\Gamma
and admits a fixed point
y
corresponding to a
\Gamma
-invariant valuation
\varphi^{\nr}
of the root group datum
(Z_{G_{K^{\nr}}}(S^{\nr})(K^{\nr}),(U_{a^{\nr}}(K^{\nr}))_{a^{\nr}\in\Phi^{\nr}})
.
It then follows from
[
Lou21
, Théorème 4.9]
that the valuation
\varphi^{\nr}
descends to a valuation
\varphi
of the root group datum
(Z_{G_{K}}(S)(K),(U_{a}(K))_{a\in\Phi})
, corresponding to a point
x\in A(G_{K},S)
. By
[
BT72
, 9.1.17]
, there is then a
G(K)
-equivariant isometry

p_{K^{\nr}/K}:\mathcal{B}(G,K)\to\mathcal{B}(G,K^{\nr})

such that
p_{K^{\nr}/K}(x)=y
. We then have
p_{K^{\nr}/K}(A(G_{K},S))=A(G_{K^{\nr}},S^{\nr})^{\Gamma}
and, by
G(K)
-equivariance, the inclusion
p_{K^{\nr}/K}(\mathcal{B}(G,K))\subset\mathcal{B}(G,K^{\nr})^{\Gamma}
, which completes the proof of the existence statement. By
[
BT72
, 9.2.14 and 9.2.15]
, the map
p_{K^{\nr}/K}
is in fact the unique
G(K)
-equivariant isometry from
\mathcal{B}(G,K)
to
\mathcal{B}(G,K^{\nr})
that maps into
\mathcal{B}(G,K^{\nr})^{\Gamma}
, which establishes the uniqueness statement.

The additional statements follow directly from
[
Lou21
, Théorème 4.9]
.
∎

For technical reasons to be detailed below, we also establish a similar result for embedded buildings – or inner façades in the terminology of
[
Rou20
]
– associated to centralisers of tori.

Recall that, if
S
is a
K
-split torus in
G_{K}
, then the union
\mathcal{B}(Z_{G_{K}}(S),G_{K},K)
of the apartments
A(G_{K},T)
, where
T
ranges among the maximal
K
-split tori of
G_{K}
that contain
S
, is closely related to the Bruhat-Tits building
\mathcal{B}(Z_{G_{K}}(S),K)
(see the Appendix for precise statements). In particular, Proposition
6
implies the following result.

Proposition 7
.

Let
K
be a discretely valued extension of
k
with perfect residue field and
S
be a
K
-split torus in
G_{K}
. Then, the action of
\Gal(K^{\nr}/K)
on
\mathcal{B}(G,K^{\nr})
stabilises the subspace
\mathcal{B}(Z_{G_{K}}(S),G,K^{\nr})
and we have

(\mathcal{B}(Z_{G_{K}}(S),G,K^{\nr}))^{\Gal(K^{\nr}/K)}=p_{K^{\nr}/K}(\mathcal{B}(Z_{G_{K}}(S),G,K))

Proof.

First of all, note that Conditions 1 and 3 of Proposition
6
imply that

p_{K^{\nr}/K}(\mathcal{B}(Z_{G_{K}}(S),G,K))\subset\mathcal{B}(Z_{G_{K}}(S),G,K^{\nr})^{\Gal(K^{\nr}/K)}.

For the reverse inclusion, we apply Proposition
6
to the group
Z_{G_{K}}(S)
. To pass from the façade
\mathcal{B}(Z_{G_{K}}(S),G,K^{\nr})
to the building
\mathcal{B}(Z_{G_{K}}(S),K^{\nr})
, we need to mod out, which we do below.
Let
T
be a maximal
K
-split torus of
G_{K}
containing
S
. By
[
Lou21
, Lemme 4.8]
, we have at our disposal a maximal
K^{\nr}
-split torus
T^{\nr}
of
G_{K^{\nr}}
containing
T_{K^{\nr}}
that is defined over
K
, and we have

p_{K^{\nr}/K}(A(G_{K},T))=A(G_{K^{\nr}},T^{\nr})^{\Gal(K^{\nr}/K)}.

Consider the vector spaces

\begin{array}[]{ccc}V^{\nr}&=&\ker(V(G_{K^{\nr}},T^{\nr})\to V((Z_{G_{K}}(S))_{K^{\nr}},T^{\nr}))\\
V&=&\ker(V(G_{K},T)\to V(Z_{G_{K}}(S),T))\end{array}.

The map
X_{*}(T)\to X_{*}(T^{\nr})
given by extension of scalars induces a linear injection
\begin{array}[]{ccc}V&\hookrightarrow&V^{\nr}\\
v&\mapsto&v_{K^{\nr}}\end{array}
that identifies
V
with the set of
\Gal(K^{\nr}/K)
-fixed points of
V^{\nr}
. Indeed, the proof of Theorem
1
establishes that the action of
\Gal(K^{\nr}/K)
on
V(G_{K^{\nr}},T^{\nr})
is induced by the pullback action on cocharacters.

Then, we claim that the restriction of
p_{K^{\nr}/K}
to
\mathcal{B}(Z_{G_{K}}(S),G,K)
is
V
-equivariant, ie. that for each
x\in\mathcal{B}(Z_{G_{K}}(S),G,K)
and each
v\in V
, we have

p_{K^{\nr}/K}(x+v)=p_{K^{\nr}/K}(x)+v_{K^{\nr}}.

Because the action of
V
commutes with that of
Z_{G_{K}}(S)(K)
(Property 2 of the Appendix), it is sufficient to prove the above for
x\in A(G_{K},T)
.

Recall that the centraliser
Z_{G_{K}}(T)(K)
acts on
A(G_{K},T)
by translation via the map

\nu:Z_{G_{K}}(T)(K)\longrightarrow V(G_{K},T)

such that, for each
z\in Z_{G_{K}}(T)(K)
and
\alpha\in\Phi(G_{K},T)
, we have

\langle\alpha,\nu(z)\rangle=-\omega(\alpha(z)).

Likewise, we have an analogously defined map

\nu^{\nr}:Z_{G_{K^{\nr}}}(T^{\nr})(K^{\nr}) \longrightarrow V(G_{K^{\nr}},T^{\nr}).

In particular, the center
Z(Z_{G_{K}}(S))(K)
acts on
A(G_{K},T)
by translations by elements of
V
and on
A(G_{K^{\nr}},T^{\nr})
by elements of
V^{\nr}
and we have, for each
z\in Z(Z_{G_{K}}(S))(K)

\nu^{\nr}(z)=\nu(z)_{K^{\nr}}.

Because
p_{K^{\nr}/K}
is
G(K)
-equivariant, we have

p_{K^{\nr}/K}(x+\nu(z))=p_{K^{\nr}/K}(x)+\nu(z)_{K^{\nr}}

for each
x\in A(G_{K},T)
and
z\in Z(Z_{G_{K}}(S))(K)
. Because both sides of the equation are affine and because the convex hull of
\{\nu(z),z\in Z(Z_{G_{K}}(S))(K)\}
is
V
, we get the result.

Now let

\widetilde{\pi}^{\nr}:\mathcal{B}(Z_{G_{K}}(S),G,K^{\nr})\to\mathcal{B}(Z_{G_{K}}(S),K^{\nr})

be the essentialisation map described in the Appendix. Because it is invariant under the action of
V^{\nr}
, the map
\widetilde{\pi}^{\nr}\circ p_{K^{\nr}/K}
is invariant under the action of
V
. Therefore, there exists a unique map
j
that makes the following diagram commute
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(Z_{G_{K}}(S),G,K^{\nr})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(Z_{G_{K}}(S),K^{\nr})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(Z_{G_{K}}(S),G,K)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(Z_{G_{K}}(S),K)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\pi}^{\nr}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{K^{\nr}/K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\pi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!j}

Moreover:

1.

The restriction of
j
to
A(Z_{G_{K}}(S),T)
fits into the following commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(G_{K^{\nr}},T^{\nr})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A((Z_{G_{K}}(S))_{K^{\nr}},T^{\nr})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(G_{K},T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(Z_{G_{K}}(S),T)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\pi}^{\nr}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{K^{\nr}/K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\pi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

.

In particular, the restriction of
j
to
A(Z_{G_{K}}(S),T)
is an affine injection.

2.

The map
j
is
G(K)
-equivariant. In particular, the restriction of
j
to each apartment
A(G_{K},T^{\prime})
is an affine injection into an apartment
A(G_{K^{\nr}},(T^{\prime})^{\nr})
for some maximal
K^{\nr}
-split torus
(T^{\prime})^{\nr}
containing
(T^{\prime})_{K^{\nr}}
.

3.

Because
\widetilde{\pi}^{\nr}
is
\Gal(K^{\nr}/K)
-equivariant (Proposition
63
), we have

\widetilde{\pi}^{\nr}(p_{K^{\nr}/K}(\mathcal{B}(Z_{G_{K}}(S),G,K)))\subset\mathcal{B}(Z_{G_{K}}(S),K^{\nr})^{\Gal(K^{\nr}/K)}

and therefore

j(\mathcal{B}(Z_{G_{K}}(S),K))\subset\mathcal{B}(Z_{G_{K}}(S),K^{\nr})^{\Gal(K^{\nr}/K)}.

In other words, the map
j
is none other than the map

p^{1}_{K^{\nr}/K}:\mathcal{B}(Z_{G_{K}}(S),K)\to\mathcal{B}(Z_{G_{K}}(S),K^{\nr})

given by Proposition
6
.

Now, let
y^{\nr}\in\mathcal{B}(Z_{G_{K}}(S),G,K^{\nr})^{\Gal(K^{\nr}/K)}
. Because
\widetilde{\pi}^{\nr}
is
\Gal(K^{\nr}/K)
-equivariant, we have

\widetilde{\pi}^{\nr}(y^{\nr})\in\mathcal{B}(Z_{G_{K}}(S),K^{\nr})^{\Gal(K^{\nr}/K)}.

By Proposition
6
, there exists
x\in\mathcal{B}(Z_{G_{K}}(S),K)
such that

\widetilde{\pi}^{\nr}(y^{\nr})=p^{1}_{K^{\nr}/K}(x).

Because
\widetilde{\pi}
is onto, there exists
z\in\mathcal{B}(Z_{G_{K}}(S),G,K)
such that
\widetilde{\pi}(z)=x
. Then, we have

\widetilde{\pi}^{\nr}(p_{K^{\nr}/K}(z))=p^{1}_{K^{\nr}/K}(\widetilde{\pi}(z))=\widetilde{\pi}^{\nr}(y^{\nr}).

Therefore, there exists
v^{\nr}\in V^{\nr}
such that
y^{\nr}=p_{K^{\nr}/K}(z)+v^{\nr}
. Because both
y^{\nr}
and
p^{1}_{K^{\nr}/K}(z)
are fixed under the action of the Galois group, so must
v^{\nr}
be (Proposition
63
, 3). Consequently,
v^{\nr}
can be written
v^{\nr}=v_{K^{\nr}}
for some
v\in V
and we thus have

y^{\nr}=p_{K^{\nr}/K}(z+v)\in p_{K^{\nr}/K}(\mathcal{B}(Z_{G_{K}}(S),G,K)),

which completes the proof.
∎

The quasi-split to pseudo-split case

The second special case is that of a finite extension
L/K
such that
G_{K}
is quasi-split and
G_{L}
is pseudo-split. The main difficulty here is to exhibit the image of a given special point. Because this image will be constructed as a valuation of a root data, which will involve concrete manipulations of parameterisations of root groups, this proof will be noticeably longer than the other ones.

Proposition 8
.

Let
K
and
L
be finite separable extensions of
k
, with
K\subset L
. Assume that
L/K
is Galois, that
G_{K}
is quasi-split and that
G_{L}
is pseudo-split. Then, there exists a unique map

p_{L/K}:\mathcal{B}(G,K)\to\mathcal{B}(G,L)

such that

1.

For each maximal
K
-split torus
S\subset G_{K}
, there exists a maximal
L
-split torus
T\subset G_{L}
such that
S_{L}\subset T
and such that the map
p_{L/K}
restricts to an affine isomorphism of
A(G,S)
onto an affine subspace of
A(G_{L},T)
.

2.

The map
p_{L/K}
is
G(K)
-equivariant.

3.

We have the inclusion
p_{L/K}(\mathcal{B}(G,K))\subset\mathcal{B}(G,L)^{\Gal(L/K)}
.

Lemma 9
.

With the notations of Proposition
8
, if
S\subset G_{K}
is a maximal
K
-split torus, then there exists a unique maximal torus
T\subset G_{K}
that contains
S
. The torus
T
splits over
L
and the set of fixed points of the apartment
A(G_{L},T_{L})
under the induced action of
\Gal(L/K)
is an affine subspace with direction
X_{*}(S_{L})_{\mathbb{R}}/X_{*}(Z(G_{L}))_{\mathbb{R}}
. Moreover, we have
Z_{G_{K}}(S)=Z_{G_{K}}(T)
as well as
N_{G_{K}}(S)\subset N_{G_{K}}(T)
.

Proof.

Because
G_{K}
is quasi-split, the centraliser
Z_{G_{K}}(S)
is nilpotent and therefore contains a unique maximal torus
T
that lies in the center
Z(Z_{G_{K}}(S))

[
Mil17
, Theorem 16.47]
. It immediately follows that
Z_{G_{K}}(S)=Z_{G_{K}}(T)
. Moreover, for each
K
-algebra
R
, if
n\in N_{G_{K}}(S)(R)
, then
n
normalises the subgroup
Z_{G_{R}}(S_{R})
and, because
T_{R}
is characteristic in
Z_{G_{R}}(S_{R})
,
n
must also normalise
T_{R}
. Hence
N_{G_{K}}(S)\subset N_{G_{K}}(T)
.

Applying the same theorem to
Z_{G_{L}}(S_{L})=(Z_{G_{K}}(S))_{L}
, we note that
T_{L}
is the only maximal torus of
G_{L}
that contains
S_{L}
. But
S_{L}
is split and therefore contained in a maximal
L
-split torus of
G_{L}
which, because
G_{L}
is pseudo-split, must be a maximal torus and can thus only be
T_{L}
. Hence, the torus
T_{L}
must be split.

Because
T_{L}
is defined over
K
, the action of
\Gal(L/K)
on
\mathcal{B}(G,L)
stabilises its associated apartment
A(G_{L},T_{L})
and induces an action by affine automorphisms. In particular, because
\Gal(L/K)
is compact, the action of
\Gal(L/K)
admits a fixed point. Setting this point as origin defines an isomorphism of affine spaces between
A(G_{L},T_{L})
and
V(G_{L},T_{L})
that identifies the action of
\Gal(L/K)
on
A(G_{L},T_{L})
with the action on
V(G_{L},T_{L})
induced by the pullback action on cocharacters of
T_{L}
. Because the fixed locus of the
\Gal(L/K)
-action on
V(G_{L},T_{L})=X_{*}(T_{L})_{\mathbb{R}}/X_{*}(Z(G_{L}))_{\mathbb{R}}
is precisely
X_{*}(S_{L})_{\mathbb{R}}/X_{*}(Z(G_{L}))_{\mathbb{R}}
, we get the expected result.
∎

Proof of Proposition
8
.

The spirit of the proof is very similar to that of Proposition
6
. As in the previous case, we prove that, letting
\Phi=\Phi(G,S)
be the relative root system of
G
and
\widetilde{\Phi}=\Phi(G_{L},T_{L})
be its absolute root system, the valuation
(\varphi_{a}^{y})_{a\in\widetilde{\Phi}}
associated to
y
descends to the valuation
(\varphi_{a}^{x})_{a\in\Phi}
associated to
x
in the sense of
[
BT72
, 9.1.11]
. The strategy goes as follows: Letting
x
be a special vertex in
A(G,S)
, we give an explicit construction of a valuation of the root data
(Z(L),(U_{a}(L))_{a\in\widetilde{\Phi}})
that descends to
(\varphi_{a}^{x})_{a\in\Phi}
and prove that it is fixed under the Galois action. Proposition
[
BT72
, 9.1.17]
then directly yields a map satisfying 1-3 and Propositions
[
BT72
, 9.2.14 and 9.2.15]
then ensure the uniqueness.

Let
x
be a special vertex in
A(G,S)
. We may assume without loss of generality that there exists a Chevalley quasi-system in
G^{\pred}
along
S

[
Lou21
, Définition 2.4]
, ie. a family of isomorphisms
(\zeta_{a})_{a\in\Phi}
of Weil restrictions of groups of the form
\SL_{2},\SU_{3}
, or
\BC_{1}
onto the universal covers
\widetilde{G}_{a}
of the groups of
K
-rank 1
G_{a}=\langle U_{a},U_{-a}\rangle
associated to each non-divisible root
a\in\Phi_{\nd}
, such that the valuation
(\varphi_{a}^{x})_{a\in\Phi}
associated to
x
is given by the formulas in
[
Lou21
, Définition 3.1]
.

We construct an associated Chevalley quasi-system in
G_{L}
along
T_{L}
. Note that, under the assumption that
G_{L}
is pseudo-split, each
(G_{a})_{L}
is pseudo-split with
(T\cap G_{a})_{L}
as a split maximal torus
[
CGP15
, Corollary A.2.7]
. Because
G
is pseudo-split over
K
, there exists a pseudo-parabolic subgroup
B\subset G
such that
B_{L}
is a minimal pseudo-parabolic
L
-subgroup of
G_{L}
. Denote by
\Delta\subset\Phi(G,S)
the system of relative simple roots associated to
B
and by
\widetilde{\Delta}\subset\Phi(G_{L},T_{L})
the system of absolute simple roots associated to
B_{L}
. Recall
[
CGP15
, Proof of Theorem C.2.15, Step 5]
that the restriction map
\alpha\mapsto\alpha_{|S}
establishes a bijection between the set of
\Gal(L/K)
-orbits in
\widetilde{\Delta}
and the set
\Delta
.
Let
a\in\Delta
be a relative simple root, let
S_{a}=(S\cap G_{a})^{0}_{\red}
, and let
\widetilde{S}_{a}
be the unique maximal
L
-split torus of
\widetilde{G}_{a}
that maps onto
S_{a}
.

1.

Assume that the isomorphism
\zeta_{a}
has as domain a Weil restriction
R_{K^{\prime}/K}(\SL_{2,K^{\prime}})
. The base change
R_{K^{\prime}/K}(\SL_{2,K^{\prime}})_{L}
decomposes into a direct product

R_{K^{\prime}/K}(\SL_{2,K^{\prime}})_{L}=R_{K^{\prime}\otimes_{K}L/L}(\SL_{2,K^{\prime}\otimes L})\simeq\prod_{i=1}^{r}R_{L^{\prime}_{i}/L}(\SL_{2,L^{\prime}_{i}}),

where
K^{\prime}\otimes_{K}L=\prod_{i=1}^{r}L^{\prime}_{i}
is the canonical decomposition of the finite reduced
L
-algebra
K^{\prime}\otimes_{K}L
into a product of extensions of
L
and we have a commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i=1}^{r}R_{L^{\prime}_{i}/L}(\SL_{2,L^{\prime}_{i}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{K^{\prime}\otimes_{K}L/L}(\SL_{2,K^{\prime}\otimes_{K}L})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\widetilde{G}_{a})_{L}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\diag\left(\begin{pmatrix}*&&\\
&&*\end{pmatrix}_{L}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}*&&\\
&&*\end{pmatrix}_{L}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\widetilde{S}_{a})_{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\zeta_{a})_{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subset}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subset}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\zeta_{a})_{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subset}

.

Here, the notation
\diag\left(\begin{pmatrix}*&\\
&*\end{pmatrix}_{L}\right)
refers to the diagonal embedding of the torus
\begin{pmatrix}*&\\
&*\end{pmatrix}_{L}
of
\SL_{2,L}
in the product
\prod_{i}R_{L^{\prime}_{i}/L}(\SL_{2,L^{\prime}_{i}})
.
Because
(G_{a})_{L}
is pseudo-split, so is
\prod_{i}R_{L^{\prime}_{i}/L}(\SL_{2,L^{\prime}_{i}})
, which implies that each
R_{L^{\prime}_{i}/L}(\SL_{2,L^{\prime}_{i}})
is pseudo-split and therefore
[
CGP15
, Proposition A.5.15 (2)]
that each extension
L^{\prime}_{i}/L
is purely inseparable. It follows that the torus
\prod_{i}\begin{pmatrix}*&\\
&*\end{pmatrix}_{L}
is the unique maximal torus of
\prod_{i}R_{L^{\prime}_{i}/L}(\SL_{2,L^{\prime}_{i}})
containing the split torus
\diag\left(\begin{pmatrix}*&\\
&*\end{pmatrix}_{L}\right)
. The assumption that
\zeta_{a}
maps the subgroup
R_{K^{\prime}\otimes_{K}L/L}\begin{pmatrix}1&\mathbb{G}_{a,K^{\prime}\otimes_{K}L}\\
&1\end{pmatrix}
to
U_{a}
implies that
a
pulls back along
\zeta_{a}
to the character
\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathbb{G}_{m,L}&\to&\mathbb{G}_{m,L}\\
t&\mapsto&t^{2}\end{array}
. Consequently, pulling back along
(\zeta_{a})_{L}
establishes a Galois-equivariant one-to-one correspondence between the roots
\alpha\in\widetilde{\Delta}
such that
\alpha_{|S_{L}}=a_{L}
and the roots of
\prod_{i=1}^{r}R_{L^{\prime}_{i}/L}(\SL_{2,L^{\prime}_{i}})
whose restriction to
\diag\begin{pmatrix}*&\\
&*\end{pmatrix}_{L}
is the character
\begin{pmatrix}t&\\
&t^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\mapsto t^{2}
, namely the maps
\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathbb{G}_{m,L}^{r}&\to&\mathbb{G}_{m,L}\\
(t_{1},\dots,t_{r})&\mapsto&t_{i}^{2}\end{array}
for
i\in[\![1,r]\!]
. We relabel the factor fields of
K^{\prime}\otimes_{K}L
accordingly:
K^{\prime}\otimes_{K}L=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\widetilde{\Delta}\\
\alpha_{|S_{L}}=a_{L}\end{subarray}}L^{\prime}_{\alpha}
.

It follows that the factors
R_{L^{\prime}_{\alpha}/L}(\SL_{2,L^{\prime}_{\alpha}})
are the subgroups of
L
-rank 1 of
R_{K^{\prime}\otimes_{K}L/L}(\SL_{2,K^{\prime}\otimes_{K}L})
associated to the roots relative to
\prod_{i}\begin{pmatrix}*&\\
&*\end{pmatrix}_{L}
whose restriction to
\diag\begin{pmatrix}*&\\
&*\end{pmatrix}_{L}
is the character
\begin{pmatrix}t&\\
&t^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\mapsto t^{2}
. Accordingly, we have a direct product decomposition

(\widetilde{G_{a}})_{L}=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\widetilde{\Delta}\\
\alpha_{|S_{L}}=a_{L}\end{subarray}}\widetilde{(G_{L})_{\alpha}}

and the map
(\zeta_{a})_{L}
decomposes into a product

(\zeta_{a})_{L}=\prod_{\alpha_{|S_{L}}=a_{L}}\zeta_{\alpha},

where each
\zeta_{\alpha}:R_{L^{\prime}_{\alpha}/L}(\SL_{2,L^{\prime}_{\alpha}})\to\widetilde{(G_{L})_{\alpha}}
is an isomorphism that maps the upper triangular matrices to the root group
U_{\alpha}
and whose restriction to the torus
\begin{pmatrix}*&\\
&*\end{pmatrix}
is the coroot
\alpha^{\vee}
.

Observe also that, for each
\sigma\in\Gal(L/K)
, the automorphism induced by
\sigma
on
K^{\prime}\otimes_{K}L
restricts to an isomorphism of valued fields
L^{\prime}_{\alpha}\overset{\sim}{\to}L^{\prime}_{\sigma(\alpha)}
for each
\alpha\in\widetilde{\Delta}
such that
\alpha_{|S_{L}}=a_{L}
. Because
(\zeta_{a})_{L}
is defined over
K
, we have

\sigma\circ\zeta_{\alpha}\circ\sigma^{-1}=\zeta_{\sigma(\alpha)}

for each
\alpha\in\widetilde{\Delta}
such that
\alpha_{|S_{L}}=a_{L}
.

Consequently, if for each
\alpha\in\widetilde{\Delta}
such that
\alpha_{|S_{L}}=a_{L}
we define a function
\varphi_{\alpha}:U_{\alpha}(L)\to\mathbb{R}
by setting, for each

,

\varphi_{\alpha}\left(\zeta_{\alpha}\begin{pmatrix}1&x\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\right)=\omega(x),

then we have

\varphi_{\alpha}\circ\sigma^{-1}=\varphi_{\sigma(\alpha)}

for each
\sigma\in\Gal(L/K)
.

Moreover, under the identification
\zeta_{a}
, the inclusion
U_{a}(K)\subset U_{a}(L)=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\widetilde{\Delta}\\
\alpha_{|S_{L}}=a_{L}\end{subarray}}U_{\alpha}(L)
pulls back to the diagonal map
K^{\prime}\to K^{\prime}\otimes_{K}L=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\widetilde{\Delta}\\
\alpha_{|S_{L}}=a_{L}\end{subarray}}L^{\prime}_{\alpha}
. Therefore, in the decomposition of an element
u\in U_{a}(K)\setminus\{1\}
as
u=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\widetilde{\Delta}\\
\alpha_{|S_{L}}=a_{L}\end{subarray}}u_{\alpha}
, with
u_{\alpha}\in U_{\alpha}(L)
, we have
\varphi^{x}_{a}(u)=\varphi_{\alpha}(u_{\alpha})
for each
\alpha\in\widetilde{\Delta}
such that
\alpha_{|S_{L}}=a_{L}
.

2.

Next, assume that the isomorphism
\zeta_{a}
has as domain a Weil restriction
R_{K^{\prime}/K}(\BC_{1,K^{\prime}})
. The exact same reductions as in step 1 apply and we may write
K^{\prime}\otimes_{K}L=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\widetilde{\Delta}\\
\alpha_{|S_{L}}=a_{L}\end{subarray}}L^{\prime}_{\alpha}
and

R_{K^{\prime}\otimes_{K}L/L}(\BC_{1,K^{\prime}\otimes_{K}L})=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\widetilde{\Delta}\\
\alpha_{|S_{L}}=a_{L}\end{subarray}}R_{L^{\prime}_{\alpha}/L}(\BC_{1,L^{\prime}_{\alpha}}).

Likewise, we have a direct product decomposition

(\widetilde{G_{a}})_{L}=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\widetilde{\Delta}\\
\alpha_{|S_{L}}=a_{L}\end{subarray}}\widetilde{(G_{L})_{\alpha}}

and the map
(\zeta_{a})_{L}
decomposes into a product

(\zeta_{a})_{L}=\prod_{\alpha_{|S_{L}}=a_{L}}\zeta_{\alpha},

where each
\zeta_{\alpha}:R_{L^{\prime}_{\alpha}/L}(\BC_{1,L^{\prime}_{\alpha}})\to\widetilde{(G_{L})_{\alpha}}
is an isomorphism that maps the ”upper triangular unipotent matrices”
3
3

3

That is, the root group of
R_{L^{\prime}_{\alpha}/L}(\BC_{1,L^{\prime}_{\alpha}})
that lifts the group of upper triangular unipotent matrices in

and whose restriction to the torus

&*\end{pmatrix}_{L}
is the coroot
\alpha^{\vee}
. Like before, the fact that

is defined over

implies that

\sigma\circ\zeta_{\alpha}\circ\sigma^{-1}=\zeta_{\sigma(\alpha)}

for each

and
\alpha\in\widetilde{\Delta}
such that
\alpha_{|S_{L}}=a_{L}
.

Let
\eta\in K^{\prime 1/2}\setminus K^{\prime}
such that the function

is given by

\varphi_{a}^{x}(x_{a}(u,v))=\frac{1}{2}\omega(\eta u^{2}+v),

where

to the subgroup of unipotent upper triangular matrices in

. Then, if for each

we have for each such

and each

\varphi_{\alpha}\circ\sigma^{-1}=\varphi_{\sigma(\alpha)}.

Moreover, under the identification
\zeta_{a}
, the inclusion

\alpha_{|S_{L}}=a_{L}\end{subarray}}U_{\alpha}(L)
pulls back to the diagonal map

\alpha_{|S_{L}}=a_{L}\end{subarray}}(L^{\prime}_{\alpha})^{1/2}\times L^{\prime}_{\alpha}
. Therefore, in the decomposition of an element
u\in U_{a}(K)\setminus\{1\}
as
u=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\widetilde{\Delta}\\
\alpha_{|S_{L}}=a_{L}\end{subarray}}u_{\alpha}
, with
u_{\alpha}\in U_{\alpha}(L)
, we have
\varphi^{x}_{a}(u)=\varphi_{\alpha}(u_{\alpha})
for each
\alpha\in\widetilde{\Delta}
such that
\alpha_{|S_{L}}=a_{L}
.

3.

Finally, assume that the isomorphism
\zeta_{a}
has as domain a Weil restriction

, where the

Likewise, we have a commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i\in I_{a}}R_{L^{\prime}_{i}/L}(\SL_{3,L^{\prime}_{i}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{K^{\prime}\otimes_{K}L/L}(\SL_{3,K^{\prime}\otimes L})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\widetilde{G}_{a})_{L}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{pmatrix}*&&&&\\
&&1&&\\
&&&&*\end{pmatrix}_{L}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\widetilde{S}_{a})_{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subset}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

The torus
\prod_{i\in I_{a}}\begin{pmatrix}*&&\\

&&*\end{pmatrix}_{L}
