&1&\\
&&t^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\mapsto t
on
\diag\left(\begin{pmatrix}*&&\\
&1&\\
&&*\end{pmatrix}_{L}\right)
, therefore we have

\{\alpha\in\widetilde{\Delta},\alpha_{|S_{L}}=a_{L}\}=\bigcup_{i\in I_{a}}\{\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}\}\text{ and }\{\alpha\in\widetilde{\Phi},\alpha_{|S_{L}}=2a_{L}\}=\{\alpha_{i}+\beta_{i},i\in I_{a}\}

For each
i\in I_{a}
, set

\zeta_{\alpha_{i}}:\begin{array}[]{ccc}R_{L^{\prime}_{i}/L}(SL_{2,L^{\prime}_{i}})&\longrightarrow&G_{\alpha_{i}}\\
\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}&\longmapsto&\zeta_{a}\left(\begin{pmatrix}a&b&\\
c&d&\\
&&1\end{pmatrix}\right)\end{array},

\zeta_{\beta_{i}}:\begin{array}[]{ccc}R_{L^{\prime}_{i}/L}(SL_{2,L^{\prime}_{i}})&\longrightarrow&G_{\beta_{i}}\\
\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}&\longmapsto&\zeta_{a}\left(\begin{pmatrix}1&&\\
&a&b\\
&c&d\end{pmatrix}\right)\end{array},

and

\zeta_{\alpha_{i}+\beta_{i}}:\begin{array}[]{ccc}R_{L^{\prime}_{i}/L}(SL_{2,L^{\prime}_{i}})&\longrightarrow&G_{\alpha_{i}+\beta_{i}}\\
\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}&\longmapsto&\zeta_{a}\left(\begin{pmatrix}a&&b\\
&1&\\
c&&d\end{pmatrix}\right)\end{array}.

Here, we think of
R_{L^{\prime}_{i}/L}(SL_{3,L^{\prime}_{i}})
as a direct factor of
R_{K^{\prime}_{2}\otimes_{K}L}(SL_{3,K^{\prime}_{2}\otimes_{K}L})
.

Then, for each
i\in I_{a}
and
\sigma\in\Gal(L/K)
, we have

\sigma\circ\zeta_{\alpha_{i}}\circ\sigma^{-1}=\zeta_{\alpha_{\sigma(i)}}=\zeta_{\sigma(\alpha_{i})},

\sigma\circ\zeta_{\beta_{i}}\circ\sigma^{-1}=\zeta_{\beta_{\sigma(i)}}=\zeta_{\sigma(\beta_{i})},

and

\sigma\circ\zeta_{\alpha_{i}+\beta_{i}}\circ\sigma^{-1}=\zeta_{\sigma(\alpha_{i}+\beta_{i})}.

Once again, if, for each
\alpha\in\widetilde{\Delta}
such that
\alpha_{|S_{L}}\in\{a_{L},2a_{L}\}
, we define
\varphi_{\alpha}:U_{\alpha}\to\mathbb{R}
by

\varphi_{\alpha}\left(\zeta_{\alpha}\begin{pmatrix}1&x\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\right)=\omega(x),

then for each
\sigma\in\Gal(L/K)
and each such
\alpha
, we have

\varphi_{\alpha}\circ\sigma^{-1}=\varphi_{\sigma(\alpha)}.

Recall also that, denoting by
H_{0}(K^{\prime},K_{2}^{\prime})
the
K^{\prime}_{2}
-variety

H_{0}(K^{\prime},K_{2}^{\prime})=\{(x,y)\in R_{K^{\prime}/K^{\prime}_{2}}(\mathbb{A}_{K^{\prime}}^{2}),y+\tau(y)=x\tau(x)\},

where
\tau
is the nontrivial element of
\Gal(K^{\prime}/K^{\prime}_{2})
, the group of upper-triangular unipotent matrices in
R_{K_{2}^{\prime}/K}SU_{3,K^{\prime}/K^{\prime}_{2}}
is identified with
R_{K^{\prime}_{2}/K}(H_{0}(K^{\prime},K^{\prime}_{2}))
via the map

\mu:(x,y)\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}1&-\tau(x)&-y\\
&1&x\\
&&1\end{pmatrix}.

Then, the inclusion
U_{a}(K)\subset U_{a}(L)=\prod_{i\in I_{a}}U_{\alpha_{i}}(L)U_{\alpha_{i}+\beta_{i}}(L)U_{\beta_{i}}(L)
spells out explicitly as the map

\zeta_{a}(\mu(x,y))\mapsto\prod_{i\in I_{a}}\zeta_{\alpha_{i}}(-\tau(x))\zeta_{\alpha_{i}+\beta_{i}}(\tau(y))\zeta_{\beta_{i}}(x).

Then, if
u=\zeta_{a}(\mu(x,y))\in U_{a}(K)\setminus\{1\}
decomposes as
u=\prod_{i\in I_{a}}u_{\alpha_{i}}u_{\alpha_{i}+\beta_{i}}u_{\beta_{i}}
, we have for each
i\in I_{a}
:

\varphi_{\alpha_{i}}(u_{\alpha_{i}})=\varphi_{\beta_{i}}(u_{\beta_{i}})=\omega(x)\geq\varphi_{a}^{x}(u)

and

\varphi_{\alpha_{i}+\beta_{i}}(u_{\alpha_{i}+\beta_{i}})=\omega(y)=2\varphi_{a}^{x}(u).

We have thus constructed a quasi-épinglage
(\zeta_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\widetilde{\Delta}}

[
Lou21
, Définition 2.4]
in
G_{L}
along
T_{L}
. By
[
Lou21
, Proof of Proposition 2.5]
, this quasi-épinglage extends to a Chevalley quasi-system
(\zeta_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\widetilde{\Phi}}
in
G^{\pred}_{L}
along
T_{L}
. Let us now extend our family
(\varphi_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\widetilde{\Delta}}
to a valuation
(\varphi_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\widetilde{\Phi}_{\nd}}
of the root data
(Z_{G}(T)(L),(U_{\alpha}(L))_{\alpha\in\widetilde{\Phi}})
. Our main tool is the fact that, for all non-divisible roots
\alpha
and
\beta
in
\widetilde{\Phi}_{\nd}
, the parametrisation
\inta(m_{\alpha})\circ\zeta_{\beta}
is
similar
to
\zeta_{s_{\alpha}(\beta)}
, in the sense that is differs from
\zeta_{s_{\alpha}(\beta)}
by precomposition with a field
L
-automorphism of
\bar{L}
that maps the domain of
\zeta_{\beta}
onto that of
\zeta_{s_{\alpha}(\beta)}
, and by a possible sign change.

Recall from
[
Lou21
, Définition 2.4]
that, if
(\zeta_{a})_{a\in\Phi}
is a Chevalley quasi-system, we let

m_{a}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\zeta_{a}\begin{pmatrix}&1\\
-1&\end{pmatrix}&\text{if }\dom(\zeta_{a})\text{ is of the form }R_{K^{\prime}/K}(SL_{2,K^{\prime}})\\
\zeta_{a}\begin{pmatrix}&&-1\\
&-1&\\
-1&&\end{pmatrix}&\text{if }\dom(\zeta_{a})\text{ is of the form }R_{K_{2}^{\prime}/K}(SU_{3,K^{\prime}/K^{\prime}_{2}})\\
x_{-a}(0,-1)x_{a}(0,1)x_{-a}(0,-1)&\text{if }\dom(\zeta_{a})\text{ is of the form }R_{K^{\prime}/K}(BC_{1,K^{\prime}})\end{array}\right..

The computations we made above allow us to relate the
(m_{a})_{a\in\Phi_{\nd}}
and the
(m_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\widetilde{\Phi}_{\nd}}
as follows

1.

If
a\in\Delta
is such that
\zeta_{a}
has as domain a Weil restriction
R_{K^{\prime}/K}(SL_{2,K^{\prime}})
or
R_{K^{\prime}/K}(BC_{1,K^{\prime}})
, then we have
m_{a}=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\widetilde{\Phi}\\
\alpha_{|S}=a\end{subarray}}m_{\alpha}.

2.

If
a\in\Delta
is such that
\zeta_{a}
has as domain a Weil restriction
R_{K^{\prime}_{2}/K}(SU_{3,K^{\prime}/K^{\prime}_{2}})
, then for some order on the roots, we have
m_{a}=\lambda(-1)\left(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\widetilde{\Phi}\\
\alpha_{|S}\in\{a,2a\}\end{subarray}}m_{\alpha}\right)
, for some cocharacter
\lambda\in X_{*}(T)
.

This allows one to define valuation functions by precomposing those already constructed by products of
\inta(m_{\alpha})
, and to transfer properties the general case that were proved above for the
\varphi_{\alpha}
, where
\widetilde{\alpha}\in\widetilde{\Phi}
with
\alpha_{|S}\in\Delta\cup(2\Delta\cap\Phi)
.
The reason we expect this transfer to work is that, for valuations of root data, we do have such a compatibility with conjugacy by specific elements of
N_{G}(T)(L)
, see
[
BT72
, Proposition 6.2.7]
– with the notations of this proposition, we have
m_{\alpha}\in M_{\alpha,0}
for each
\alpha
.

Observe
[
Bou81
, Ch. VI, §1, Proposition 15]
that each non-divisible root
a\in\Phi_{\nd}
can be mapped to
\Delta
by some element
w\in W(G,S)(K)
. For each
a\in\Phi_{\nd}\setminus\Delta
, fix once and for all a simple root
b_{a}\in\Delta
and an element
w_{a}\in W(G,S)(K)
such that
w_{a}(a)=b_{a}
.

Finally, recall that, as observed in the proof of Lemma
9
, we have an inclusion

W(G,S)(K)\hookrightarrow W(G_{L},T_{L})(L).

This inclusion has the property that, for any character
\chi
and any
w\in W(G,S)(K)
, we have
w(\chi)_{|S}=w(\chi_{|S})
.

Now, let
\alpha\in\widetilde{\Phi}_{\nd}\setminus\widetilde{\Delta}
whose restriction to
S
is a root
ja
, with
j\in\{1,2\}
and
a\in\Phi_{\nd}\setminus\Delta
. Set

\varphi_{\alpha}=\varphi_{\beta_{\alpha}}\circ\inta(m_{\alpha_{1}})\circ\dots\inta(m_{\alpha_{r}}),

where
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{r},\beta_{\alpha}\in\widetilde{\Delta}
, with
\beta_{\alpha}=w_{a}(\alpha)
and
w_{a}=s_{\alpha_{1}}\dots s_{\alpha_{r}}
.
This function does not depend on the decomposition of
w_{a}
as a product of simple reflections. Indeed, if
\zeta_{\alpha}
has as domain a Weil restriction
R_{L^{\prime}/L}(SL_{2,L^{\prime}})
, then because
(\zeta_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\widetilde{\Phi}_{\nd}}
is a Chevalley quasi-system, there exists a
\tau\in\Aut_{L}(\overline{L})
such that, for each
x\in L^{\prime}
, we have

\inta(m_{\alpha_{1}})\circ\dots\inta(m_{\alpha_{r}})\circ\zeta_{\alpha}\begin{pmatrix}1&x\\
&1\end{pmatrix}=\zeta_{\beta_{\alpha}}\begin{pmatrix}1&\pm\tau(x)\\
&1\end{pmatrix},

and therefore

\varphi_{\alpha}\left(\zeta_{\alpha}\begin{pmatrix}1&x\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\right)=\varphi_{\beta_{\alpha}}\left(\zeta_{\beta_{\alpha}}\begin{pmatrix}1&\pm\tau(x)\\
&1\end{pmatrix}\right)=\omega(x).

On the other hand, if
\zeta_{\alpha}
has as domain a Weil restriction
R_{L^{\prime}/L}(BC_{1,L^{\prime}})
, then likewise, there exists
\tau\in\Aut_{L}(\overline{L})
such that, for each
(u,v)\in L^{\prime 1/2}\times L^{\prime}
, we have

\inta(m_{\alpha_{1}})\circ\dots\inta(m_{\alpha_{r}})\circ\zeta_{\alpha}(x_{\alpha}(u,v))=\zeta_{\beta_{\alpha}}(x_{\beta_{\alpha}}(\tau(u),\tau(v))).

Therefore, there exists
\eta\in L^{\prime 1/2}\setminus L^{\prime}
such that

\varphi_{\alpha}(x_{\alpha}(u,v))=\varphi_{\beta_{\alpha}}(x_{\beta_{\alpha}}(\tau(u),\tau(v)))=\frac{1}{2}\omega(\eta u^{2}+v)

for all
(u,v)\in L^{\prime 1/2}\times L^{\prime}
.

It now follows from
[
Lou21
, Proposition 3.2]
that the collection
(\varphi_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\widetilde{\Phi}_{\nd}}
defined above constitutes a valuation of the root data
(Z_{G}(T)(L),(U_{\alpha}(L))_{\alpha\in\widetilde{\Phi}})
corresponding to a point
z\in A(G_{L},T)
. Furthermore, we claim that, if
a\in\Phi_{\nd}
is a non-divisible root and
u\in U_{a}(K)\setminus\{1\}
, we have the equivalence

u\in U_{a,r}^{x}\iff u\in\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\widetilde{\Phi}_{\nd}\\
\alpha_{|S_{L}}=a_{L}\end{subarray}}U_{\alpha,r}^{z}\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\widetilde{\Phi}_{\nd}\\
\alpha_{|S_{L}}=2a_{L}\end{subarray}}U_{\alpha,2r}^{z},

that is

\varphi_{a}^{x}(u)=\sup\left\{r\in\mathbb{R},u\in\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\widetilde{\Phi}_{\nd}\\
\alpha_{|S_{L}}=a_{L}\end{subarray}}U_{\alpha,r}^{z}\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\widetilde{\Phi}_{\nd}\\
\alpha_{|S_{L}}=2a_{L}\end{subarray}}U_{\alpha,2r}^{z}\right\}.

This follows from earlier computations in the case where
a\in\Delta
. The general case will be reduced to that of simple roots. Let
a\in\Phi_{\nd}\setminus\Delta
.
Let
a_{1},\dots,a_{p}\in\Delta
be such that
w_{a}
admits the following decomposition as a product of simple reflections

w_{a}=s_{a_{1}}\dots s_{a_{p}}.

Let
u\in U_{a}(K)\setminus\{1\}
. On the one hand, by
[
BT72
, Proposition 6.2.7]
, we have

\varphi_{a}^{x}(u)=\varphi_{b_{a}}^{x}(\inta(m_{a_{1}})\circ\cdots\inta(m_{a_{p}})(u)).

On the other hand, if
\alpha\in\widetilde{\Phi}
is such that
\alpha_{|S}\in\{a,2a\}
, then because

w_{a}=\left(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha^{\prime}\in\widetilde{\Phi}\\
\alpha^{\prime}_{|S}\{a_{1},2a_{1}\}\end{subarray}}s_{\alpha^{\prime}}\right)\cdots\left(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha^{\prime}\in\widetilde{\Phi}\\
\alpha^{\prime}_{|S}\in\{a_{p},2a_{p}\}\end{subarray}}s_{\alpha^{\prime}}\right)

is a decomposition of
w_{a}
as a product of reflections in
W(G,T)(L)
, the component
u_{\alpha}
of
u
in
U_{\alpha}(L)
satisfies

\begin{array}[]{ccl}\varphi_{\alpha}(u_{\alpha})&=&\varphi_{\beta_{\alpha}}\left(\left(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha^{\prime}\in\widetilde{\Phi}\\
\alpha^{\prime}_{|S}\in\{a_{1},2a_{1}\}\end{subarray}}\inta(m_{\alpha^{\prime}})\right)\circ\cdots\left(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha^{\prime}\in\widetilde{\Phi}\\
\alpha^{\prime}_{|S}\in\{a_{p},2a_{p}\}\end{subarray}}\inta(m_{\alpha^{\prime}})\right)(u_{\alpha})\right)\\
&=&\varphi_{\beta_{\alpha}}(\inta(\lambda(-1))\circ\inta(m_{a_{1}})\circ\cdots\inta(m_{a_{p}})(u_{\alpha}))\end{array}

for some
\lambda\in X_{*}(T)
.
We can then check explicitly using our formulas for the épinglage
(\varphi_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\widetilde{\Delta}}
that
\varphi_{\beta_{\alpha}}\circ\inta(\lambda(-1))=\varphi_{\beta_{\alpha}}
.
Now, because
\beta_{\alpha}=w_{a}(\alpha)
lifts
b_{a}=w_{a}(a)
, and because
\inta(m_{a_{1}})\circ\cdots\inta(m_{a_{p}})(u_{\alpha})
is the component of
\inta(m_{a_{1}})\circ\cdots\inta(m_{a_{p}})(u)
in
U_{\beta_{\alpha}}(L)
, we deduce from previous computations that

\begin{array}[]{cc}\varphi_{\alpha}(u_{\alpha})\geq\varphi_{a}^{x}(u)&\text{if }\alpha_{|S}=a\\
\varphi_{\alpha}(u_{\alpha})=2\varphi_{a}^{x}(u)&\text{if }\alpha_{|S}=2a\end{array}.

This completes the proof of the fact that the valuation
(\varphi_{a}^{x})_{a\in\Phi}
is induced by the valuation
(\varphi_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\widetilde{\Phi}}
in the sense of
[
BT72
, Définition 9.1.11]
. By
[
BT72
, Proposition 9.1.17]
, there exists a
G(K)
-equivariant map

j:\mathcal{B}(G,K)\to\mathcal{B}(G,L)

that maps
x
to
z
and is an isometric embedding if the metric on
V(G_{K},S)
is chosen to be induced by a choice of inner product on
V(G_{L},T)
. In particular, it is affine on apartments, which establishes conditions 1.

Our last task is to check that
z\in\mathcal{B}(G,L)^{\Gal(L/K)}
, which amounts to proving that the valuation
(\varphi_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\widetilde{\Phi}_{\nd}}
is
\Gal(L/K)
-invariant.
We proved that, for all
\alpha\in\widetilde{\Phi}
and
\sigma\in\Gal(L/K)
, we have

\varphi_{\alpha}\circ\sigma^{-1}=\varphi_{\sigma(\alpha)}.

Now, if
\alpha\in\widetilde{\Phi}_{\nd}
satisfies
\alpha_{|S}\in\{a,2a\}
with
a\in\Phi_{\nd}\setminus\Delta
, then writing
w_{a}=s_{\alpha_{1}}\circ\cdots s_{\alpha_{r}}
as a product of simple reflections in the absolute Weyl group
W(G,T)(L)
, we have for each
\sigma\in\Gal(L/K)
:

\begin{array}[]{ccl}\varphi_{\alpha}\circ\sigma^{-1}&=&\varphi_{\beta_{\alpha}}\circ\inta(m_{\alpha_{1}})\circ\cdots\circ\inta(m_{\alpha_{r}})\circ\sigma^{-1}\\
&=&\varphi_{\beta_{\alpha}}\circ\sigma^{-1}\circ\inta(\sigma(m_{\alpha_{1}}))\circ\dots\inta(\sigma(m_{\alpha_{r}}))\\
&=&\varphi_{\sigma(\beta_{\alpha})}\circ\inta(\sigma(m_{\alpha_{1}}))\circ\dots\inta(\sigma(m_{\alpha_{r}}))\end{array}.

Because
w_{a}\in W(G,S)(K)
is
\Gal(L/K)
-invariant, we have

\sigma(\beta_{\alpha})=\sigma(w_{a}(\alpha))=w_{a}(\sigma(\alpha))=\beta_{\sigma(\alpha)}.

Moreover, given the compatibility between the parameterisations
\zeta_{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in\widetilde{\Delta}
and the action of
\Gal(L/K)
, we have
\sigma(m_{\alpha_{i}})=m_{\sigma(\alpha_{i})}
for each
i\in[\![1,r]\!]
.
We deduce that

\begin{array}[]{ccl}\varphi_{\alpha}\circ\sigma^{-1}&=&\varphi_{\beta_{\sigma(\alpha)}}\circ\inta(m_{\sigma(\alpha_{1})})\circ\cdots\inta(m_{\sigma(\alpha_{r})})\\
&=&\varphi_{\sigma(\alpha)}\end{array},

as
w_{a}=\sigma(w_{a})=s_{\sigma(\alpha_{1})}\cdots s_{\sigma(\alpha_{r})}
. This completes the proof of the
\Gal(L/K)
-invariance of
(\varphi_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\widetilde{\Phi}_{\nd}}
, and therefore
z
is fixed under the action of
\Gal(L/K)
on
\mathcal{B}(G,L)
.

By
G(K)
-equivariance of
j
, we deduce that
j(G(K)\cdot x)\subset\mathcal{B}(G,L)^{\Gal(L/K)}
. Finally, because
\mathcal{B}(G,K)
is the convex hull of
G(K)\cdot x
, we get
j(\mathcal{B}(G,K))\subset\mathcal{B}(G,L)^{\Gal(L/K)}
.

The above map
j
is therefore a map from
\mathcal{B}(G,K)
to
\mathcal{B}(G,L)
that satisfies conditions 1-3, which completes the proof of Proposition
8
.

∎

The general case

We are now in position to prove the general functoriality theorem. Let us begin with the following technical corollary of Theorem
1
as well as Propositions
6
and
8
, which will prove very useful below.

Proposition 10
.

Let
K
and
L
be finite separable extensions of
k
.

1.

Assume that
G_{K}
and
G_{L}
have the same relative rank. Let
K_{0}
and
L_{0}
be extensions of
k
such that the following inclusions hold

\begin{matrix}K&\subset&L\\
\cup&&\cup\\
K_{0}&\subset&L_{0}\end{matrix}.

Assume that
K
is Galois over
K_{0}
and that
L
is Galois over
L_{0}
. Then, the map
p_{L/K}
is
\Gal(L/L_{0})
-equivariant, if we let
\Gal(L/L_{0})
act on
\mathcal{B}(G,K)
via the restriction map
\res_{L/K}:\Gal(L/L_{0})\to\Gal(K/K_{0})
.

2.

Assume that
K
and
L
satisfy the conditions of Proposition
6
or
8
. Let
K_{0}\subset K
be a subextension of
k
such that
K
and
L
are Galois over
K_{0}
. Then, the map
p_{L/K}
is
\Gal(L/K_{0})
-equivariant, if we let
\Gal(L/K_{0})
act on
\mathcal{B}(G,K)
via the restriction map
\res_{L/K}:\Gal(L/K_{0})\to\Gal(K/K_{0})
.

Proof.

1.

Let
\gamma\in\Gal(L/L_{0})
and
\gamma_{K}=\res_{L/K}(\gamma)
. Then, by Theorems
1
and
5
, the map

p_{\gamma}\circ p_{L/K}\circ p_{\gamma_{K}^{-1}}:\mathcal{B}(G,K)\to\mathcal{B}(G,L)

satisfies conditions 1 and 2 of Theorem
1
with
\sigma:K\to L
being the inclusion, hence we must have
p_{\gamma} \circ p_{L/K}\circ p_{\gamma_{K}^{-1}}=p_{L/K}
.

2.

Let
\gamma\in\Gal(L/K_{0})
and
\gamma_{K}=\res_{L/K}(\gamma)
. To prove that
p_{\gamma}\circ p_{L/K}\circ p_{\gamma_{K}^{-1}}=p_{L/K}
, it will be sufficient, because of the uniqueness properties of Propositions
6
and
8
to prove that the map
p_{\gamma}\circ p_{L/K}\circ p_{\gamma_{K}^{-1}}
satisfy conditions 1-3.
As previously observed, conditions 1 and 2 follow directly from Theorem
5
. Lastly, we need to check that

p_{\gamma}\circ p_{L/K}\circ p_{\gamma_{K}^{-1}}(\mathcal{B}(G,K))\subset\mathcal{B}(G,L)^{\Gal(L/K)},

that is

p_{\gamma}\circ p_{L/K}(\mathcal{B}(G,K))\subset\mathcal{B}(G,L)^{\Gal(L/K)}.

It is sufficient to check that
p_{\gamma}
maps the
\Gal(L/K)
-fixed locus of
\mathcal{B}(G,L)
to itself, which is immediate because
\Gal(L/K)
is normal in
\Gal(L/K_{0})
.

∎

We now state and prove the main theorem.

Theorem 11
.

There exists a unique family
(p_{L/K}:\mathcal{B}(G,K)\to\mathcal{B}(G,L))
indexed by pairs of finite separable extensions of
k
such that
K\subset L
satisfying the following properties:

1.

For each
K\subset L
, each
S\subset G_{K}
, there exists
T\subset G_{L}
such that
S_{L}\subset T
and such that the map
p_{L/K}
restricts to an affine isomorphism of
A(G_{K},S)
onto an affine subspace of
A(G_{L},T)
.

2.

For each
K\subset L
, the map
p_{L/K}
is
G(K)
-equivariant.

3.

Whenever
L/K
is Galois, we have
p_{L/K}(\mathcal{B}(G,K))\subset(\mathcal{B}(G,L))^{\Gal(L/K)}
.

4.

For each finite separable extensions
K,L,M
of
k
such that
K\subset L\subset M
, we have

p_{M/K}=p_{M/L}\circ p_{L/K}.

Proof.

We follow the proof of
[
Rou77
, Théorème 5.2]
, with minimal adjustments. We first establish the uniqueness assertion.

(Uniqueness)
Let
(p_{L/K})_{k\subset K\subset L}
be a system of maps satisfying conditions 1-4. Observe that, if
K\subset L
, with
L=K^{\nr}
, then
p_{L/K}
must be the map constructed in Proposition
6
. Likewise, if
K\subset L
, with
G_{K}
quasi-split and
G_{L}
pseudo-split, then
p_{L/K}
must be the map constructed in Proposition
8
. Now, let
K
and
L
be arbitrary finite separable extensions of
k
with
K\subset L
.

Consider the strict henselisation
K^{\nr}
(resp.
L^{\nr}
) of
K
(resp.
L
). Then, the groups
G_{K^{\nr}}
and
G_{L^{\nr}}
are quasi-split by
[
Lou21
, Corollaire 2.3]
. There is thus a unique minimal finite Galois extension
\widetilde{K^{\nr}}/K^{\nr}
(resp.
\widetilde{L^{\nr}}/L^{\nr}
) such that
G_{\widetilde{K^{\nr}}}
(resp.
G_{\widetilde{L^{\nr}}}
) is pseudo-split. Then, the extensions
K^{\nr}
and
\widetilde{K^{\nr}}
(resp.
L^{\nr}
and
\widetilde{L^{\nr}}
) are Galois over
K
(resp.
L
), the group
G_{K^{\nr}}
(resp.
G_{L^{\nr}}
) is quasi-split and the group
G_{\widetilde{K^{\nr}}}
(resp.
G_{\widetilde{L^{\nr}}}
) is pseudo-split.

It then follows from condition 4 and Propositions
6
and
8
that the following diagram commutes
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(G,K)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(G,K^{\nr})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(G,\widetilde{K^{\nr}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(G,L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(G,L^{\nr})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(G,\widetilde{L^{\nr}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{K^{\nr}/K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{L/K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\widetilde{K^{\nr}}/K^{\nr}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\widetilde{L^{\nr}}/\widetilde{K^{\nr}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{L^{\nr}/L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\widetilde{L^{\nr}}/L^{\nr}}}

.

Because all maps in the diagram are injective, the map
p_{L/K}
is uniquely determined in terms of the maps
p_{K^{\nr}/K},p_{\widetilde{K^{\nr}}/K^{\nr}},p_{\widetilde{L^{\nr}}/\widetilde{K^{\nr}}},p_{\widetilde{L^{\nr}}/L^{\nr}}
, and
p_{L^{\nr}/L}
, each of which is uniquely determined in terms of
G
,
K
, and
L
as a consequence of either Proposition
6
or Proposition
8
.

(Existence)
Let
K
and
L
be arbitrary finite separable extensions of
k
with
K\subset L
. We consider the extensions – with the same notations as above

K_{1}=K^{\nr},K_{2}=\widetilde{K^{\nr}},L_{1}=L^{\nr},L_{2}=\widetilde{L^{\nr}}.

Consider

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\Gamma=\Gal(K_{2}/K),&\Gamma_{1}=\Gal(K_{1}/K),&\Gamma_{2}=\Gal(K_{2}/K_{1})\\
\Gamma^{\prime}=\Gal(L_{2}/L),&\Gamma^{\prime}_{1}=\Gal(L_{1}/L),&\Gamma^{\prime}_{2}=\Gal(L_{2}/L_{1})\end{array}.

Restriction maps fit in a commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma^{\prime}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma^{\prime}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subset}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\res_{K_{2}/K_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subset}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\res_{L_{2}/K_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\res_{L_{2}/L_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\res_{L_{2}/K_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\res_{L_{1}/K_{1}}}

.

By Theorem
5
and Propositions
6
and
8
, we may then form the following diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(G,K)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(G,K_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(G,K_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(G,L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(G,L_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(G,L_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{K_{1}/K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{K_{2}/K_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{L_{2}/K_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{L_{1}/L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma^{\prime}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{L_{2}/L_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma^{\prime}}

.

Because all maps in the diagram are injective, it is sufficient, in order to construct a map
\mathcal{B}(G,K)\to\mathcal{B}(G,L)
that makes the rectangle commute, to prove that

p_{L_{2}/K_{2}}\circ p_{K_{2}/K_{1}}\circ p_{K_{1}/K}(\mathcal{B}(G,K))\subset p_{L_{2}/L_{1}}\circ p_{L_{1}/L}(\mathcal{B}(G,L)).

We proceed in two steps in order to exploit the features of the two special cases: First, we construct a map
p_{L_{1}/K_{1}}:\mathcal{B}(G,K_{1})\to\mathcal{B}(G,L_{1})
making the right-hand square commute. Then, we exploit the uniqueness of this map to construct the map
p_{L/K}
.

Step 1:
By the same argument as before, it is sufficient to prove that

p_{L_{2}/K_{2}}\circ p_{K_{2}/K_{1}}(\mathcal{B}(G,K_{1}))\subset p_{L_{2}/L_{1}}(\mathcal{B}(G,L_{1})).

Furthermore, by
G(K_{1})
-equivariance, it is sufficient to check that

p_{L_{2}/K_{2}}\circ p_{K_{2}/K_{1}}(A(G_{K_{1}},S_{1}))\subset p_{L_{2}/L_{1}}(\mathcal{B}(G,L_{1}))

for some maximal
K_{1}
-split torus
S_{1}\subset G_{K_{1}}
. Let
S_{1}
be such a maximal
K_{1}
-split torus in
G_{K_{1}}
. By Proposition
8
and Lemma
9
, the image
p_{K_{2}/K_{1}}(A(G_{K_{1}},S_{1}))
lies in the apartment
A(G_{K_{2}},S_{2})
associated to the unique maximal
K_{2}
-split torus
S_{2}\subset G_{K_{2}}
that contains
(S_{1})_{K_{2}}
. Now, by Theorem
1
, the image
p_{L_{2}/K_{2}}(A(G_{K_{2}},S_{2}))
is precisely
A(G_{L_{2}},T_{2})
, with
T_{2}=(S_{2})_{L_{2}}
. As
T_{2}
is defined over
K_{1}
(Lemma
9
), it is in particular defined over
L_{1}
. Hence, the action of
\Gamma^{\prime}_{2}
on
B(G,L_{2})
leaves
A(G_{L_{2}},T_{2})
invariant. Because
p_{L_{2}/K_{2}}
is
\Gamma^{\prime}_{2}
-equivariant, by Proposition
10
, we have the inclusion

p_{L_{2}/K_{2}}\circ p_{K_{2}/K_{1}}(A(G_{K_{1}},S_{1}))\subset A(G_{L_{2}},T_{2})^{\Gamma^{\prime}_{2}}.

To conclude, let
T_{1}
be a maximal
L_{1}
-split torus in
G_{L_{1}}
containing
(S_{1})_{L_{1}}
. By the same reasoning as above, the image
p_{L_{2}/L_{1}}(A(G_{L_{1}},T_{1}))
lies in the apartment
A(G_{L_{2}},T^{\prime}_{2})
associated to the unique maximal
L_{2}
-split torus of
G_{L_{2}}
containing
(T_{1})_{L_{2}}
. Furthermore, by construction of
p_{L_{2}/L_{1}}
, we must then have

p_{L_{2}/L_{1}}(A(G_{L_{1}},S_{1}))=A(G_{L_{2}},T^{\prime}_{2})^{\Gamma^{\prime}_{2}}.

Observe that the torus
T^{\prime}_{2}
must then contain
(S_{1})_{L_{2}}
. By uniqueness of the maximal torus in
(Z_{G_{K_{1}}}(S_{1}))_{L_{2}}
, we must then have
T^{\prime}_{2}=T_{2}
. Hence, we have the inclusion

p_{L_{2}/K_{2}}\circ p_{K_{2}/K_{1}}(A(G_{K_{1}},S_{1}))\subset p_{L_{2}/L_{1}}(A(G_{L_{1}},T_{1})),

which completes the proof.

We have thus proved the existence of a map
p_{L_{1}/K_{1}}:\mathcal{B}(G,K_{1})\to\mathcal{B}(G,L_{1})
such that the square
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(G,K_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(G,K_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(G,L_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(G,L_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{L_{1}/K_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{K_{2}/K_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{L_{2}/K_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{L_{2}/L_{1}}}

commutes. By
G(K_{1})
-equivariance and injectivity of
p_{L_{2}/K_{2}}\circ p_{K_{2}/K_{1}}
and
p_{L_{2}/L_{1}}
, the map
p_{L_{1}/K_{1}}
must be
G(K_{1})
-equivariant. Moreover, it follows from the construction that it maps each apartment
A(G_{K_{1}},S_{1})
to the apartment
A(G_{L_{1}},T_{1})
associated to the unique maximal
L_{1}
-split torus such that
(S_{1})_{L_{1}}\subset T_{1}
. The restriction map

p_{L_{1}/K_{1}}:A(G_{K_{1}},S_{1})\to A(G_{L_{1}},T_{1})

is then affine because, letting
S_{2}
be the unique maximal
K_{2}
-split torus of
G_{K_{2}}
containing
S_{1}
and
T_{2}=(S_{2})_{L_{2}}
, we have the commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(G_{K_{1}},S_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(G_{K_{2}},S_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(G_{L_{1}},T_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(G_{L_{2}},T_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{L_{1}/K_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{K_{2}/K_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{L_{2}/K_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{L_{2}/L_{1}}}

,

where
p_{L_{2}/K_{2}}\circ p_{K_{2}/K_{1}}
and
p_{L_{2}/L_{1}}
are affine and injective.

We also prove that
p_{L_{1}/K_{1}}
is
\Gamma^{\prime}_{1}
-equivariant, which will also follow formally from Galois-equivariance properties of the other maps in the square. Let
\gamma^{\prime}_{1}\in\Gamma^{\prime}_{1}
, a lift
\gamma^{\prime}
of
\gamma^{\prime}_{1}
to
L_{2}
, the restriction
\gamma=\res_{L_{2}/K_{2}}(\gamma^{\prime})
, and the restriction
\gamma_{1}=\res_{K_{2}/K_{1}}(\gamma)
. Then, Proposition
10
implies that

\begin{array}[]{ccll}p_{L_{2}/L_{1}}\circ p_{\gamma^{\prime}_{1}}\circ p_{L_{1}/K_{1}}\circ p_{\gamma_{1}}^{-1}&=&p_{\gamma^{\prime}}\circ p_{L_{2}/L_{1}}\circ j\circ p_{\gamma_{1}}^{-1}&(\Gamma^{\prime}\text{-equivariance of }p_{L_{2}/L_{1}})\\
&=&p_{\gamma^{\prime}}\circ p_{L_{2}/K_{2}}\circ p_{K_{2}/K_{1}}\circ p_{\gamma_{1}}^{-1}&(\text{commutativity of the diagram})\\
&=&p_{L_{2}/K_{2}}\circ p_{\gamma}\circ p_{K_{2}/K_{1}}\circ p_{\gamma_{1}}^{-1}&(\Gamma^{\prime}\text{- equivariance of }p_{L_{2}/K_{2}})\\
&=&p_{L_{2}/K_{2}}\circ p_{K_{2}/K_{1}}&(\Gamma\text{-equivariance of }p_{K_{2}/K_{1}})\\
&=&p_{L_{2}/L_{1}}\circ p_{L_{1}/K_{1}}\end{array},

and therefore

p_{\gamma^{\prime}_{1}}\circ p_{L_{1}/K_{1}}\circ p_{\gamma_{1}}^{-1}=p_{L_{1}/K_{1}}.

Step 2:
Our second and final step is to deduce that, given an apartment
A(G_{K},S)
in
\mathcal{B}(G,K)
, we have

p_{L_{1}/K_{1}}\circ p_{K_{1}/K}(A(G_{K},S))\subset p_{L_{1}/L}(A(G_{L},T)),

for some maximal
L
-split torus
T
of
G_{L}
such that
S_{L}\subset T
.
First of all, it follows from Proposition
6
and the construction of
p_{L_{1}/K_{1}}
that the image
p_{L_{1}/K_{1}}\circ p_{K_{1}/K}(A(G_{K},S))
lies in the apartment
A(G_{L_{1}},T_{1})
associated a certain maximal
L_{1}
-split torus
T_{1}
containing
S
and is
\Gamma^{\prime}_{1}
-equivariant.
Consequently, we have the inclusion

p_{L_{1}/K_{1}}\circ p_{K_{1}/K}(A(G_{K},S))\subset(\mathcal{B}(Z_{G_{K}}(S),G,L_{1}))^{\Gamma^{\prime}_{1}},

with the notations of the Appendix.
By Proposition
7
, we deduce

p_{L_{1}/K_{1}}\circ p_{K_{1}/K}(A(G_{K},S))\subset p_{L_{1}/L}(\mathcal{B}(Z_{G_{K}}(S),G,L)).

Let
x\in A(G_{K},S)
. The above inclusion implies that there exists a maximal
L
-split torus
T
of
G_{L}
containing
S_{L}
such that

p_{L_{1}/K_{1}}\circ p_{K_{1}/K}(x)\in p_{L_{1}/L}(A(G_{L},T))

Because the right-hand side is stable under the action of
S(K)\subset T(L)
, we get that

p_{L_{1}/K_{1}}\circ p_{K_{1}/K}(S(K)\cdot x)\subset p_{L_{1}/L}(A(G_{L},T))

and, passing to the convex hull,

p_{L_{1}/K_{1}}\circ p_{K_{1}/K}(A(G_{K},S))\subset p_{L_{1}/L}(A(G_{L},T)).

We then deduce by the same arguments as before that there must exist a
G(K)
-equivariant map

p_{L/K}:\mathcal{B}(G,K)\to\mathcal{B}(G,L)

such that
p_{L_{1}/K_{1}}\circ p_{K_{1}/K}=p_{L_{1}/L}\circ p_{L/K}
. Moreover, we have
p_{L/K}(A(G_{K},S))\subset A(G_{L},T)
and, by
G(K)
-equivariance, we find that for each
S
, there must exist a maximal
L
-split torus
T
containing
S_{L}
such that
p_{L/K}(A(G_{K},S))\subset A(G_{L},T)
. The same argument as before then proves that the restriction of
p_{L/K}
to
A(G_{K},S)
is affine, which establishes that
p_{L/K}
satisfies condition 1.
To complete the proof of Theorem
11
, we now need to establish conditions 3 and 4.

Condition 4 is an easy consequence of the uniqueness conditions. Indeed, if
K\subset L\subset M
with
M/k
a finite separable extension, then we have a commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(G,K)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(G,K_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(G,L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(G,L_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(G,M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(G,M_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{K_{2}/K_{1}}\circ p_{K_{1}/K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{L/K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{L_{2}/K_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{L_{2}/L_{1}}\circ p_{L_{1}/L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{M/L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{M_{2}/L_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{M_{2}/M_{1}}\circ p_{M_{1}/M}}

.

Furthermore, because
G_{K_{2}}
is pseudo-split, we know by Theorem
1
that
p_{M_{2}/L_{2}}\circ p_{L_{2}/K_{2}}=p_{M_{2}/K_{2}}
.
Therefore, we have the commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(G,K)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(G,K_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(G,M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(G,M_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{K_{2}/K_{1}}\circ p_{K_{1}/K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{M/L}\circ p_{L/K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{M_{2}/K_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{M_{2}/M_{1}}\circ p_{M_{1}/M}}

Because
p_{M/K}
is, by construction, the only map
\mathcal{B}(G,K)\to\mathcal{B}(G,M)
that makes the above rectangle commute, we must have

p_{M/K}=p_{M/L}\circ p_{L/K}.

Lastly, establishing condition 3 merely requires adapting the Galois-equivariance arguments we previously brought forth. Indeed, if
L/K
is Galois, then
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
are Galois over
K
. Furthermore, setting

\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\prime}=\Gal(L_{2}/K),\widetilde{\Gamma}_{1}=\Gal(L_{1}/K),\Sigma=\Gal(L/K),

we have a diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\prime}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\res_{K_{2}/K_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\res_{L_{2}/L_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\res_{L_{2}/K_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\res_{L_{1}/K_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\res_{L_{1}/L}}

.

Recall that, by Theorem
5
, we have the following actions
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(G,K)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(G,K_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(G,K_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(G,L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(G,L_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(G,L_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{L/K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{K_{1}/K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{K_{2}/K_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{L_{2}/K_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{L_{1}/L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\prime}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{L_{2}/L_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\prime}}

.

Then, repeated applications of Proposition
10
yield

p_{L_{2}/K_{2}}\circ p_{K_{2}/K_{1}}\circ p_{K_{1}/K}(\mathcal{B}(G,K))\subset\mathcal{B}(G,L_{2})^{\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\prime}}.

By commutativity of the diagram, we get

p_{L_{2}/L_{1}}\circ p_{L_{1}/L}\circ p_{L/K}(\mathcal{B}(G,K))\subset\mathcal{B}(G,L_{2})^{\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\prime}}.

More precisely, we may write

p_{L_{2}/L_{1}}\circ p_{L_{1}/L}\circ p_{L/K}(\mathcal{B}(G,K))\subset\mathcal{B}(G,L_{2})^{\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\prime}}\cap p_{L_{2}/L_{1}}\circ p_{L_{1}/L}(\mathcal{B}(G,L)).

Then, using Proposition
10
and the injectivity of
p_{L_{2}/L_{1}}\circ p_{L_{1}/L}
, we get

p_{L_{2}/L_{1}}\circ p_{L_{1}/L}\circ p_{L/K}(\mathcal{B}(G,K))\subset p_{L_{2}/L_{1}}\circ p_{L_{1}/L}(\mathcal{B}(G,L)^{\Sigma}),

and finally by injectivity

p_{L/K}(\mathcal{B}(G,K))\subset\mathcal{B}(G,L)^{\Sigma},

which completes the proof.
∎

Remark 12
.

We used Proposition
7
in Step 2 of the existence proof of Theorem
11
to ensure that
p_{L/K}
maps the apartment into an apartment
A(G_{L},T)
for a torus
T\subset G_{L}

that contains
S_{L}
. At that stage of the proof, we could have argued directly from Proposition
6
that
p_{L_{1}/K_{1}}\circ p_{K_{1}/K}(A(G_{K},S))
lies in
\mathcal{B}(G,L_{1})^{\Gamma^{\prime}_{1}}=p_{L_{1}/L}(\mathcal{B}(G,L))
, and thus that, by convexity, the image lies in the image of some apartment
p_{L_{1}/L}(A(G_{L},T))
. However, the torus
T
need not contain
S_{L}
.

We conclude this section by generalising two results that held in the two special cases of Theorem
11
we studied earlier.

First of all, we prove that, given an apartment
A
in a building
\mathcal{B}(G,K)
, there exists a Galois extension
L/K
such that
G_{L}
is pseudo-split and such that
A
is the set of fixed points of an apartment
A_{L}\subset\mathcal{B}(G,L)
that is stable under the action of
\Gal(L/K)
.

Proposition 13
.

Let
K
be a finite separable extension of
k
and let
A(G,S)
be an apartment in
\mathcal{B}(G,K)
. There exists a maximal torus
T
of
G
containing
S
and a finite Galois extension
L/K
satisfying the following:

1.

The torus
T_{L}
is split.

2.

The injection
p_{L/K}:\mathcal{B}(G,K)\to\mathcal{B}(G,L)
maps
A(G,S)
isomorphically onto the set of
\Gal(L/K)
-fixed points of
A(G_{L},T_{L})
.

Proof.

Let
K^{\nr}
be the strict henselisation of
K
and
\widetilde{K}^{\nr}
be the minimal Galois extension that splits
G_{K^{\nr}}
. By
[
Lou21
, Lemme 4.8]
, there exists a maximal
K^{\nr}
-split torus
S^{\nr}
that contains
S_{K^{\nr}}
and is defined over
K
. Because
G_{K^{\nr}}
is quasi-split, there exists a unique maximal torus
T
of
G_{K^{\nr}}
that contains
S^{\nr}
. The torus
T
is defined over
K
, because
S^{\nr}
is, and splits over
\widetilde{K}^{\nr}
. Moreover, Proposition
6
yields

p_{K^{\nr}/K}(A(G,S))\subset A(G_{K^{\nr}},S^{\nr})

and Proposition
8
yields

p_{\widetilde{K}^{\nr}/K^{\nr}}(A(G_{K^{\nr}},S^{\nr}))\subset A(G_{\widetilde{K}^{\nr}},T_{\widetilde{K}^{\nr}}).

In particular,

p_{\widetilde{K}^{\nr}/K}(A(G,S))\subset A(G_{\widetilde{K}^{\nr}},T_{\widetilde{K}^{\nr}}).

Now, let
L\subset\widetilde{K}^{\nr}
be a finite Galois extension of
K
such that
T_{L}
is split. By Theorem
1
, we have

p_{\widetilde{K}^{\nr}/L}(A(G_{L},T_{L}))=A(G_{\widetilde{K}^{\nr}},T_{\widetilde{K}^{\nr}}).

In other words,

p_{\widetilde{K}^{\nr}/L}\circ p_{L/K}((A(G,S))\subset p_{\widetilde{K}^{\nr}/L}(A(G_{L},T_{L}))

and, by injectivity, we have

p_{L/K}(A(G,S))\subset A(G_{L},T_{L}).

Because
T
is defined over
K
, the apartment
A(G_{L},T_{L})
is stable under the action of
\Gal(L/K)
and the set of
\Gal(L/K)
-fixed points is a non-empty affine space under
V(G_{L},T_{L})^{\Gal(L/K)}=V(G,S)
. By considering dimensions, we thus see that

p_{L/K}(A(G,S))=A(G_{L},T_{L})^{\Gal(L/K)}.

∎

Secondly, we prove a general version of Proposition
10
.

Theorem 14
.

Let
K,L,M
be finite separable extensions of
k
such that
K\subset L\subset M
. Assume that
L
and
M
are Galois over
K
. Then, the map
p_{M/L}
is
\Gal(M/K)
-equivariant if we let
\Gal(M/K)
act on
\mathcal{B}(G,L)
through the restriction map
\res_{M/L}:\Gal(M/K)\to\Gal(L/K)
.

Proof.

The argument is a combination of Proposition
10
and some ideas of the proof of Theorem
14
. Recall that, by construction, the map
p_{M/L}
is the only map that makes the following diagram commute

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(G,L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(G,L_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(G,L_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(G,M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(G,M_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(G,M_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{M/L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{L_{1}/L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{L_{2}/L_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{M_{2}/L_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{M_{1}/M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{M_{2}/M_{1}}}

,

where, as in the proof of Theorem
11
, we let

L_{1}=L^{\nr},L_{2}=\widetilde{L^{\nr}},M_{1}=M^{\nr},M_{2}=\widetilde{M^{\nr}}.

Note that all extensions in question are Galois over
K
under the assumption that
L
and
M
are Galois over
K
.
Then, letting
\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gal(M/K)
and denoting by
\gamma^{\prime}_{1}\in\Gal(M_{1}/K)
a lift of
\gamma^{\prime}
, and by
\gamma^{\prime}_{2}\in\Gal(M_{2}/K)
a lift of
\gamma^{\prime}_{1}
, and letting
\gamma_{2}=\res^{M_{2}}_{L_{2}}(\gamma^{\prime}_{2})\in\Gal(L_{2}/K)
, as well as
\gamma_{1}=\res^{L_{2}}_{L_{1}}(\gamma_{2})\in\Gal(L_{1}/K)
, and finally
\gamma=\res^{L^{\nr}}_{L}(\gamma)\in\Gal(L/K)
, repeated applications of Proposition
10
yield:

\begin{array}[]{ccl}p_{M_{2}/M_{1}}\circ p_{M_{1}/M}\circ p_{\gamma^{\prime}}\circ p_{M/L}&=&p_{M_{2}/M_{1}}\circ p_{\gamma^{\prime}_{1}}\circ p_{M_{1}/M}\circ p_{M/L}\\
&=&p_{\gamma^{\prime}_{2}}\circ p_{M_{2}/M_{1}}\circ p_{M_{1}/M}\circ p_{M/L}\\
&=&p_{\gamma^{\prime}_{2}}\circ p_{M_{2}/L_{2}}\circ p_{L_{2}/L_{1}}\circ p_{L_{1}/L}\\
&=&p_{M_{2}/L_{2}}\circ p_{\gamma_{2}}\circ p_{L_{2}/L_{1}}\circ p_{L_{1}/L}\\
&=&p_{M_{2}/L_{2}}\circ p_{L_{2}/L_{1}}\circ p_{\gamma_{1}}\circ p_{L_{1}/L}\\
&=&p_{M_{2}/L_{2}}\circ p_{L_{2}/L_{1}}\circ p_{L_{1}/L}\circ p_{\gamma}\end{array}.

It follows that
p_{\gamma^{\prime}}\circ p_{M/L}\circ p_{\gamma}
makes the above rectangle commute, hence we must have

p_{\gamma^{\prime}}\circ p_{M/L}\circ p_{\gamma}=p_{M/L}.

∎

2
Mapping the building into
G^{\an}

Let
G
be a quasi-reductive group over a local field
k
. We denote its valuation by
\omega
and by
|\cdot|
the absolute value
|\cdot|:x\mapsto e^{-\omega(x)}
. In this section, we construct an injection

\vartheta:\mathcal{B}(G,k)\to G^{\an}

following the general philosophy of
[
RTW10
]
. Recall that the idea is to associate to each special vertex
x
the unique point
\vartheta(x)
in the Shilov boundary of
(\mathcal{G}_{x}^{\circ})^{\an}
, where
\mathcal{G}_{x}^{\circ}
is the parahoric subgroup associated to
x
in
[
Lou21
, Théorème 4.5]
. In the case where the point
x
is not a special vertex, its image is
\pr_{K/k}(\vartheta_{K}(x_{K}))
, where
K
is a suitably chosen non-archimedean extension of
k
such that
x_{K}
becomes a special vertex in
\mathcal{B}(G,K)
.

In our case, because we are reliant on Bruhat-Tits theory for quasi-reductive groups as exposed in
[
Lou21
]
, which requires discretely valued base fields with perfect residue fields, we cannot make sense of the above definition unless the point
x
becomes a special vertex in
\mathcal{B}(G,K)
for a
finite
Galois extension
K/k
(for the simple reason that we do not have a working definition of
\mathcal{B}(G,K)
for an arbitrary non-archimedean extension
K/k
).

The main observation we make here is that there is a large enough amount of such points that embedding them determines a unique embedding of the whole building by a density argument.

2.1
Virtually special points in the building

We first introduce the notion of a virtually special point of the building, which formalises the aforementioned condition, and collect some basic facts. We conclude by proving that virtually special points are dense in the building.

Definition 15
.

Let
x\in\mathcal{B}(G,k)
. We say that
x
is
virtually special
if there exists a finite Galois extension
K/k
such that
G_{K}
is pseudo-split and the canonical injection

p_{K/k}:\mathcal{B}(G,k)\to\mathcal{B}(G,K)

defined in Theorem
11
maps
x
to a special vertex. We denote by
\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{Q}}(G,k)
the set of virtually special points in
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
. If
S\subset G
is a maximal split torus, we denote by
A_{\mathbb{Q}}(G,S)
the set of virtually special points in the apartment
A(G,S)
.

For ease of notation, whenever
K
is a finite Galois extension of
k
and
x\in\mathcal{B}(G,k)
, we will write
x_{K}
instead of
p_{K/k}(x)
in all the following.

The following proposition is a direct consequence of the definition.

Proposition 16
.

If
k^{\prime}/k
is a finite Galois extension, then we have

\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{Q}}(G,k)=p_{k^{\prime}/k}^{-1}(\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{Q}}(G,k^{\prime})).

We also check that the set of virtually special points is preserved under the action of
G(k)
.

Proposition 17
.

The action of
G(k)
on
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
stabilises
\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{Q}}(G,k)
.

Proof.

Let
x\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{Q}}(G,k)
and
g\in G(k)
. Let
K/k
be a finite Galois extension such that
x_{K}
is a special vertex. Because
G(K)
acts on
\mathcal{B}(G,K)
by simplicial automorphisms, the point
gx_{K}
is a special vertex. Moreover, the map
p_{K/k}
is
G(k)
-equivariant, hence
gx_{K}=p_{K/k}(gx)
, which concludes.
∎

To conclude these introductory observations, we check that the set of virtually special points is preserved under the action of a Galois group.

Proposition 18
.

If
k^{\prime}/k
is a finite Galois extension, then the action of
\Gal(k^{\prime}/k)
stabilises
\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{Q}}(G,k^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Let
x\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{Q}}(G,k^{\prime})
and
\sigma\in\Gal(k^{\prime}/k)
. Let
K
be a finite Galois extension of
k
containing
k^{\prime}
such that
G_{K}
is pseudo-split and
x_{K}
is a special vertex. Let
\widetilde{\sigma}\in\Gal(K/k)
be an automorphism of
K
that extends
\sigma
. Then, because the action of
\Gal(K/k)
on
\mathcal{B}(G,K)
preserves the simplicial structure, the point
\widetilde{\sigma}(x_{K})
is special. Moreover, by Theorem
14
, we have
\widetilde{\sigma}(x_{K})=\sigma(x)_{K}
. Hence, the point
\sigma(x)
is virtually special.
∎

Our goal is now to prove the following proposition.

Proposition 19
.

The subset
\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{Q}}(G,k)
is dense in
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
. More precisely, for any facet
\mathbf{f}\subset\mathcal{B}(G,k)
, the subset
\mathbf{f}\cap\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{Q}}(G,k)
is dense in
\mathbf{f}
.

This key result will be established in two steps. We first treat the case where
G
is pseudo-split over
k
. This relies on proving that, for an appropriate choice of coordinate system, the set of virtually special points in an apartment
A(G,T)
is precisely the set of points with rational coordinates.
Then, we treat the general case by exploiting the pseudo-split case. Letting
K/k
be a suitable finite Galois extension such that
G_{K}
is pseudo-split, we prove that, if
x
is a vertex in
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
, then
x_{K}
is the centroid of the facet
\mathbf{f}
of
\mathcal{B}(G,K)
that contains it. In particular, the point
x_{K}
is a convex combination of vertices of
\mathcal{B}(G,K)
with rational coefficients, hence virtually special.

Step 1: The pseudo-split case.

Assume that
G
is pseudo-split over
k
. Let
A=A(G,T)
be an apartment in
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
and
o
be a special vertex in
A
. Denote by
\Phi
the root system
\Phi(G,T)
associated to
T
. Recall from Proposition
3
that, if
K/k
is a finite Galois extension, then the canonical injection
p_{K/k}:\mathcal{B}(G,k)\to\mathcal{B}(G,K)
identifies
A
with the apartment
A_{K}=A(G_{K},T_{K})
in such a way that special vertices in
A
are mapped to special vertices in
A_{K}
. In particular, the point
o_{K}
is a special vertex of
A_{K}
and the affine roots in
A_{K}
are the

\alpha_{a_{K},\gamma}^{o_{K}}=\{x\in A_{K},\langle a_{K},x-o_{K}\rangle+\gamma\geq 0\}

for
a\in\Phi
and
\gamma\in\Gamma^{\prime}_{a_{K}}
. It then follows from
[
BT72
, 1.3.7]
that a special vertex of
A_{K}
is a point
x
such that, for each root
a\in\Phi
, there exists either
\gamma\in\Gamma^{\prime}_{a_{K}}
such that
x\in\partial\alpha^{o_{K}}_{a_{K},\gamma}
or a
\gamma\in\Gamma^{\prime}_{2a_{K}}
such that
x\in\alpha^{o_{K}}_{2a_{K},\gamma}
.

Note also that, because
G
is pseudo-split over
k
, then for each root
a\in\Phi
, we have

\widetilde{G}^{a_{K}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}R_{K\otimes_{k}k_{a}/K}(SL_{2,K\otimes_{k}k_{a}})&\text{if }a\text{ is not multipliable}\\
R_{K\otimes_{k}k_{2a}/K}(BC_{1,K\otimes_{k}k_{2a}})&\text{if }a\text{ is multipliable}\end{array}\right.,

where
k_{a}/k
(resp.
k_{2a}/k
) is purely inseparable. Then, by the proof of
[
Lou21
, Lemme 4.4]
, for each
a\in\Phi
, we have

\Gamma^{\prime}_{a_{K}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\omega((K\otimes_{k}k_{a})^{\times})&\text{if }a\text{ is not multipliable}\\
\frac{1}{4}\omega((K\otimes_{k}k_{2a})^{\times})\setminus\frac{1}{2}\omega((K\otimes_{k}k_{2a})^{\times})&\text{if }a\text{ is multipliable}\end{array}\right..

Note in particular that, letting
e(K/k)
be the ramification index of the extension
K/k
, then

\Gamma^{\prime}_{a_{K}}=\frac{1}{e(K/k)}\Gamma^{\prime}_{a}

and that, if
a
is not multipliable, then

\Gamma^{\prime}_{a}=\omega(k_{a}^{\times})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\mathbb{Z}&\text{if }[k_{a}:k]=1\\
\frac{1}{p^{m_{a}}}\mathbb{Z}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.,

where
p=\mathrm{char}(k)
and
m_{a}
is the least positive integer
m
such that
x^{p^{m}}\in k
for each
x\in k_{a}
.

We are now in position to prove that virtually special points are exactly those points
x\in A
such that
\langle a,x-o\rangle\in\mathbb{Q}
for each
a\in\Phi
. First, by inspection, we see that for each finite Galois extension
K/k
and each root
a\in\Phi
, we have
\Gamma^{\prime}_{a_{K}}\cup\Gamma^{\prime}_{2a_{K}}\subset\mathbb{Q}
. In particular, if
x\in A
is mapped by
p_{K/k}
to a special vertex in
A_{K}
for some finite Galois extension
K/k
, then we have

\langle a,x-o\rangle=\langle a_{K},x_{K}-o_{K}\rangle\in\mathbb{Q}

for each root
a\in\Phi
. Conversely, if
\langle a,x-o\rangle\in\mathbb{Q}
for each
a\in\Phi
, then there exists
N
such that, for each non-multipliable
a
,
\langle a,x-o\rangle\in\frac{1}{N}\Gamma^{\prime}_{a}
(the key point being that, when
a
is non-multipliable, the subset
\Gamma^{\prime}_{a}
is a nontrivial subgroup of
\mathbb{Q}
). Then, letting
K/k
be a totally ramified extension of degree
N
, we immediately check that
x_{K}
is a special vertex in
A_{K}
.

Letting
\Delta
be a basis for
\Phi
, it follows that the virtually special points in
A
are exactly the points
x
with rational coordinates in
A
, setting
o
as an origin and within the coordinate system given by the dual basis of
\Delta
. It is clear from this characterisation that the set of virtually special points in
A
is dense in
A
and closed under convex combinations with rational weights.

We conclude this discussion by proving that furthermore the virtually special points in any facet are dense in that facet. First of all, any vertex
x
is a virtually special point. Indeed, it must satisfy
\langle a,x-o\rangle\in\Gamma^{\prime}_{a}
for
a
ranging in a subset of
\Phi
that forms a basis for
\Span_{\mathbb{Q}}(\Phi)
, and therefore we must have
\langle a,x-o\rangle\in\mathbb{Q}
for all
a\in\Delta
. Next, if
\mathbf{f}
is an arbitrary facet of
A
, the extremal points
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{r}\}
of
\overline{\mathbf{f}}
are vertices of
A
, and thus virtually special points. Then, the convex combinations of the
x_{i}
with positive rational coefficients form a dense subset of
\mathbf{f}
comprised of virtually special points.
∎

Step 2: The general case.

We now let
G
be arbitrary. As in the first step, we prove that vertices are virtually special. Fix a vertex
x
in an apartment
A(G,S)
of
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
. Recall from Proposition
13
that there exists a Galois extension
K/k
and a maximal torus
T
of
G
containing
S
such that

p_{K/k}(A(G,S))=A(G_{K},T_{K})^{\Gal(K/k)}.

Denote by
\mathbf{f}\subset A(G_{K},T_{K})
the facet of
\mathcal{B}(G,K)
that contains
x_{K}
. Because the action of the group
G(K)
preserves the polysimplicial structure and because
p_{K/k}
is
G(k)
-equivariant, the stabiliser
G(k)_{x}
stabilises the facet
\mathbf{f}
. Further, the group
G(k)_{x}
stabilises the set
\{z_{1},\dots,z_{r}\}
of vertices of
\mathbf{f}
. In particular, the centroid
\gamma=\frac{1}{r}\sum_{i=1}^{r}z_{i}
is fixed by
G(k)_{x}
. Indeed, the group
G(k)_{x}
acts isometrically on
\mathcal{B}(G,K)
hence, for any
g\in G(k)_{x}
, the map

g:A(G_{K},T_{K})\to gA(G_{K},T_{K})

induced by
g
is an isometry and thus affine, hence preserves convex combinations.

On the other hand, observe that the Galois group
\Gamma=\Gal(K/k)
acts on
A(G_{K},T_{K})
by affine automorphisms that preserve the polysimplicial structure, and fixes
x_{K}
. Consequently, the group
\Gamma
preserves the facet
\mathbf{f}
and, by similar arguments, also fixes the centroid
\gamma
. Hence, there exists
y\in A(G,S)
such that
\gamma=y_{K}
. By
G(k)
-equivariance and injectivity of
p_{K/k}
, the group
G(k)_{x}
must then fix the point
y
. Because
x
is a vertex, it is the only point of
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
fixed by
G(k)_{x}

[
Sol18
, Proposition 3.10 (a)]
, hence
x=y
and we have

x_{K}=\frac{1}{r}\sum_{i=1}^{r}z_{i}.

By Step 1, each of the
z_{i}
is virtually special, hence, as a convex combination of the
z_{i}
with rational weights, so is
x_{K}
. By Proposition
16
, we deduce that
x
is virtually special. We then deduce that all convex combinations of vertices with rational coefficients are virtually special points, which proves that virtually special points are dense in every facet (and in particular in
A(G,S)
).
∎

We conclude with this transitivity property:

Proposition 20
.

Let
x,y\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{Q}}(G,k)
. Then, there exists a finite Galois extension
K/k
, a split maximal torus
T
in
G_{K}
, and
t\in T(K)
such that
y_{K}=tx_{K}
.

Proof.

We may and do assume that
G
is pseudo-split over
k
.

Let
A(G,T)
be an apartment of
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
that contains
x
and
y
. Let
\Delta
be a basis for
\Phi(G,T)
. Then, it follows from the proof of Proposition
19
that we have
\langle a,y-x\rangle\in\mathbb{Q}
for each
a\in\Delta
. Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\forall a\in\Delta,\langle a,y-x\rangle\in\frac{1}{N}\omega(k^{\times}).

Then, if
K/k
is a totally ramified Galois extension of degree
ND
, where
D
is the determinant of the Cartan matrix
(\langle a,(a^{\prime})^{\vee}\rangle)_{a,a^{\prime}\in\Delta}
of
\Phi(G,T)
, there exists a
t\in T(K)
such that

\forall a\in\Delta,\langle a,y_{K}-x_{K}\rangle=-\omega(a(t))

and therefore
y_{K}=tx_{K}
.
Indeed, letting
a^{\vee}\in X_{*}(T)
denote the coroot associated to
a
for each
a\in\Delta
, we may look for
t
of the form
\prod_{a\in\Delta}a^{\vee}(\varpi_{K})^{n_{a}}
for
n_{a}\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Then, the condition
y_{K}=tx_{K}
is equivalent to the condition

\forall a\in\Delta,\sum_{a^{\prime}\in\Delta}\langle a,(a^{\prime})^{\vee}\rangle n_{a^{\prime}}=\frac{ND}{\omega(\varpi)}\langle a,y_{K}-x_{K}\rangle\in D\mathbb{Z}.

This system has a unique solution
(n_{a})_{a\in\Delta}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\Delta}
, which completes the proof.

∎

Note that, in the case when
G
is pseudo-split over
k
, the proof yields a better result.

Corollary 21
.

Assume that
G
is pseudo-split over
k
. Let
x
and
y
in
\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{Q}}(G,k)
. Then, for any split maximal torus
T
such that the apartment
A(G,T)
contains
x
and
y
, there exists a finite Galois extension
K/k
and an element
t\in T(K)
such that
y_{K}=tx_{K}
.

2.2
Mapping the virtually special points

We now construct a canonical
G(k)
-equivariant map

\vartheta:\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{Q}}(G,k)\to G^{\an}

The idea is to associate to each point its ”analytic stabiliser”, defined as follows: Let
K/k
be a finite Galois extension such that
G_{K}
is pseudo-split and such that
x_{K}
is special. Denote by
\mathcal{G}_{x_{K}}^{\circ}
the parahoric
K^{\circ}
-model of
G_{K}
associated to the facet
\{x_{K}\}
, as defined in
[
Lou21
, 4.2]
. Then, the Berkovich analytification
(\mathcal{G}_{x_{K}}^{\circ})^{\an}
constructed in
[
RTW10
, 1.2.4]
is a strictly
K
-affinoid subgroup of
G^{\an}
and, by
[
RTW10
, Proposition 1.3]
, the projection
G_{x}:=\pr_{K/k}((\mathcal{G}_{x_{K}}^{\circ})^{\an})
is a
k
-affinoid subgroup of
G
. This subgroup does not depend on the choice of the extension
K
. Indeed, if
K
and
K^{\prime}
are two such extensions and
L
a common finite Galois extension, then it follows from
[
Lou21
, Théorème 4.5]
that

\mathcal{G}_{x_{L}}^{\circ}=\mathcal{G}_{x_{K}}^{\circ}\otimes_{K^{\circ}}{L^{\circ}}=\mathcal{G}_{x_{K^{\prime}}}^{\circ}\otimes_{K^{\circ}}{L^{\circ}}

and therefore

(\mathcal{G}_{x_{L}}^{\circ})^{\an}=(\mathcal{G}_{x_{K}}^{\circ})^{\an}\hat{\otimes}_{K}L=(\mathcal{G}_{x_{K^{\prime}}}^{\circ})^{\an}\hat{\otimes}_{K^{\prime}}L

and finally

\pr_{L/k}((\mathcal{G}_{x_{L}}^{\circ})^{\an})=\pr_{K/k}(\pr_{L/K}((\mathcal{G}_{x_{L}}^{\circ})^{\an}))=\pr_{K/k}((\mathcal{G}_{x_{K}}^{\circ})^{\an})

and, by the same argument

\pr_{L/k}((\mathcal{G}_{x_{L}}^{\circ})^{\an})=\pr_{K^{\prime}/k}((\mathcal{G}_{x_{K^{\prime}}}^{\circ})^{\an}).

Then, we have the following proposition:

Proposition 22
.

Let
x
be a point in
\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{Q}}(G,k)
. The
k
-affinoid subgroup
G_{x}
has a unique Shilov boundary point, which we denote by
\vartheta(x)
.

Proof.

Consider a finite Galois extension such that
G_{K}
is pseudo-split and
x_{K}
is a special vertex.

By construction, the
K
-analytic subgroup
G_{x}\hat{\otimes}_{k}K
is the analytification of the
K^{\circ}
-group scheme
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}_{x_{K}}^{\circ}
and, as such, is strictly
K
-affinoid. Because
\mathcal{G}
is smooth, it follows from
[
RTW10
, 1.2.4]
and
[
Ber90
, 2.4.4]
that the reduction map

\rho:\mathcal{G}^{\an}\to\mathcal{G}_{\widetilde{K}}

establishes a one-to-one correspondence between the Shilov boundary of
G_{x}\hat{\otimes}_{k}K
and the generic points of the irreducible components of the reduction
\mathcal{G}_{\widetilde{K}}
.

As
\mathcal{G}_{\widetilde{K}}
is connected, the strictly
K
-affinoid domain
G_{x}\hat{\otimes}_{k}K
has only one Shilov boundary point and the same goes for
G_{x}
as the projection
\pr_{K/k}
maps the Shilov boundary of
G_{x}\hat{\otimes}_{k}K
onto that of
G_{x}

[
Ber90
, Proof of Proposition 2.4.5]
.

∎

Proposition 23
.

For any
x\in B_{\mathbb{Q}}(G,k)
and
g\in G(k)
, we have

G_{gx}=gG_{x}g^{-1}\text{ as well as }\vartheta(gx)=g\vartheta(x)g^{-1}.

Proof.

Let
K/k
be a finite Galois extension such that
G_{K}
is pseudo-split and
x_{K}
is a special vertex of
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
. Denote by
\mathcal{G}_{x_{K}}
the parahoric
K^{\circ}
-model of
G
associated to
x_{K}
. Then, the automorphism
\inta(g)
of
G
induces an isomorphism from
\mathcal{G}_{x_{K}}
to a
K^{\circ}
-model
\mathcal{H}
of
G
. We prove that
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{G}_{gx_{K}}
, which will establish that the analytic automorphism
\inta(g)^{\an}
of
G^{\an}
maps
G_{x}
to
G_{gx}
.

The group
\mathcal{H}
is smooth, connected, affine and, for each finite unramified extension
K^{\prime}/K
, we have

\mathcal{H}(K^{\prime\circ})=gP_{x_{K^{\prime}}}^{\circ}g^{-1}.

By
[
Lou21
, Théorème 4.5]
, it is sufficient to prove that
gP_{x_{K^{\prime}}}^{\circ}g^{-1}=P_{gx_{K^{\prime}}}^{\circ}
for each unramified extension
K^{\prime}/K
to conclude. Let
T
be a split maximal
K
-torus such that the apartment
A(G_{K},T)
contains
x_{K}
and
gx_{K}
. The Bruhat decomposition
[
Sol18
, Theorem 3.14 (a)]
rewrites as

G(K)=P_{gx_{K}}N_{G}(T)(K)P_{x_{K}}.

By construction, we have

N_{G}(T)(K)P_{x_{K}}=N_{G}(T)(K)P^{\circ}_{x_{K}}=P_{x_{K}}^{\circ}N_{G}(T)(K)

and therefore we also have
G(K)=P_{gx_{K}}^{\circ}N_{G}(T)(K)P_{x_{K}}^{\circ}
. Further, because
x_{K}
is special, we may write
G(K)=P_{gx_{K}}^{\circ}Z_{G}(T)(K)P_{x_{K}}^{\circ}
. Write
g=pzp^{\prime}
, with
p\in P_{gx_{K}}^{\circ}
,
p^{\prime}\in P_{x_{K}}^{\circ}
,
z\in Z_{G}(T)(K)
.
We then have
gx=zx
and it is sufficient to check that
zP_{x_{K}}^{\circ}z^{-1}=P^{\circ}_{zx_{K}}
.

First of all, for any
a\in\Phi(G_{K},T)
and
r\in\mathbb{R}
, we have
zU_{a,r}z^{-1}=U_{a,r-\omega(a(z))}
4
4

4

We think of
X^{*}(T)
as included in
X^{*}(Z_{G}(T))\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Q}
, and thus

zU_{a,x_{K}}z^{-1}=U_{a,z\cdot x_{K}}\text{ as well as }zU_{x_{K}}z^{-1}=U_{z\cdot x_{K}}.

Consequently,

z\mathcal{Z}^{\circ}(K^{\circ})(N_{G}(T)(K)\cap U_{x_{K}})z^{-1}=\mathcal{Z}^{\circ}(K^{\circ})(N_{G}(T)(K)\cap U_{z\cdot x_{K}}).

Because
P_{x_{K}}^{\circ}
(resp.
P_{z\cdot x_{K}}^{\circ}
) is spanned by the
U_{a,x_{K}}
(resp.
U_{a,z\cdot x_{K}}
) and
\mathcal{Z}^{\circ}(K^{\circ})(N_{G}(T)(K)\cap U_{x_{K}})
(resp.

\mathcal{Z}^{\circ}(K^{\circ})(N_{G}(T)(K)\cap U_{z\cdot x_{K}})
), we do have
zP_{x_{K}}^{\circ}z^{-1}=P^{\circ}_{z\cdot x_{K}}
.

Because the same reasoning applies if we substitute any finite unramified extension
K^{\prime}
of
K
for
K
in the above proof, we find
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{G}_{g\cdot x_{K}}^{\circ}
. Passing to Berkovich analytifications, the automorphism
\inta(g)^{\an}
maps
G_{x}
onto
G_{gx}
. As it is an analytic isomorphism, it also maps the unique Shilov boundary point of
G_{x}
to that of
G_{gx}
, whence

\vartheta(gx)=g\vartheta(x)g^{-1}.

∎

Lemma 24
.

Let
K/k
be a finite Galois extension. We have the commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{Q}}(G,K)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{K}^{\an}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{Q}}(G,k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\an}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vartheta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{K/k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{K/k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vartheta}

.

Proof.

Let
x\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{Q}}(G,k)
and
L/K
be a finite Galois extension such that
G_{L}
is pseudo-split and
x_{L}
is a special point. Then, we have

G_{x_{K}}=\pr_{L/K}((\mathcal{G}_{x_{L}}^{\circ})^{\an})

as well as

G_{x}=\pr_{L/k}((\mathcal{G}_{x_{L}}^{\circ})^{\an})=\pr_{K/k}(G_{x_{K}}).

Because the projection
\pr_{K/k}
maps Shilov boundaries to Shilov boundaries, we have
\vartheta(x)=\pr_{K/k}(\vartheta(x_{K}))
.
∎

2.3
Extending to the building

Our goal is now to extend the map
\vartheta
to the whole of
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
. Given that we have the commutative diagram established in Lemma
24
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{Q}}(G,K)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{K}^{\an}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{Q}}(G,k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\an}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vartheta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{K/k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{K/k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vartheta}

for each finite Galois extension
K/k
, it is sufficient to work in the case where
G
is pseudo-split, which we henceforth assume.

We begin by establishing a formula for the norm
\vartheta(x)
associated to a point of the building.

Let
T
be a split maximal torus and let
\Phi=\Phi(G,T)
be the root system corresponding to
T
. Consider a minimal pseudo-parabolic subgroup
P
containing
T
and let
\Phi^{+}=\Phi(P,T)
be the set of positive roots corresponding to
P
. Denote by
\Phi_{\nd}
the set of non divisible roots in
\Phi
and by
\Phi^{+}_{\nd}=\Phi^{+}\cap\Phi_{\nd}
. Then the multiplication map yields an open immersion

\prod_{\alpha\in\Phi^{+}_{\nd}}U_{-\alpha}\times Z_{G}(T)\times\prod_{\alpha\in\Phi^{+}_{\nd}}U_{\alpha}\hookrightarrow G

whose image is independent of the order on
\Phi^{+}_{\nd}
. We denote this image by
\Omega(T,P)
and call it the open Bruhat cell corresponding to the pair
(T,P)
.

If
o
is a special point in
A(G,T)
, then,
o
determines integral models
\mathcal{U}_{a,o}
for each
U_{a}
and an integral model
\mathcal{G}
for
G
defined in
[
Lou21
, 4.2]
. Letting
\mathcal{Z}^{\circ}
be the open subgroup of the Néron model of
Z=Z_{G}(T)
containing its generic fibre and the connected component of its special fibre, it follows from
[
Lou21
, Théorème 4.5]
that the above open immersion descends to an open immersion of
k^{\circ}
-schemes

\prod_{\alpha\in\Phi^{+}_{\nd}}\mathcal{U}_{-\alpha,o}\times\mathcal{Z}^{\circ}\times\prod_{\alpha\in\Phi^{+}_{\nd}}\mathcal{U}_{\alpha,o}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{G}.

We denote by
\Omega(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{P})
the image of this immersion.

For each non-divisible root
\alpha\in\,\Phi_{\nd}
, we have an
\mathcal{T}
-equivariant isomorphism of
k^{\circ}
-schemes:

\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\simeq\mathcal{U}_{2{\alpha}}\times\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}/\mathcal{U}_{2{\alpha}},

where
\mathcal{T}=\Spec(k^{\circ}[X^{*}(T)])\subset\mathcal{Z}^{\circ}
. Moreover, each
\mathcal{U}_{2{\alpha}}
(resp.
\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}/\mathcal{U}_{2{\alpha}}
) is an affine space over
k^{\circ}
on which
\mathcal{T}
acts linearly via the character
2{\alpha}
(resp.
{\alpha}
).
Fix
\mathcal{T}
-equivariant isomorphisms

u_{\alpha}:\mathbb{A}^{N_{\alpha}}_{k^{\circ}}\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}/\mathcal{U}_{2{\alpha}}\text{ and }u_{2{\alpha}}:\mathbb{A}^{N_{2{\alpha}}}_{k^{\circ}}\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathcal{U}_{2{\alpha}}

for each non-divisible root
{\alpha}
. These yield a
\mathcal{T}
-equivariant open immersion of
k^{\circ}
-schemes

\prod_{{\alpha}\in\ \Phi^{+}}\mathbb{A}^{N_{-\alpha}}_{k^{\circ}}\times\mathcal{Z}^{\circ}\times\prod_{{\alpha}\in\ \Phi^{+}}\mathbb{A}^{N_{\alpha}}_{k^{\circ}}\overset{\sim}{\to}\Omega(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{P})\subset\mathcal{G}.

Lastly, we denote by
\vartheta_{Z}(\ast)
the only point in the Shilov boundary of
\mathcal{Z}^{\an}
, a multiplicative norm on
k[Z]
. The uniqueness of that point follows from Proposition
22
applied to
Z
. Note that the Bruhat-Tits building of the quasi-reductive group
Z

[
CGP15
, Remark C.2.12 (1)]
reduces to a point, which makes this notation consistent with the above.

Proposition 25
.

Assume that
G
is pseudo-split. We keep the above notation. Let

{\mathbf{\Phi}}=\{(a,i)\in\,\Phi\times\mathbb{N},1\leq i\leq N_{a}\}.

1.

The point
\vartheta(o)
lies in
\Omega(T,P)^{\an}
and satisfies for each
f=\sum_{\nu\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbf{\Phi}}}f_{\nu}\xi^{\nu}\in k[\Omega(T,P)]=k[Z][(\xi_{\alpha,i})_{(\alpha,i)\in{\mathbf{\Phi}}}]
,

|f|(\vartheta(o))=\max_{\nu\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbf{\Phi}}}|f_{\nu}|(\vartheta_{Z}(\ast)).

2.

Given the identification between
A(G,T)
and
V(G,T)
given by
o
, we have for each
x\in A_{\mathbb{Q}}(G,T)
and
f\in k[\Omega(T,P)]
:

|f|(\vartheta(x))=\max_{\nu\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbf{\Phi}}}|f_{\nu}|(\vartheta_{Z}(\ast))\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\Phi\\
1\leq i\leq N_{\alpha}\end{subarray}}e^{\nu(\alpha,i)\langle\alpha,x\rangle}.

(2)

Proof.

1.

The proof is analogous to that of
[
RTW10
, Proposition 2.6]
.

Recall that
\vartheta(o)
is the only point of the Shilov boundary of the affinoid subgroup
\mathcal{G}^{\an}
of
G^{\an}
, that is, by
[
RTW10
, 1.2.4]
, the only point in
\mathcal{G}^{\an}
that is mapped to the generic point of
\mathcal{G}_{\widetilde{k}}
by the reduction map

\rho:\mathcal{G}^{\an}\to\mathcal{G}_{\widetilde{k}}.

Because
\Omega(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{P})
is an open subset of
\mathcal{G}
that meets the special fibre, its special fibre
\Omega(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{P})_{\widetilde{k}}
contains the generic point of
\mathcal{G}
. As a consequence of the commutativity of the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{0.79184pt}{-14.38034pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

the open subset
\Omega(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{P})^{\an}
of
\mathcal{G}^{\an}
must contain
\vartheta(o)
.
Moreover, the condition that
\vartheta(o)
reduces to the generic point of
\Omega(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{P})_{\widetilde{k}}
may be rewritten as the claim that, for any function
f\in k[\Omega(T,P)]=k[Z][(\xi_{a,i})_{a\in\,_{k}\Phi,1\leq i\leq N_{a}}]
, we have the equivalences:

|f|(\vartheta(o))\leq 1\Longleftrightarrow f\in k^{\circ}[\mathcal{Z}][(\xi_{a,i})_{(\alpha,i)\in{\mathbf{\Phi}}}]

and

|f|(\vartheta(o))<1\Longleftrightarrow f\text{ maps to zero in }(\widetilde{k}\otimes_{k^{\circ}}k^{\circ}[\mathcal{Z}])[(\xi_{\alpha,i})_{(\alpha,i)\in{\mathbf{\Phi}}}].

It follows that the restriction of
\vartheta(o)
to
k[Z]
is the Shilov boundary of the affinoid subgroup
\mathcal{Z}^{\an}\subset Z^{\an}
.

2.

By Proposition
20
there exist a finite Galois extension
K
and
t\in T(K)
such that
x_{K}=t\cdot o_{K}
. By Proposition
23
, we have
\vartheta(x_{K})=t\vartheta(o_{K})t^{-1}
. In particular, because
\inta(t)
preserves
\Omega(T,P)
, we must have
\vartheta(x_{K})\in\Omega(T,P)^{\an}
. Moreover, we have

|f|(\vartheta(x))=|f|(\vartheta(x_{K}))=|\tau^{*}(f)|(\vartheta(o_{K})),

where
\tau
is the only map that makes the following diagram commute
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{{\alpha}\in\ \Phi^{+}}\mathbb{A}^{N_{-\alpha}}_{K}\times\mathcal{Z}\times\prod_{{\alpha}\in\ \Phi^{+}}\mathbb{A}^{N_{\alpha}}_{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{{\alpha}\in\ \Phi^{+}}\mathbb{A}^{N_{-\alpha}}_{K}\times\mathcal{Z}\times\prod_{{\alpha}\in\ \Phi^{+}}\mathbb{A}^{N_{\alpha}}_{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega(T,P)_{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega(T,P)_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{int}(t_{K})_{|\Omega(T,P)_{K}}}

.

Now, because
T_{K}
normalises
U_{\alpha,K}
for each
\alpha\in\Phi_{\nd}
and, further, preserves the weight space decomposition
U_{\alpha,K}\simeq U_{\alpha,K}/U_{2\alpha,K}\times U_{2\alpha,K}
, it follows that
\tau
acts on each
\mathbb{A}_{K}^{N_{\alpha}}
by multiplication by the character
\alpha
.
In other words,

\tau^{*}:K[Z][(\xi_{\alpha,i})_{(\alpha,i)\in{\mathbf{\Phi}}}]\to K[Z][(\xi_{\alpha,i})_{(\alpha,i)\in{\mathbf{\Phi}}}]

is the only morphism of
K[Z]
-algebras that maps
\xi_{\alpha,i}
to
\alpha(t)\xi_{\alpha,i}
for each
\alpha\in\Phi
and
i\in[\![1,N_{\alpha}]\!]
. Consequently, if
f=\sum_{\nu\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbf{\Phi}}}f_{\nu}\xi^{\nu}
, then

\tau^{*}(f)=\sum_{\nu\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbf{\Phi}}}f_{\nu}\prod_{(\alpha,i)\in{\mathbf{\Phi}}}\alpha(t)^{\nu(a,i)}\xi^{\nu}

and therefore

|f|(\vartheta(x))=\max_{\nu\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbf{\Phi}}}|f_{\nu}|(\vartheta_{Z}(\ast))\prod_{(\alpha,i)\in{\mathbf{\Phi}}}|\alpha(t)|^{\nu(a,i)}.

Now, given that,
o_{K}
is taken as origin of
A(G_{K},T_{K})
and that, by definition of the apartment
[
Lou21
, Définition 3.3]
,
t
acts on
A(G_{K},T_{K})
by translation by the vector
\nu(t)\in V(G_{K},T_{K})
such that
\langle\alpha,\nu(t)\rangle=-\omega(\alpha(t))
, we have

|f|(\vartheta(x))=\max_{\nu\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbf{\Phi}}}|f_{\nu}|(\vartheta_{Z}(\ast))\prod_{(\alpha,i)\in{\mathbf{\Phi}}}e^{\nu(\alpha,i)\langle\alpha,x\rangle}.

∎

Theorem 26
.

The map
\vartheta
extends uniquely to a continuous
G(k)
-equivariant map

\vartheta:\mathcal{B}(G,k)\to G^{\an}.

Proof.

Note that, because
\vartheta
is already
G(k)
-equivariant, the continuity of the
G(k)
-action on
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
and
G^{\an}
will immediately imply that the extension is equivariant.
Consequently, all we need to do is prove that, for any point
x\in\mathcal{B}(G,k)
and any sequence
(x_{n})\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{Q}}(G,k)^{\mathbb{N}}
converging to
x
, the sequence
\vartheta(x_{n})
converges in
G^{\an}
and that the limit depends only on
x
.

The proof comprises two steps, one mainly using Berkovich, and one using Bruhat-Tits theory. First of all, we treat the case of a sequence of virtually special points in an apartment converging to a point in that apartment, which follows easily from Proposition
25
. Next, we reduce the general case to the first step using the geometry of the building. The main observation is the fact that, from any sequence of points in the building converging to a point
x
, we may extract a subsequence lying in a single apartment containing
x
, a consequence of the local finiteness of the building.

Step 1:
Assume that
x\in A(G,T)
. Then, it follows from Proposition
25
that, for any sequence
(x_{n})\in A_{\mathbb{Q}}(G,T)^{\mathbb{N}}
converging to
x
, the sequence
\vartheta(x_{n})
converges in
\Omega(T,P)^{\an}\subset G^{\an}
and that the limit depends only on
x
. Namely, for any
f\in k[\Omega(T,P)]
, we have

|f|(\vartheta(x_{n}))=\max_{\nu\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbf{\Phi}}}|f_{\nu}|(\vartheta_{Z}(\ast))\prod_{(\alpha,i)\in{\mathbf{\Phi}}}e^{\nu(\alpha,i)\langle\alpha,x_{n}\rangle}\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\max_{\nu\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbf{\Phi}}}|f_{\nu}|(\vartheta_{Z}(\ast))\prod_{(\alpha,i)\in{\mathbf{\Phi}}}e^{\nu(\alpha,i)\langle\alpha,x\rangle}.

Step 2:
Now, consider an arbitrary sequence
(y_{n})\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{Q}}(G,k)^{\mathbb{N}}
converging to
x
. Consider
\mathbf{f}\subset\mathcal{B}(G,k)
the facet of
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
containing
x
, that is the only relatively open polysimplex of
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
containing
x
. We may associate to it the parahoric
k^{\circ}
-group scheme
\mathcal{G}^{0}_{\mathbf{f}}
. By
[
Lou21
, Théorème 4.7]
, the
star
of
\mathbf{f}
, that is the set of facets of
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
whose closure contains
\mathbf{f}
, is in one-to-one correspondence with the set of parabolic subgroups of
\mathcal{G}^{0}_{\mathbf{f},\widetilde{k}}
. In particular, because
k
is local, the residue field
\widetilde{k}
is finite and the set of parabolic
\widetilde{k}
-subgroups of
\mathcal{G}^{0}_{\mathbf{f},\widetilde{k}}
is finite and the star of
\mathbf{f}
is therefore finite.

Because the union of the facets in the star of
\mathbf{f}
is a neighbourhood of
x

[
Bro89
, Lemma VI.3A]
, it follows that it contains the
y_{n}
for all sufficiently large
n
. Because the star of
\mathbf{f}
is a finite collection, there must exist a facet
\mathbf{f}^{\prime}
such that
\mathbf{f}\subset\overline{\mathbf{f}^{\prime}}
and a subsequence
(y_{n_{j}})
such that
y_{n_{j}}\in\mathbf{f}^{\prime}
for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
. Moreover, by
[
BT72
, 7.4.9]
, the group
\mathcal{G}^{0}_{\mathbf{f}}(k^{\circ})
acts transitively on the apartments containing
\mathbf{f}
. Therefore, there exists
g\in\mathcal{G}^{0}_{\mathbf{f}}(k^{\circ})
such that
\mathbf{f}^{\prime}\subset g\cdot A(G,T)
. Consequently, we may write
y_{n_{j}}=gx_{n_{j}},
with
x_{n_{j}}\in A_{\mathbb{Q}}(G,T)
for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
. Note that the sequence
(x_{n_{j}})
also converges to
x
. It will suffice to prove that
\lim_{j\to\infty}\vartheta(y_{n_{j}})=\lim_{j\to\infty}\vartheta(x_{n_{j}})
.

Because
\vartheta
is
G(k)
-equivariant, we have
\vartheta(y_{n_{j}})=g\vartheta(x_{n_{j}})g^{-1}
for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
. By continuity of the action, we have

\lim_{j\to\infty}\vartheta(y_{n_{j}})=g\left(\lim_{j\to\infty}\vartheta(x_{n_{j}})\right)g^{-1}.

By the first step, we know that the limit
\lim_{j\to\infty}\vartheta(x_{n_{j}})
is independent of the choice of the sequence of points in
A_{\mathbb{Q}}(G,T)
converging to
x
. Moreover, by Proposition
19
, we know that there exists a sequence
(u_{n})
of virtually special points in
\mathbf{f}\cap\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{Q}}(G,k)
that converges to
x
. Therefore, we have

\lim_{j\to\infty}\vartheta(y_{n_{j}})=g\left(\lim_{j\to\infty}\vartheta(u_{j})\right)g^{-1}=\lim_{j\to\infty}\vartheta(gu_{j})=\lim_{j\to\infty}\vartheta(u_{j}).

The exact same argument applies to any subsequence of
(y_{n})
, which establishes the fact that from any subsequence of
(\vartheta(y_{n}))
, we may extract a subsequence that converges to the common limit of the sequences
(\vartheta(x_{n}))
, for
(x_{n})
an arbitrary sequence of points in
A_{\mathbb{Q}}(G,T)
that converges to
x
. Hence,
(\vartheta(y_{n}))
converges to that same limit.

This proves that, for any sequence of points
(x_{n})
in
\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{Q}}(G,k)
converging to a point
x
in
A(G,T)
, the limit
\lim_{n\to\infty}\vartheta(x_{n})
exists and depends only on
x
. The general case easily follows. Indeed, if
y\in\mathcal{B}(G,k)
, then there exists
g\in G(k)
such that
gy\in A(G,T)
and, for any sequence of virtually special points
(y_{n})
converging to
y
, the sequence
(\vartheta(y_{n}))
converges to a limit depending only on
y
if and only if the same holds for the sequence
(\vartheta(gy_{n}))=(g\vartheta(y_{n})g^{-1})
.
∎

Note that the density of virtually special points and the continuity of
\vartheta
yield the following as a direct corollary of Lemma
24
.

Proposition 27
.

Let
K/k
be a finite Galois extension. We have the commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(G,K)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{K}^{\an}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(G,k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\an}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vartheta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{K/k}^{\an}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{K/k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vartheta}

.

3
Embedding in pseudo-flag varieties

In this section, our goal is to prove that we can recover the family of polyhedral compactifications of
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
constructed in
[
Cha10
, Chap. 1]
– the abstract building-theoretic analog of Satake’s compactifications of symmetric spaces – by mapping
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
in the
(G/P_{G}(\mu))^{\an}
for various
\mu\in X_{*}(G)
.

3.1
Dependence on the pseudo-parabolic subgroup

Let
P
be a pseudo-parabolic subgroup. We check that the composition

\vartheta_{P}:\mathcal{B}(G,k)\overset{\vartheta}{\longrightarrow}G^{\an}\overset{\lambda_{P}}{\longrightarrow}(G/P)^{\an}

depends only on the conjugacy class of
P
, in the following sense.

Proposition 28
.

Let
Q
be a pseudo-parabolic subgroup of
G
conjugate to
P
and
h\in G(k)
such that
Q=hPh^{-1}
. Then, setting the isomorphism

\iota_{Q,P}:\begin{array}[]{ccc}G/P&\to&G/Q\\
gP&\mapsto&gh^{-1}Q\end{array},

induced by the orbit map
g\mapsto gh^{-1}Q
, we have
\vartheta_{Q}=\iota_{Q,P}^{\an}\circ\vartheta_{P}
.

Proof.

Because both sides of the equation are continuous maps on
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
, it is sufficient to prove that they agree on the dense subset of virtually special points. Moreover, Proposition
27
allows us to reduce to proving the equality for special vertices in the case when
G
is pseudo-split over
k
.

Now, assume that
G
is pseudo-split and consider a special vertex
x\in\mathcal{B}(G,k)
. Denote by
\mathcal{G}
the parahoric
k^{\circ}
-group scheme
\mathcal{G}_{x}^{\circ}
associated to
x
. Let
\mu
be a cocharacter of
G
such that
P=P_{G}(\mu)
. Then, there exists a maximal split torus
T\subset G
such that
\mu
factors through
T
. Now, by construction,
\mathcal{G}
contains the split
k^{\circ}
-torus
\mathcal{T}=\Spec(k^{\circ}[X^{*}(T)])
and
\mu
descends to a cocharacter
\mu^{\circ}
of
\mathcal{T}
.
Let
\mathcal{P}
be the subgroup of
\mathcal{G}
defined by
\mathcal{P}=P_{\mathcal{G}}(\mu^{\circ})
. Then, the image
\vartheta_{P}(x)\in(G/P)^{\an}
is the unique preimage of the generic point of
(\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{P})_{\widetilde{k}}
under the reduction map

\rho_{\mathcal{P}}:(\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{P})^{\an}\to(\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{P})_{\widetilde{k}}.

Indeed, the commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\an}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(G/P)^{\an}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}^{\an}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{P})^{\an}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}_{\widetilde{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{P})_{\widetilde{k}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{P}^{\an}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subset}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}^{\an}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\mathcal{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subset}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\lambda_{\widetilde{P}})_{\widetilde{k}}}

and the surjectivity of the map
(\lambda_{\mathcal{P}})_{\widetilde{k}}:\mathcal{G}_{\widetilde{k}}\to(\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{P})_{\widetilde{k}}
imply that
\vartheta(x)
, which by construction is the unique preimage of the generic point of
\mathcal{G}_{\widetilde{k}}
under the reduction map
\rho
, maps to a preimage of the generic point of
(\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{P})_{\widetilde{k}}
in
(\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{P})^{\an}
. To establish the uniqueness of the preimage, recall that, for any non-empty affine open subset
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{P}
, the space
\mathcal{U}^{\an}
contains a unique preimage of the generic point of
(\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{P})_{\widetilde{k}}
by
[
Ber90
, Proposition 2.4.4]
. The fact that, by construction,
(\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{P})^{\an}
is covered by subsets of the form
\mathcal{U}^{\an}
and that any two of these meet along a subset of the same type allows one to conclude.

Next, observe that due to the Iwasawa decomposition
[
Sol18
, Theorem 3.12]
, we may write
h=kp
, with
k\in\mathcal{G}(k^{\circ})
and
p\in P(k)
. Hence, we have
Q=kPk^{-1}=P_{G}(k\cdot\mu)
. Set
\mathcal{Q}=P_{\mathcal{G}}(k\cdot\mu^{\circ})
. Then, we have
\mathcal{Q}=k\mathcal{P}k^{-1}
and the map
\iota_{Q,P}
descends to a map

\iota_{\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{P}}:\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{P}&\to&\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{Q}\\
g\mathcal{P}&\mapsto&gk^{-1}\mathcal{Q}\end{array}.

Therefore, the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(G/P)^{\an}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(G/Q)^{\an}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{P})^{\an}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{Q})^{\an}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{P})_{\widetilde{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{Q})_{\widetilde{k}}\par}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{Q,P}^{\an}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subset}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{P}}^{\an}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\mathcal{P}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\mathcal{Q}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subset}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\iota_{\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{P}})_{\widetilde{k}}}

commutes. Because
(\iota_{\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{P}})_{\widetilde{k}}
maps generic points to generic points, the map
\iota_{Q,P}^{\an}
maps the unique preimage in
(\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{P})^{\an}
of the generic point of
(\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{P})_{\widetilde{k}}
to the unique preimage in
(\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{Q})^{\an}
of the generic point of
(\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{Q})_{\widetilde{k}}
. In other words,

\vartheta_{Q}(x)=\iota_{Q,P}^{\an}(\vartheta_{P}(x)).

∎

Remark 29
.

The map
\iota_{Q,P}
does not depend on the choice of
h
. Indeed, if
h^{\prime}\in G(k)
also satisfies
Q=h^{\prime}Ph^{\prime-1}
, then, by
[
CGP15
, Proposition 3.5.7]
, we have
h^{\prime}h^{-1}\in N_{G}(Q)(k)=Q(k)
, and thus
h^{-1}Q=h^{\prime-1}Q
.

Corollary 30
.

The map
\vartheta_{P}
is
G(k)
-equivariant.

Proof.

Let
g\in G(k)
. We have the following commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G/P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G/g^{-1}Pg}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot g^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{g^{-1}Pg}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{P,g^{-1}Pg}}

,

in which all maps are
G(k)
