-equivariant for the action by left-translation.
Now, recall from Proposition
23
that, for each
x\in\mathcal{B}(G,k)
, we have

\vartheta(gx)=g\vartheta(x)g^{-1}.

Consequently, we have

\vartheta_{P}(gx)=\lambda^{\an}_{P}(g\vartheta(x)g^{-1})=g\lambda^{\an}_{P}(\vartheta(x)g^{-1}).

By the above diagram, we have

\lambda_{P}^{\an}(\vartheta(x)g^{-1})=\iota_{P,gPg^{-1}}^{\an}(\vartheta_{g^{-1}Pg}(x))=\vartheta_{P}(x).

Finally, we find

\vartheta_{P}(gx)=g\vartheta_{P}(x),

which completes the proof.
∎

3.2
Restricting to an apartment: An explicit formula

The following sections will establish that, if
P
is a suitable pseudo-parabolic subgroup of
G
, then
\vartheta_{P}
is an embedding with relatively compact image. The argument hinges on an explicit formula for the restriction of
\vartheta_{P}
to an apartment
A(G,T)
of the building such that
T\subset P
in the case where
G
is pseudo-split. The argument is essentially identical – yet not reducible – to that of Proposition
25
.

Assume that
G
is pseudo-split over
k
and let
A
be an apartment in
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
associated to a maximal split torus
T
. We denote by
\Phi=\Phi(G,T)
the root system of
G
corresponding to
T
. Let
o
be a special vertex in
A
and denote by
\mathcal{G}
the parahoric
k^{\circ}
-model of
G
associated to
o
. For each
\alpha\in\Phi_{\nd}
, we denote by
\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\subset\mathcal{G}
the
k^{\circ}
-model of the root group
U_{\alpha}
associated to
o
.

Let
P=P_{G}(\mu)
be a pseudo-parabolic subgroup containing
T
and
U^{-}=U_{G}(-\mu)
. Arguing as in the proof of Proposition
28
, we may find a
k^{\circ}
-cocharacter
\mu^{\circ}:\mathbb{G}_{m,k^{\circ}}\to\mathcal{G}
such that
\mu^{\circ}\otimes_{k^{\circ}}k=\mu
. Then, let
\mathcal{P}=P_{\mathcal{G}}(\mu^{\circ})
and
\mathcal{U}^{-}=U_{\mathcal{G}}(-\mu^{\circ})
. Let

\Psi=\Phi(U^{-},T)=\{\alpha\in\Phi,\langle\alpha,\mu\rangle<0\}.

Then, we claim that the multiplication map

m:\prod_{\alpha\in\Psi_{\nd}}\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\to\mathcal{U}^{-}

is a
\mathcal{T}
-equivariant isomorphism of
k^{\circ}
-schemes.

For each non-divisible root
\alpha\in\,\Psi_{\nd}
, we have a
\mathcal{T}
-equivariant isomorphism of
k^{\circ}
-schemes:

\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\simeq\mathcal{U}_{2{\alpha}}\times\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}/\mathcal{U}_{2{\alpha}}.

Moreover, each
\mathcal{U}_{2{\alpha}}
(resp.
\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}/\mathcal{U}_{2{\alpha}}
) is an affine space over
k^{\circ}
on which
\mathcal{T}
acts linearly via the character
2{\alpha}
(resp.
{\alpha}
).
For each
\alpha\in\Phi
, set
N_{\alpha}=\dim_{k}(U_{\alpha}/U_{2\alpha})
and fix
\mathcal{T}
-equivariant isomorphisms

u_{\alpha}:\mathbb{A}^{N_{\alpha}}_{k^{\circ}}\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}/\mathcal{U}_{2{\alpha}}\text{ and }u_{2{\alpha}}:\mathbb{A}^{N_{2{\alpha}}}_{k^{\circ}}\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathcal{U}_{2{\alpha}}

for each non-divisible root
{\alpha}
. These yield a
\mathcal{T}
-equivariant isomorphism of
k^{\circ}
-schemes

\prod_{\alpha\in\Psi}\mathbb{A}^{N_{\alpha}}_{k^{\circ}}\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathcal{U}^{-}.

In the following, we identify
k[U^{-}]
to the polynomial algebra
k[(\xi_{\alpha,i})_{(\alpha,i)\in\,\Psi\times\mathbb{N},1\leq i\leq N_{\alpha}}]
using this isomorphism.

Remark 31
.

We call a system of coordinates
(\xi_{\alpha,i})_{(\alpha,i)\in\mathbf{\Psi}}
on
U^{-}
so constructed
adapted to
the point
o
.

Denote by
\Omega(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{P})
the image of the open immersion defined by the multiplication map
[
CGP15
, Proposition 2.1.8 (3)]

m:\mathcal{U}^{-}\times\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{G}.

and let
\Omega(T,P)=\Omega(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{P})\otimes_{k^{\circ}}k
.

Proposition 32
.

We keep all the above notation. Let
x
be a point in
A
. We let
\Psi=\Phi(U_{G}(-\mu),T)
and

\mathbf{\Psi}=\{(\alpha,i)\in\,\Psi\times\mathbb{N},1\leq i\leq N_{\alpha}\}.

1.

The image
\vartheta_{P}(x)
lies in
(U^{-})^{\an}\simeq(\Omega(T,P)/P)^{\an}\subset(G/P)^{\an}
.

2.

For each
f=\sum_{\nu\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbf{\Psi}}}f_{\nu}\xi^{\nu}\in k[U_{G}(-\mu)]=k\left[(\xi_{\alpha,i})_{(\alpha,i)\in{\mathbf{\Psi}}}\right]
, we have

|f(\vartheta_{P}(x))|=\max_{\nu\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbf{\Psi}}}|f_{\nu}|\prod_{(\alpha,i)\in{\mathbf{\Psi}}}e^{\nu(\alpha,i)\langle\alpha,x\rangle}.

Proof.

The arguments are essentially the same as those of Proposition
25
.

The statement will follow from the fact that
\vartheta(x)
lies in
\Omega(T,P)^{\an}
for all
x\in A
. To establish this, we proceed in three steps, first proving it for the origin
o
, then extending to any virtually special point, and finally concluding by density.

1.

First of all, because
\Omega(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{P})
is an open subset of
\mathcal{G}
that meets the special fibre of
\mathcal{G}
, the special fibre
\Omega(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{P})_{\widetilde{k}}
contains the generic point of
\mathcal{G}_{\widetilde{k}}
. Therefore, the analytification
\Omega(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{P})^{\an}
contains
\vartheta(o)
. Now, because the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{P})^{\an}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Omega(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{P})/\mathcal{P})^{\an}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{P})_{\widetilde{k}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Omega(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{P})/\mathcal{P})_{\widetilde{k}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}^{\an}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{\mathcal{P}_{\widetilde{k}}}}

commutes and the bottom arrow is surjective (and therefore dominant), the image
\vartheta_{P}(o)
is the unique Shilov boundary of
(\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{P})^{\an}
.
Identifying
(\Omega(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{P})/\mathcal{P})^{\an}
to
(\mathcal{U}^{-})^{\an}
via
\lambda_{\mathcal{P}}^{\an}
, the point
\vartheta_{P}(o)
may be understood as the only seminorm such that, for each
f\in k[U^{-}]=k[(\xi_{\alpha,i})_{(\alpha,i)\in{\mathbf{\Psi}}}]
, we have

|f(\vartheta_{P}(o))|\leq 1\iff f\in k^{\circ}[(\xi_{\alpha,i})_{(\alpha,i)\in{\mathbf{\Psi}}}]

and

|f(\vartheta_{P}(o))|<1\iff f\text{ maps to zero in }\widetilde{k}[(\xi_{\alpha,i})_{(\alpha,i)\in\mathbf{\Psi}}].

In other words, if
f=\sum_{\nu\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbf{\Psi}}}f_{\nu}\xi^{\nu}\in k[(\xi_{\alpha,i})]
, we have

|f(\vartheta_{P}(o))|=\max_{\nu\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbf{\Psi}}}|f_{\nu}|.

2.

Next, consider a virtually special point
x\in A_{\mathbb{Q}}(T,k)
. By Corollary
21
, we have at our disposal a finite Galois extension
K/k
and a
t\in T(K)
such that
x_{K}=to_{K}
. Then, we have
\vartheta(x_{K})=t\vartheta(o_{K})t^{-1}
and, because
\Omega(T,P)
is normalised by
T
, we have
\vartheta(x_{K})\in\Omega(T,P)_{K}^{\an}
and, consequently,
\vartheta(x)=\pr_{K/k}^{\an}(\vartheta(x_{K}))\in\Omega(T,P)^{\an}
, and finally
\vartheta_{P}(x)\in(\Omega(T,P)/P)^{\an}
. Moreover, for
f\in k[U^{-}]
, we have

|f|(\vartheta_{P}(x))=|f|(\vartheta_{P}(x_{K}))=|\tau^{*}(f)|(\vartheta_{P}(o_{K})),

where
\tau
is the only map that makes the following diagram commute
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{{\alpha}\in\Psi}\mathbb{A}^{N_{\alpha}}_{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{{\alpha}\in\Psi}\mathbb{A}^{N_{\alpha}}_{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(U^{-})_{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(U^{-})_{K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{int}(t_{K})}

.

In other words,
\tau^{*}
is the only
k
-automorphism of
k[(\xi_{\alpha,i})_{(\alpha,i)\in{\mathbf{\Psi}}}]
that maps
\xi_{\alpha,i}
to
\alpha(t)\xi_{\alpha,i}
for each
\alpha\in\Psi
and
i\in[\![1,N_{\alpha}]\!]
. We deduce that if
f=\sum_{\nu\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbf{\Psi}}}f_{\nu}\xi^{\nu}\in k[(\xi_{\alpha,i})_{(\alpha,i)\in{\mathbf{\Psi}}}]
, we have

\tau^{*}(f)=\sum_{\nu\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbf{\Psi}}}\left(f_{\nu}\prod_{(\alpha,i)\in{\mathbf{\Psi}}}\alpha(t)^{\nu(\alpha,i)}\right)\xi^{\nu}

and thus

|f|(\vartheta_{P}(x_{K}))=\max_{\nu\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbf{\Psi}}}|f_{\nu}|\prod_{(\alpha,i)\in{\mathbf{\Psi}}}|\alpha(t)|^{\nu(\alpha,i)}=\max_{\nu\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbf{\Psi}}}|f_{\nu}|\prod_{(\alpha,i)\in{\mathbf{\Psi}}}e^{\nu(\alpha,i)\langle\alpha,x\rangle}

using point 1.

3.

If
x
is an arbitrary point in
A
, then there exists a sequence
(x_{n})
of points in
A_{\mathbb{Q}}(T,k)
that converges to
x
. Then, it is clear from the continuity in
x
of the formula in point 2. that the sequence
(\vartheta_{P}(x_{n}))
converges to a seminorm on
k[U^{-}]
, that is that
\vartheta_{P}(x)\in\Omega(T,P)^{\an}
and that the formula for
\vartheta_{P}(x)
is as announced.

∎

3.3
Injectivity of the map

This section establishes the injectivity of the map
\vartheta_{P}
under the condition that
P
is
of non-degenerate type
, a property we define below.

Definition 33
.

Let
G
be a quasi-reductive group over a field
k
and
P
be a pseudo-parabolic subgroup of
G
. We say that
P
is
of non-degenerate type
if it does not contain any minimal nontrivial
k
-isotropic perfect smooth connected normal subgroup of
G
.

The property of being of non-degenerate type admits the following Lie-theoretic characterisations.

Proposition 34
.

Let
G
be a quasi-reductive group over a field
k
and
P
be a pseudo-parabolic subgroup of
G
. Fix a
k
-split maximal torus
S\subset P
and
P_{0}
a minimal pseudo-parabolic subgroup such that
S\subset P_{0}\subset P
. Denote by
P=L_{P}\ltimes U
the Levi decomposition of
P
corresponding to
S
. Let
\Phi=\Phi(G,S)
be the root system associated to
S
and
\Delta\subset\Phi
be the system of simple roots corresponding to
P_{0}
.
The following are equivalent.

1.

P
is of non-degenerate type.

2.

The type
I=\Phi(L_{P},S)\cap\Delta
of
P
contains no connected components of the Dynkin diagram
\Delta
.

3.

The subset
-\Psi=\Phi(U,S)
spans
V^{*}(G,S)=\Span_{\mathbb{R}}(\Phi)
as a vector space.

Proof.

It follows from
[
CGP15
, Proposition C.2.32]
that the minimal nontrivial
k
-isotropic perfect smooth connected normal subgroup of
G
are the
N_{\Phi_{i}}=\langle U_{\alpha},\alpha\in\Phi_{i}\rangle
for
\Phi_{i}
ranging in the set of irreducible components of the root system
\Phi
. Because
P
contains the root group
U_{\alpha}
if and only if
\alpha\in\Phi(P,S)
, we have the equivalences

N_{\Phi_{i}}\subset P\iff\Phi_{i}\subset\Phi(P,S)\iff\Phi_{i}\subset\Phi(L_{P},S)=\Phi(P,S)\cap-\Phi(P,S).

The equivalence
1\iff 2
immediately follows as the connected components of the Dynkin diagram
\Delta
are precisely the
\Delta\cap\Phi_{i}
for
\Phi_{i}
ranging among the irreducible components of
\Phi
.

We now prove the equivalence
2\iff 3
. Assume that
I
contains a connected component
\Delta_{i}=\Delta\cap\Phi_{i}
of the Dynkin diagram
\Delta
. Then, we have
\Phi_{i}\subset\Phi(L_{P},S)
and therefore
\Phi(U,S)=\Phi(P,S)\setminus\Phi(L_{P},S)\subset\bigsqcup_{j\neq i}\Phi_{j}
and therefore
\Span_{\mathbb{R}}(\Phi(U,S))
is contained in
\Span_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\bigsqcup_{j\neq i}\Phi_{j}\right)
, which is a proper subspace of
\Span_{\mathbb{R}}(\Phi)
because the
\Phi_{i}
are pairwise orthogonal. Conversely, if
I
contains no connected component of
\Delta
, let
\Phi_{i}
be an irreducible component of
\Phi
. By assumption, there exists an
\alpha\in\Phi_{i}
such that
\alpha\in\Delta\setminus I
. Because
P_{0}\subset P
, that is
\Delta\subset\Phi(P,S)
, we must have
\alpha\in\Phi(U,S)
. Then, for each
\beta\in I\cap\Phi_{i}
, the root
s_{\alpha}(\beta)=\beta-\underbrace{\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle}_{<0}\alpha
is a sum of elements of
\Delta
and is not contained in the span of
I
, hence it lies in
\Phi(U,S)
. Therefore, the subspace
\Span_{\mathbb{R}}(\Phi(U,S))
contains
I\cap\Phi_{i}
. Because it contains
(\Delta\setminus I)\cap\Phi_{i}
, it actually contains
\Delta\cap\Phi_{i}
and therefore contains
\Phi_{i}
.

∎

Proposition 35
.

The map
\vartheta_{P}
is injective if and only if
P
is of non-degenerate type

Proof.

Observe that, because
\vartheta_{P}
is
G(k)
-equivariant and because any two points lie in an apartment, the map
\vartheta_{P}
is injective if and only if there exists an apartment
A
such that
\vartheta_{P|A}
is injective.

Let
S
be a maximal
k
-split torus contained in
P
. Let
o
be a special point of
A(G,k)
and
K
be a finite Galois extension such that
G_{K}
is pseudo-split and
o_{K}
is special. Let
T
be a maximal
K
-split torus that contains
S_{K}
such that
p_{K/k}(A(G,S))\subset A(G_{K},T)
. By Propositions
27
and
32
, we have the commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(G_{K},T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(U^{-})_{K}^{\an}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(G,S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(U^{-})^{\an}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vartheta_{P_{K}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{K/k}^{\an}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{K/k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vartheta_{P}}

,

where
U^{-}
is the
k
-split unipotent radical of the opposite pseudo-parabolic subgroup to
P
with respect to
S
. With the notations of Section
3.2
associated to the point
o_{K}
, we have, for each
f=\sum_{\nu\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbf{\Psi}}}f_{\nu}\xi^{\nu}\in K[U^{-}]=K[(\xi_{\alpha,i})_{(\alpha,i)\in{\mathbf{\Psi}}}]
and
x\in A(G_{K},T)
:

|f(\vartheta_{P_{K}}(x))|=\max_{\nu\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbf{\Psi}}}|f_{\nu}|\prod_{(\alpha,i)\in{\mathbf{\Psi}}}e^{\nu(\alpha,i)\langle\alpha,x-o_{K}\rangle}.

(3)

Note also that, for each character
\chi\in X^{*}(T)
and each cocharacter
\lambda\in X_{*}(S)
, we have the following compatibility property between duality pairings

\langle\chi,\lambda_{K}\rangle=\langle\chi_{|S},\lambda\rangle.

Now, let
a\in\Phi(U^{-},S)
be a relative weight of the Lie algebra of
U^{-}
, and
\phi\in\Lie(U^{-})^{*}\subset k[U^{-}]
be a nonzero weight vector of weight
-a
. Then, we may write

\phi=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\alpha,i)\in{\mathbf{\Psi}}\\
\alpha_{|S}=a\end{subarray}}\phi_{\alpha,i}\xi_{\alpha,i},

with
\phi_{\alpha,i}\in K
and not all
\phi_{\alpha,i}
equal to zero. Hence, we have, for all
x\in A(G,S)
:

|\phi(\vartheta_{P}(x))|=|\phi_{K}(\vartheta_{P_{K}}(x_{K}))|=\left(\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\alpha,i)\in{\mathbf{\Psi}}\\
\alpha_{|S}=a\end{subarray}}|\phi_{\alpha,i}|\right)e^{\langle a,x-o\rangle}.

Therefore, if
x,y\in A(G,S)
satisfy
\vartheta_{P}(x)=\vartheta_{P}(y)
, then
\langle a,x-o\rangle=\langle a,y-o\rangle
for each
a\in\Phi(U^{-},S)
. Conversely, if
x
and
y
in
A(G,S)
satisfy
\langle a,x-y\rangle=0
for each
a\in\Phi(U^{-},S)
, then the observation following Equation (
3
) that
\vartheta_{P_{K}}(x)=\vartheta_{P_{K}}(y)
, and therefore
\vartheta_{P}(x)=\vartheta_{P}(y)
.
In other words, for
x
and
y
in
A(G,S)
, we have
\vartheta_{P}(x)=\vartheta_{P}(y)
if and only if
x-y
lies in the annihilator

\Phi(U^{-},S)^{0}=\{v\in V(G,S),\forall a\in\Phi(U^{-},S),\langle a,v\rangle=0\}.

Hence, the map
\vartheta_{P}
is injective if and only if the subset
\Phi(U^{-},S)
spans the dual space
V^{*}(G,S)
, that is if and only if
P
is of non-degenerate type by Proposition
34
.
∎

3.4
Relative compactness

In this section, we establish that, if
P
is a pseudo-parabolic subgroup of
G
, then the image of the map
\vartheta_{P}
is relatively compact. As usual, it will suffice to consider the case when
G
is pseudo-split. The main insight is that the Cartan decomposition
[
Sol18
, Theorem 3.13 (d)]
allows one to restrict to proving that sequences taking values in a suitably chosen cone admit a convergent subsequence, which will follow from the previous section.

Proposition 36
.

The map
\vartheta_{P}
has relatively compact image. In particular, if
P
is of non-degenerate type, then
\vartheta_{P}
factors through a
G(k)
-equivariant compactification of
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
.

Proof.

First of all, thanks to Proposition
27
, we have the following commutative diagram of continuous maps
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(G,K)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(G/P)_{K}^{\an}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(G,k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(G/P)^{\an}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vartheta_{P_{K}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{K/k}^{\an}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{K/k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vartheta_{P}}

for any finite Galois extension
K/k
. It follows that, if
\vartheta_{P_{K}}
has relatively compact image, then so does
\vartheta_{P}
.

It is therefore sufficient to prove our claim in the case where
G
is pseudo-split over
k
, which we will assume in what follows. Let
T
be a maximal split torus contained in
P
and
\mu\in X_{*}(T)
be a cocharacter such that
P=P_{G}(\mu)
. Set
U^{-}=U_{G}(-\mu)
. Fix a special vertex
o
in
A(G,T)
, set
\Psi=\Phi(U^{-},T)
, and consider the cone

C(P)=\{x\in A(G,T)\ |\ \forall\alpha\in\Psi,\langle\alpha,x-o\rangle\leq 0\}.

Observe that we have

A(G,T)=W(G,T)(k)\cdot C(P),

where the action of
W(G,T)(k)=\dfrac{N_{G}(T)(k)}{Z_{G}(T)(k)}
on
A(G,T)
is given by the identification of
A(G,T)
with
V(G,T)
setting
o
as origin.
Moreover, because
o
is special, the projection

N_{G}(T)(k)_{o}\to W(G,T)(k)

is onto, hence we have

A(G,T)=N_{G}(T)(k)_{o}\cdot C(P).

Moreover, by
[
Sol18
, Theorem 3.11]
, we have

\mathcal{B}(G,k)=G(k)_{o}\cdot A(G,T),

and therefore

\mathcal{B}(G,k)=G(k)_{o}\cdot C(P).

Because
\vartheta_{P}
is
G(k)
-equivariant and continuous and because
G(k)_{o}
acts through its compact quotient
G(k)_{o}/Z(G)(k)
, proving that
\vartheta_{P}
has relatively compact image reduces to proving that its restriction to
C(P)
has relatively compact image. Now, let
(x_{n})
be a sequence in
C(P)
. Identify
A(G,T)
to
V(G,T)
using
o
as origin and assume the notations of Section
3.2
. Then, by Proposition
32
, we have
\vartheta_{P}(x_{n})\in(U^{-})^{\an}
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
and, for each
f=\sum_{\nu\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbf{\Psi}}}f_{\nu}\xi^{\nu}\in k[U^{-}]=k[(\xi_{\alpha,i})_{(\alpha,i)\in{\mathbf{\Psi}}}]
, we have

|f|(\vartheta_{P}(x_{n}))=\max_{\nu\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbf{\Psi}}}|f_{\nu}|\prod_{(\alpha,i)\in{\mathbf{\Psi}}}e^{\nu(\alpha,i)\langle\alpha,x_{n}\rangle}.

For each
\alpha\in\Psi
, the sequence
(e^{\langle\alpha,x_{n}\rangle})
is bounded. Consequently, there exists a subsequence
(x_{n_{j}})
such that
(e^{\langle\alpha,x_{n_{j}}\rangle})
converges for each
\alpha\in\Psi
. Then, for each
f\in k[U^{-}]
, the sequence
(|f|(\vartheta_{P}(x_{n_{j}})))
converges and thus the subsequence
(\vartheta_{P}(x_{n_{j}}))
converges in
(U^{-})^{\an}
.
∎

The proof actually yields the following more precise statement.

Proposition 37
.

Let
S
be a maximal split torus in
P
and
o
a special vertex in
A(G,S)
. Consider the cone

C(P)=\{x\in A(G,S),\forall\alpha\in\Phi(R_{u,k}(P),S),\langle\alpha,x-o\rangle\geq 0\}.

Then, the image
\vartheta_{P}(C(P))
is relatively compact in the open subspace
(\Omega(S,P)/P)^{\an}\subset(G/P)^{\an}
.

3.5
Openness of the corestriction

In this section, we check that the corestriction

\vartheta_{P}:\mathcal{B}(G,k)\to\overline{\vartheta_{P}(\mathcal{B}(G,k))}

is an open map. In particular, if
P
is of non-degenerate type, we prove that
\vartheta_{P}
is an embedding.

Proposition 38
.

The corestricted map
\vartheta_{P}:\mathcal{B}(G,k)\to\overline{\vartheta_{P}(\mathcal{B}(G,k))}
is open. In particular, whenever
P
is of non-degenerate type, the map
\vartheta_{P}
is an embedding.

The proof goes in three steps. First of all, we prove that the restriction of
\vartheta_{P}
to an apartment
A(G,S)
(and thus any apartment) induces an open map to the closure of its image.
Secondly, we prove that the action map

G(k)\times\overline{\vartheta_{P}(A(G,S))}\longrightarrow\overline{\vartheta_{P}(\mathcal{B}(G,k))}

is a topological quotient map. Lastly, we deduce that the map
\vartheta_{P}:\mathcal{B}(G,k)\to\overline{\vartheta_{P}(\mathcal{B}(G,k))}
is open.

Lemma 39
.

Let
S
be a maximal split torus of
G
. The image
\vartheta_{P}(A(G,S))
is open in its closure
\overline{\vartheta_{P}(A(G,S))}
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we may assume
S
to be contained in
P
.

Let
o
be a special vertex in
A(G,S)
. Let
T
be a maximal torus of
G
and
K/k
a finite Galois extension such that

1.

The torus
T_{K}
is split.

2.

The apartment
A(G_{K},T_{K})
contains
p_{K/k}(A(G,S))
.

3.

The point
o_{K}\in A(G_{K},T_{K})
is a special vertex.

Then, as in the proof of Proposition
34
, we have the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(G_{K},T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(U^{-})_{K}^{\an}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(G,S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(U^{-})^{\an}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vartheta_{P_{K}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{K/k}^{\an}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{K/k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vartheta_{P}}

,

where
U^{-}
is the
k
-split unipotent radical of the pseudo-parabolic subgroup of
G
opposite to
P
with respect to
S
.
We may rewrite the explicit formula in Proposition
32
as the statement that, for each
x\in\vartheta_{P}(A(G,S))
and
f\in K[U^{-}]=K[(\xi_{\alpha,i})_{(\alpha,i)\in{\mathbf{\Psi}}}]
, we have

|f(x)|=\max_{\nu\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbf{\Psi}}}|f_{\nu}|\prod_{(\alpha,i)\in{\mathbf{\Psi}}}|\xi_{\alpha,i}(x)|^{\nu(\alpha,i)},

where, for each
\alpha\in\Phi(U^{-},S)
, the family
(\xi_{\alpha,i})
is a choice of coordinates on
U_{\alpha}
given by
o_{K}
through the procedure described in the introduction to Section
3.2
.
It follows from the proof of Proposition
36
that this formula extends to all points
x\in\overline{\vartheta_{P}(A(G,S))}\cap(U^{-})^{\an}
and that a point
x\in\overline{\vartheta_{P}(A(G,S))}\cap(U^{-})^{\an}
lies in
\vartheta_{P}(A(G,S))
if and only if
|\xi_{\alpha,i}(x)|>0
for all
(\alpha,i)\in{\mathbf{\Psi}}
.

It follows that the image
\vartheta_{P}(A(G,S))
is open in
\overline{\vartheta_{P}(A(G,S))}\cap(\Omega(S,P)/P)^{\an}
.
Note that the Bruhat decomposition
[
CGP15
, Theorem C.2.8]
implies that
G/P=\bigcup_{w\in W}\dot{w}\Omega(S,P)/P
, where, for each
w\in W
, we choose an arbitrary lift
\dot{w}
of
w
in
N_{G}(S)(k)
, see
[
Jan87
, II.1.9.7]
.
By
N_{G}(S)(k)
-equivariance of
\vartheta_{P}
, we deduce that
\vartheta_{P}(A(G,S))
is open in
\overline{\vartheta_{P}(A(G,S))}\cap(\dot{w}\Omega(S,P)/P)^{\an}
for each
w\in W
, and therefore open in
\overline{\vartheta_{P}(A(G,S))}
.

∎

We now conclude our first step.

Lemma 40
.

Let
S
be a maximal split torus of
G
. The map

\vartheta_{P}:A(G,S)\to\overline{\vartheta_{P}(A(G,S))}

is open.

Proof.

As in the previous lemma, we may assume that
S
is contained in
P
. Let
U^{-}
be the
k
-split unipotent radical of the pseudo-parabolic subgroup opposite to
P
with respect to
S
.
Let
o
be a special vertex in
A(G,S)
. Recall from Proposition
35
that
\vartheta_{P}
factors through the quotient map

A(G,S)\to A(G,S)/\langle\Phi(U^{-},S)\rangle^{0},

a surjective affine map, hence open. We may therefore assume that
P
is of non-degenerate type, ie. that we have the implication

(\forall a\in\Phi(U^{-},S),\langle a,x\rangle=0)\implies x=0

for each
x\in V(G,S)
.
Under this assumption, the open half-spaces

H_{a,r}^{+}=\{x\in A(G,S),\langle a,x-o\rangle>r\}\text{ and }H_{a,r}^{-}=\{x\in A(G,S),\langle a,x-o\rangle<r\}

for
a\in\Phi(U^{-},S)
form a subbase of open sets for the topology of
A(G,S)
. Because
\vartheta_{P}
is injective, we need only check that these half-spaces have an open image.
Consider a system of coordinates
(\xi_{\alpha,i})_{(\alpha,i)\in\mathbf{\Psi}}
on
U^{-}_{K}
as in Section
3.2
. Let
\phi=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\alpha,i)\in{\mathbf{\Psi}}\\
\alpha_{|S}=a\end{subarray}}\phi_{\alpha,i}\xi_{\alpha,i}\in k[U^{-}]
be a nonzero weight vector of weight
-a
in
\Lie(U^{-})^{*}
. Then, it follows from Proposition
32
that, for each
r\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\vartheta_{P}(H_{a,r}^{+})=\vartheta_{P}(A(G,S))\cap\{x\in(U^{-})^{\an},|\phi(x)|>Ce^{r}\}

and

\vartheta_{P}(H_{a,r}^{-})=\vartheta_{P}(A(G,S))\cap\{x\in(U^{-})^{\an},|\phi(x)|<Ce^{r}\},

where
C=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\alpha,i)\in{\mathbf{\Psi}}\\
\alpha_{|S}=a\end{subarray}}|\phi_{\alpha,i}|
. Consequently, for each
r\in\mathbb{R}
, the images
\vartheta_{P}(H_{a,r}^{+})
and
\vartheta_{P}(H_{a,r}^{-})
are open in
\vartheta_{P}(A(G,S))
, and thus open in
\overline{\vartheta_{P}(A(G,S))}
by Lemma
39
.
∎

Now, let
o
be a special vertex in
A(G,S)
. By
[
Sol18
, Theorem 3.12]
, we have a coarse Cartan decomposition

\mathcal{B}(G,k)=G(k)_{o}\cdot A(G,S),

and therefore

\overline{\vartheta_{P}(\mathcal{B}(G,k))}=G(k)_{o}\cdot\overline{\vartheta_{P}(A(G,S))}.

Because
G(k)_{o}\times\overline{\vartheta_{P}(A(G,S))}
and
\overline{\vartheta_{P}(\mathcal{B}(G,k))}
are compact Hausdorff by Proposition
36
, the surjective action map

\pi_{P}:G(k)_{o}\times\overline{\vartheta_{P}(A(G,S))}\to\overline{\vartheta_{P}(\mathcal{B}(G,k))}

is in fact a quotient map.

In order to conclude that
\vartheta_{P}
is an open map, we need to make one last observation.

Lemma 41
.

Let
S
be a maximal split torus of
G
and
x\in\overline{\vartheta_{P}(A(G,S))}
. If, for some
g\in G(k)
, we have
gx\in\vartheta_{P}(\mathcal{B}(G,k))
, then
x\in\vartheta_{P}(A(G,S))
.

Proof.

The proof is completely analogous to that of
[
RTW10
, Lemma 3.33]
. The key point is the fact that, using Proposition
32
, we may characterise the points of
\vartheta_{P}(A(G,S))
as the points
x
of
\overline{\vartheta_{P}(A(G,S))}
that induce norms on their local ring
\mathcal{O}_{(G/P)^{\an},x}
, a property that is clearly preserved by the action of
G(k)
.

First of all, reasoning as in the proof of Lemma
39
, we observe that there exists a
\dot{w}\in N_{G}(S)(k)
such that
\dot{w}x\in(\Omega(S,P)/P)^{\an}
. Moreover, the lemma holds for
x
if and only if it does for
\dot{w}x
. We may therefore assume that
x
lies in
\overline{\vartheta_{P}(A(G,S))}\cap(\Omega(S,P)/P)^{\an}
. Let
o
be a special vertex in
A(G,S)
. Let
T
be a maximal torus of
G
and
K/k
a finite Galois extension such that

1.

The torus
T_{K}
is split.

2.

The apartment
A(G_{K},T_{K})
contains
p_{K/k}(A(G,S))
.

3.

The point
o_{K}\in A(G_{K},T_{K})
is a special vertex.

Denote by
U^{-}
the
k
-split unipotent radical of the opposite pseudo-parabolic to
P
with respect to
S
. Identify
A(G,S)
with
V(G,S)
(resp.
A(G_{K},T_{K})
with
V(G_{K},T_{K})
) setting
o
(resp.
o_{K}
) as origin. Under this identification, the map
p_{K/k}:A(G,S)\to A(G_{K},T_{K})
is the map induced by base change on cocharacters.
Consider the sets
\Psi=\Phi(U^{-},S)
and
\Psi_{K}=\Phi(U-,T_{K})
. Let
(\xi_{\alpha,i})_{(\alpha,i)\in\mathbf{\Psi}_{K}}
be a system of coordinates on
U^{-}_{K}
that is adapted to
o_{K}
(Remark
31
).
Let
(x_{n})
be a sequence in
A(G,S)
such that
x=\lim_{n\to\infty}\vartheta_{P}(x_{n})
. The sequence
(\vartheta_{P_{K}}((x_{n})_{K}))
converges to a point
x_{K}
in
\overline{\vartheta_{P_{K}}(A(G_{K},T_{K}))}\cap(\Omega(T,P)/P)_{K}^{\an}
by Proposition
32
.
Moreover, we have the equivalences

\begin{array}[]{ccl}x_{K}\in\vartheta_{P_{K}}(A(G_{K},T_{K}))&\iff&\forall(\alpha,i)\in\mathbf{\Psi}_{K},|\xi_{\alpha,i}(x_{K})|>0\\
&\iff&x_{K}\text{ defines a norm on }\mathcal{O}_{(G/P)_{K}^{\an},x_{K}}\end{array}.

To conclude, first observe that, by Proposition
32
, for each weight
\alpha\in\Psi_{K}
and
a=\alpha_{|S}\in\Psi
its restriction to
S
, we have

|\xi_{\alpha,i}(x_{K})|=\lim_{n\to\infty}e^{\langle a,x_{n}\rangle}.

Therefore, we have the equivalence

x_{K}\in\vartheta_{P_{K}}(A(G_{K},T_{K}))\iff x\in\vartheta_{P}(A(G,S)),

and in particular the implication

x\in\vartheta_{P}(A(G,S))\implies x\text{ defines a norm on }\mathcal{O}_{(G/P)^{\an},x},

as the seminorm defined by
x
on
\mathcal{O}_{(G/P)^{\an},x}
is the restriction of the one defined by
x_{K}
on
\mathcal{O}_{(G/P)_{K}^{\an},x_{K}}
, hence a norm.
On the other hand, if
a\in\Psi
and
\phi\in\Lie(U^{-})^{*}
is a nonzero weight vector of weight
-a
, then as observed in the proof of Lemma
40
, there exists
C>0
such that

|\phi(x)|=|\phi_{K}(x_{K})|=\lim_{n\to\infty}Ce^{\langle a,x_{n}\rangle}.

It follows that, if
x
defines a norm on
\mathcal{O}_{(G/P)^{\an},x}
, then by the above computation, we have

\forall(\alpha,i)\in\mathbf{\Psi}_{K},|\xi_{\alpha,i}(x_{K})|>0,

and therefore
x\in\vartheta_{P}(A(G,S))
, by the above observations.

∎

We now carry out the proof of Proposition
38
.

Proof of Proposition
38
.

Let
U
be an open subset of
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
. Consider the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(k)_{o}\times A(G,S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(k)_{o}\times\overline{\vartheta_{P}(A(G,S))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(G,k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\vartheta_{P}(\mathcal{B}(G,k))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id\times\vartheta_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vartheta_{P}}

.

Then, the subset
V=(\id\times\vartheta_{P})(\pi^{-1}(U))
is open in
G(k)_{o}\times\overline{\vartheta_{P}(A(G,S))}
by Lemma
40
. As we established above, the rightmost map
\pi_{P}
is a quotient map. Therefore, in order to prove that
\vartheta_{P}(U)=\pi_{P}(V)
is open in
\overline{\vartheta_{P}(\mathcal{B}(G,k)}
, it is sufficient to check that
V
is
\pi_{P}
-saturated, which is a direct consequence of Lemma
41
.
∎

4
Description of the boundary

In this section, we establish that the family of compactifications of
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
constructed above corresponds to the one constructed in
[
Cha10
]
and specialises to the one constructed in
[
RTW10
]
in the reductive case. More precisely, we will prove that, if
P
is a pseudo-parabolic subgroup of
G
of non-degenerate type and
S
a maximal split torus in
P
, then the compactification

\overline{\vartheta_{P}(\mathcal{B}(G,k))}\subset(G/P)^{\an}
is
G(k)
-equivariantly homeomorphic to the compactification
\overline{\mathcal{B}(G,k)}
defined in
[
Cha10
, Section 5]
for a suitable choice of fan.
We deduce that the compactifications are stratified and interpret the strata in terms of group-theoretic data.

4.1
Polyhedral compactifications of affine buildings

In this section, we introduce terminology and results from
[
Cha10
]
that will be in constant use in what follows.

The article
[
Cha10
]
associates to each affine building
\mathcal{I}
, modeled on an affine Coxeter complex
\Sigma
, and each fan
\mathcal{F}
in the direction
\vec{\Sigma}
of
\Sigma
subject to some compatibility relations
[
Cha10
, 2.3]
a canonical compactification
\overline{I}^{\mathcal{F}}
of
\mathcal{I}
.
We give an overview of the construction, first compactifying each apartment and then glueing the compactifications of the various apartments together to get a compactification of the building.

Fans and compactifications of affine spaces

We first describe the compactifications of apartments in
[
Cha10
, Section 3]
.

The setting is that of a Euclidean space
V
whose dual is endowed with a root system
\Phi\subset V^{*}
. Denote by
W
the Weyl group of
\Phi
.
Recall that a cone in
V
is said to be
polyhedral
if it can be defined by a finite number of linear inequalities. We call a cone
relatively open
if it is open in its linear span. A polyhedral cone is relatively open if and only if it can be defined by a finite number of linear equations and strict linear inequalities.
Then, a fan in the sense of
[
Cha10
]
is a family
\mathcal{F}
of relatively open polyhedral cones of
V
satisfying the following conditions:

1.

The family
\mathcal{F}
is a finite partition of
V
.

2.

\{0\}\in\mathcal{F}
.

3.

For each
f\in\mathcal{F}
, the boundary
\partial f=\overline{f}\setminus f
is a union of cones of
\mathcal{F}
, called the
proper faces
of
f
(we also consider
g
to be a face of itself).

4.

For all cones
f
and
g
in
\mathcal{F}
, if
f
is a face of
g
, then
\overline{f}=\Span_{\mathbb{R}}(f)\cap\overline{g}
.

Example 42
.

The root system
\Phi\subset V^{*}
determines a hyperplane arrangement in
V
. The facets, in the sense of
[
Bou81
, Ch. V, §1.3]
, then form a fan
\mathcal{F}
in
V
if the root system
\Phi
is essential. In the following, we call this fan the Weyl fan. Its facets will be called Weyl facets, and its open facets called Weyl chambers.

Remark 43
.

The above definition differs slightly from the one coming from toric geometry adopted in e.g.
[
Ful93
, 1.1]
or
[
RTW10
, Appendix B]
, which we shall call a
closed fan
, namely a finite set
\mathcal{F}
of closed strictly convex polyhedral cones (ie. containing no lines) such that any two cones in
\mathcal{F}
meet along a face, and such that any face of a cone in
\mathcal{F}
belongs to
\mathcal{F}
. In that context, a face of a closed polyhedral convex cone
f
is defined as the intersection of
f
with a supporting hyperplane.
The operations of taking closures and relative interiors allow one to pass from fans on the one hand to closed fans covering
V
and vice versa. These operations also establish a correspondence between both notions of faces, as we check below.

Lemma 44
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a fan in
V
. For each cone
g
in
\mathcal{F}
, the closure
\overline{g}
is strictly convex.

Proof.

Let
g\in\mathcal{F}
be a cone of minimal dimension such that there exists
x\in V\setminus\{0\}
such that
x\in\overline{g}
and
-x\in\overline{g}
.
Note that
x
is in
g
. Indeed, otherwise, there exists a proper face
f
of
g
such that
x\in f
. Then, by Condition 4, we have
-x\in\overline{f}
, which contradicts the minimality of
g
.
Now, since
g
is relatively open and
x\in g
, it follows from
[
Roc72
, Theorem 6.1]
that
0=\frac{1}{2}(x+(-x))\in g
, which contradicts Condition 2.

∎

Lemma 45
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a fan in
V
and
g
a cone in
\mathcal{F}
. Let
f
be a relatively open polyhedral cone in
V
. The following are equivalent:

1.

The cone
f
is a face of
g
.

2.

There exists a linear form
\alpha\in V^{*}
such that
\alpha(x)\geq 0
for each
x\in g
and such that
\overline{f}=(\ker\alpha)\cap\overline{g}
.

Proof.

(Step 1:
(i)\implies(ii)
)
Assume that
f
is a face of
g
. Let us first assume that
g
is open. Then because
\overline{g}
is strictly convex, the dual cone

g^{\vee}=\left\{\alpha\in V^{*},\forall x\in g,\alpha(x)\geq 0\right\}

has non-empty interior by
[
Roc72
, Corollary 14.6.1]
. Because the annihilator

f^{0}=\{\alpha\in V^{*},f\subset\ker\alpha\}

meets the interior of
g^{\vee}
(that is, because
f
lies in a supporting hyperplane of
g
, see
[
Roc72
, Theorem 11.6]
), the intersection
f^{0}\cap g^{\vee}
has non-empty relative interior. Let
\alpha\in f^{0}\cap g^{\vee}
be a relative interior point. Let
x\in\overline{g}\setminus\Span_{\mathbb{R}}(f)
. Because
\alpha
is in the relative interior of
f^{0}\cap g^{\vee}
, it lies in the relative interior of the half-space

f^{0}\cap\{x\}^{\vee}=\{\alpha\in f^{0},\alpha(x)\geq 0\}

by
[
Roc72
, Corollary 6.5.2]
, that is the (relatively) open half-space
\{\alpha\in f^{0},\alpha(x)>0\},
hence
\alpha(x)>0
. Finally, we have

(\ker\alpha)\cap\overline{g}=\Span_{\mathbb{R}}(f)\cap\overline{g}=\overline{f}.

In the general case, let
H_{0}
be a supporting hyperplane of
g
in
\Span_{\mathbb{R}}(g)
such that
H_{0}\cap\overline{g}=\overline{f}
. Then, the subspace
H=H_{0}\oplus\Span_{\mathbb{R}}(g)^{\perp}
is a supporting hyperplane of
g
in
V
such that
H\cap\overline{g}=\overline{f}
.

(Step 2:
(ii)\implies(i)
)
Let
\alpha\in V^{*}
be a linear form such that
\alpha(x)\geq 0
for each
x\in g
and such that
\overline{f}=(\ker\alpha)\cap\overline{g}
. Because each face of
g
is relatively open, the form
\alpha
is either identically zero or only assumes positive values on it. Consequently, the intersection
(\ker\alpha)\cap\overline{g}
is a union of faces of
g
, say

(\ker\alpha)\cap\overline{g}=f_{1}\cup\dots\cup f_{r}.

Because the intersection is closed, we in fact have

(\ker\alpha)\cap\overline{g}=\overline{f}_{1}\cup\dots\cup\overline{f}_{r}.

One of the
\overline{f}_{i}
must then have non-empty interior in
\Span_{\mathbb{R}}((\ker\alpha)\cap\overline{g})
, from which we deduce that

\overline{f}_{i}=\Span_{\mathbb{R}}(f_{i})\cap\overline{g}=\Span_{\mathbb{R}}((\ker\alpha)\cap\overline{g})\cap\overline{g}=(\ker\alpha)\cap\overline{g}=\overline{f}.

Because both
f_{i}
and
f
are convex and relatively open, we conclude that
f=f_{i}
, hence
f
is indeed a face of
g
.
∎

We now assume that we are given an affine space
A
directed by
V
. Two affine convex cones
f=x+\vec{f}
and
g=y+\vec{g}
in
A
, directed by cones
\vec{f}
and
\vec{g}
in
V
, are said to be
parallel
(
f/\!/g
) if they have the same direction, that is if
\vec{f}=\vec{g}
. The cones
f
and
g
are said to be
equivalent
(
f\sim g
) if they are parallel and have non-empty intersection or, equivalently, if they share a parallel subcone, ie. if there exists a cone
h\subset f\cap g
that is parallel to
f
and
g
. Call
f\subset A
an
affine
\mathcal{F}
-cone
if its direction
\vec{f}
belongs to
\mathcal{F}
. Denote by
\mathcal{F}_{A}
the set of affine
\mathcal{F}
-cones in
A
.

Then, the compactification of
A
associated to a fan
\mathcal{F}
in
V
is the quotient of the set of affine
\mathcal{F}
-cones in
A
under the equivalence relation defined above:

\overline{A}^{\mathcal{F}}=\underbrace{\{x+\vec{f},x\in A,\vec{f}\in\mathcal{F}\}}_{\mathcal{F}_{A}}/\sim.

The equivalence classes can be grouped by direction, thereby partitioning the compactification:

\overline{A}^{\mathcal{F}}=\bigsqcup_{\vec{f}\in\mathcal{F}}A_{\vec{f}}.

For each
\vec{f}\in\mathcal{F}
, the subspace
A_{\vec{f}}=\{[x+\vec{f}],x\in A\}
is called the
façade
of
\overline{A}^{\mathcal{F}}
of type
\vec{f}
. This façade is naturally an affine space under
V/\Span_{\mathbb{R}}(\vec{f})
.
Geometrically, setting an origin at some point
o\in A
, we compactify each open cone
o+\vec{f}=\{x\in A,\forall i\in I,\alpha_{i}(x-o)>0\}
by allowing the
\alpha_{i}(x-o)
for
i\in I
, which we think of as the distance from
x
to the walls of the cone
o+\vec{f}
, to assume infinite values.

Precisely, we have the following statement

Proposition 46

(Corollary of
[
Cha10
, Proposition 3.2.4]
)
.

Let
(x_{n})
be a sequence of points in
A
, and
l=[a+\vec{f}]\in\overline{A}^{\mathcal{F}}
. Then, the sequence
(x_{n})
converges to
l
if and only if, for each linear form
\alpha\in V^{*}
such that
\vec{f}\subset\ker\alpha
, we have

\alpha(x_{n}-a)\underset{n\to+\infty}{\longrightarrow}0

and, for each
\alpha\in V^{*}
with
\alpha(x)>0
for each
x
in
\vec{f}
, we have

\alpha(x_{n}-a)\underset{n\to+\infty}{\longrightarrow}+\infty.

These façades form a stratification of
\overline{A}^{\mathcal{F}}
, specifically we have

A_{\vec{g}}\subset\overline{A_{\vec{f}}}\iff\vec{f}\text{ is a face of }\vec{g}.

Fans associated to types of pseudo-parabolic subgroups

We now define the family of fans that will concern us in the following.

Weyl chambers are in one-to-one correspondence with bases of the root system: Given a basis
\Delta
of the root system, the cone

C(\Delta)=\{x\in V,\forall\alpha\in\Delta,\alpha(x)>0\}

is a Weyl chamber. Conversely, given a Weyl chamber
C
, the intersection
C^{\vee}\cap\Phi
of the dual cone of
C
with
\Phi
is a positive system of roots that gives rise to a unique basis. Moreover, given a Weyl chamber
C
corresponding to a basis
\Delta
, the faces of
C
are in one-to-one correspondence with the subsets of
\Delta
: If
J\subset\Delta
, then the set

\{x\in V,\forall\alpha\in J,\alpha(x)=0\text{ and }\forall\alpha\in\Delta\setminus J,\alpha(x)>0\}

is a face of
C
, said to be of type
J
.

Example 47
.

Fix a Weyl chamber
C
corresponding to a basis
\Delta
of
\Phi
, and a subset
J\subset\Delta
containing no connected component of
\Delta
, considered as a Dynkin diagram. Say a face of
C
is
admissible
if its type contains no connected component of
\Delta
. Then, for each admissible face
f
of
C
of type
I
, we let
\overline{f}^{J}
denote the union of
f
and its faces of type included in
I\cup(J\cap I^{\perp})
.
Then, the set

J\cdot f=\bigcup_{w\in\langle s_{\alpha},\alpha\in J\cap I^{\perp}\rangle}w\overline{f}^{J}

is a relatively open polyhedral cone, and the set

\mathcal{F}^{J}=\bigcup_{w\in W}w\cdot\{J\cdot f,f\text{ admissible face of }C\}

is a fan in
V
by
[
Cha10
, Proposition 2.5.3]
.

When the root system is given by a maximal split torus
S
in a quasi-reductive group
G
over a field
k
, we have a natural group-theoretic interpretation for this fan.

Let
P
is a pseudo-parabolic subgroup of non-degenerate type in a quasi-reductive group
G
over a field
k
. Let
P_{0}
be a minimal pseudo-parabolic subgroup of
G
and
S
a maximal split torus such that
S\subset P_{0}\subset P
. Set
V=V(G,S)
, and
\Phi=\Phi(G,S)
. Let
W
be the Weyl group of
\Phi
.
Let
\Delta
be the basis of the root system
\Phi
corresponding to
P_{0}
and
J=\Delta\cap-\Phi(P,S)
be the type of
P
.
Set

C(P)=\{x\in V(G,S),\forall\alpha\in\Phi(\mathcal{R}_{u,k}(P),S),\langle\alpha,x\rangle\geq 0\},

and let

C=\{x\in V(G,S),\forall\alpha\in\Delta,\langle\alpha,x\rangle>0\}

be the Weyl chamber associated to
P_{0}
.
These cones are related as follows.

Proposition 48
.

With the above notation, we have

C(P)=\overline{J\cdot C}.

Consequently, the open cones of
\mathcal{F}^{J}
are the
\overset{\circ}{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{1543100}{0}{{\char 62\relax}}}\hbox{$\displaystyle C(Q)$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{1543100}{0}{{\char 62\relax}}}\hbox{$\textstyle C(Q)$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{1230359}{0}{{\char 62\relax}}}\hbox{$\scriptstyle C(Q)$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{1058281}{0}{{\char 62\relax}}}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle C(Q)$}}}}
, where
Q
ranges among the pseudo-parabolic subgroups containing
S
conjugate to
P
. Moreover, the correspondence
P\mapsto C(P)
is
W
-equivariant.

Proof.

Observe that

\overline{J\cdot C}=\bigcup_{w\in\langle s_{\alpha},\alpha\in J\rangle}w(\overline{C}),

which is the union of the closed Weyl chambers associated to the bases
w(\Delta)
for
w
in
W_{J}=\langle s_{\alpha},\alpha\in J\rangle
.
Let
L_{P}
be the Levi subgroup of
P
corresponding to
S
. Then, the reflection group
W_{J}
is the Weyl group
W(L_{P},S)
. In particular, because the conjugacy action of
L_{P}
on
P
preserves
\mathcal{R}_{u,k}(P)
, the group
W_{J}
stabilises the cone
C(P)
. Hence, because
\overline{C}\subset C(P)
, we must in turn have
\overline{J\cdot C}\subset C(P)
.

On the other hand, the cone
C(P)
is a union of Weyl facets with non-empty interior and is therefore the closure of the union of the Weyl chambers that it contains. Further, a Weyl chamber is contained in
C(P)
if and only if it corresponds to a minimal pseudo-parabolic subgroup of
G
containing
S
and contained in
P
by
[
CGP15
, Proposition 3.5.14]
. Now, by
[
CGP15
, Proposition 2.2.10]
, the map

Q\mapsto Q\cap L_{P}

establishes an
L_{P}(k)
-equivariant and order-preserving bijection between pseudo-parabolic subgroups of
P
containing
S
and pseudo-parabolic subgroups of
L_{P}
containing
S
. By
[
CGP15
, Theorem C.2.3]
and
[
CGP15
, Theorem C.2.5]
, the normaliser
N_{L_{P}}(S)(k)
acts transitively on the minimal pseudo-parabolic subgroups of
L_{P}
containing
S
, hence it acts transitively on the minimal pseudo-parabolic subgroups of
P
containing
S
. Hence, the Weyl chambers contained in
C(P)
are precisely the
w(\Delta)
for
w\in W(L_{P},S)(k)=W_{J}
. We conclude that
C(P)
is in fact equal to
\bigcup_{w\in W_{J}}w(\overline{C})=\overline{J\cdot C}
, which completes the proof.
∎

Compactifications of affine buildings

To conclude these preliminary considerations, we extend the above construction to yield compactifications of affine buildings. In
[
Lan96
]
, where the affine buildings are Bruhat-Tits buildings of reductive groups, this is obtained by way of a glueing procedure, much like the building itself: stabilisers are defined for points of the compactification of a given apartment with respect to the Weyl fan, then the compactification of
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
is defined to be the quotient of
G(k)\times\overline{A}^{\mathcal{F}}
by an equivalence relation similar to that defining the building
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
.

A salient feature of
[
Cha10
]
is that it performs the same construction while solely relying on the internal geometry of the building. For this purpose, the author introduces the notion of a cone in the building, which in some sense is a glueing of affine cones in various apartments containing a common subcone of a Weyl facet, called its
core
.

Consider an apartment
A
in an affine building
\mathcal{B}
. We denote by
V
the direction of
A
and by
\Phi\subset V^{*}
its vectorial root system. We also assume that we are given a fan
\mathcal{F}
in
V
that is invariant under the action of the Weyl group
W=W(\Phi)
. Under this assumption, the notion of an affine
\mathcal{F}
-cone can be defined in the whole building: An affine
\mathcal{F}
-cone is a subset
f\subset\mathcal{B}
such that there exist an apartment
B
containing
f
and an isomorphism of affine Coxeter complexes
\phi_{B}:A\to B
such that
\phi_{B}^{-1}(f)
is an affine
\mathcal{F}
-cone in
A
. For each apartment
B
, denote by
\mathcal{F}_{B}
the set of affine
\mathcal{F}
-cones contained in
B
.

Then, for each cone
f\in\mathcal{F}
, the
core
of
f
is the convex cone

\delta(f)=f\cap\Fix(\Stab_{W}(f)).

Example 49
.

In the case when
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}^{J}
with the notations of the previous example, then, for each face
f
of the Weyl cone
C
of admissible type
I
, the core of
J\cdot f
is the face of
C
of type
I\cup(J\cap I^{\perp})
.

Likewise, given any affine
\mathcal{F}
-cone
f=x+\vec{f}
in
A
, the core of
f
is defined as
\delta(f)=x+\delta(\vec{f})
.

We then define the cone of
\mathcal{B}
associated to an affine cone
f
in
A
to be

\widetilde{f}=\bigcup_{\delta(f)\subset B}\phi_{B}(f),

where
B
ranges among all apartments containing the core
\delta(f)
, and
\phi_{B}:A\to B
is an isomorphism of Coxeter complexes fixing the intersection
A\cap B
. Denote by
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{B}}
the set

\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{B}}=\{\widetilde{f},f\in\mathcal{F}_{A},A\text{ apartment of }\mathcal{B}\}.

It can then be checked that the
\phi_{B}(f)
all have the same core, which we also call the core of the cone
\widetilde{f}
and denote by
\delta(\widetilde{f})
.

Finally, the compactification of
\mathcal{B}
is constructed in the same way as that of
A
, ie. as equivalence classes of cones. The only problem is to define the notion of parallelism for cones in
\mathcal{B}
, which is where cores play a crucial role.
Indeed, it follows from
[
Cha10
, Corollaire 4.5.9]
that, given any two cones
F
and
G
in
\mathcal{B}
, there exists an apartment
A
that contains parallel subcones of
\delta(F)
and
\delta(G)
. We then say that
F
and
G
are
parallel
(
F/\!/G
) if there exist parallel subcones
f\subset\delta(F)
and
g\subset\delta(G)
and an apartment
A
containing
f
and
g
such that
f/\!/g
in
A
(this parallelism does not depend on the specific
A
).
Likewise, we say that
F
and
G
are
equivalent
(
F\sim G
) if
F
and
G
are parallel and have a non-empty intersection. Then, we set

\overline{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathcal{F}}=\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{B}}/\sim.

We call
\overline{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathcal{F}}
the
polyhedral compactification
of
\mathcal{B}
with respect to the fan
\mathcal{F}
.

As in the previous case, the equivalence classes can be grouped in parallelism classes:

\overline{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathcal{F}}=\bigcup_{F\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{B}}}\mathcal{B}_{F}.

For each
F\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{B}}
, the subspace
\mathcal{B}_{F}=\{G\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{B}},G/\!/F\}
is called the (outer)
façade
of
\mathcal{B}
of type
F
.
The façade
\mathcal{B}_{F}
is naturally an affine building whose apartments are the façades
A_{f}
, for apartments
A
containing affine
\mathcal{F}
-cones
f
such that
\widetilde{f}/\!/F
. These façades are either equal or disjoint, and form the strata of a topological stratification of
\overline{\mathcal{B}}^{\mathcal{F}}

[
Cha10
, §8.2.2]
.

4.2
A comparison

Because the building
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
arises from valuated root data in
G(k)

[
Sol18
, Section 3.2]
, Proposition 7.4 of
[
Cha10
]
applies. It is thus sufficient, in order to identify the aforementioned compactifications, to prove that the procedure of taking the closure of a given apartment in one space and the other yields homeomorphic spaces.

Proposition 50
.

Let
P
be a pseudo-parabolic subgroup of non-degenerate type, let
P_{0}\subset P
be a minimal pseudo-parabolic subgroup, and let
S\subset P_{0}
a maximal split torus. Denote by
\Delta
the basis of
\Phi(G,S)
associated to
P_{0}
and let
J\subset\Delta
be the type of
P
.

The map
\vartheta_{P}
extends to a continuous map

\overline{A(G,S)}^{\mathcal{F}^{J}}\to(G/P)^{\an}

that is a homeomorphism onto its image.

Proof.

By
[
GJT98
, Lemma 3.28]
, it suffices to prove that, for any two sequences
(x_{n})
and
(x^{\prime}_{n})
of points in
A(G,S)
, the following are equivalent:

(i)

The sequences
(x_{n})
and
(x^{\prime}_{n})
converge and have the same limit in
\overline{A(G,S)}^{\mathcal{F}^{J}}
.

(ii)

The sequences
(\vartheta_{P}(x_{n}))
and
(\vartheta_{P}(x^{\prime}_{n}))
converge and have the same limit in
(G/P)^{\an}
.

We use Propositions
32
and
46
to characterise convergence in one space and the other. Both characterisations can be translated into convergence conditions on the sequences
(\langle a,x_{n}\rangle)
and
(\langle a,x^{\prime}_{n}\rangle)
for certain roots
a
.

Fix a special vertex
o\in A(G,S)
. Identify
A(G,S)
with its direction
V(G,S)
setting
o
as origin.
Let

\begin{array}[]{ccl}C(P)&=&\{x\in A(G,S),\forall a\in\Phi(\mathcal{R}_{u,k}(P),S),\langle a,x\rangle\geq 0\}\end{array}.

(Step 1: (i)
\implies
(ii))

Let
(x_{n})
and
(x^{\prime}_{n})
be two sequences of points in
A(G,S)
. Assume that both
(x_{n})
and
(x^{\prime}_{n})
converge in
\overline{A(G,S)}^{\mathcal{F}^{J}}
and have the same limit
l
lying in the façade
A(G,S)_{\vec{f}}
, where
\vec{f}\in\mathcal{F}^{J}
.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
\vec{f}
is a face of
\overset{\circ}{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{1536775}{0}{{\char 62\relax}}}\hbox{$\displaystyle C(P)$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{1536775}{0}{{\char 62\relax}}}\hbox{$\textstyle C(P)$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{1221835}{0}{{\char 62\relax}}}\hbox{$\scriptstyle C(P)$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{1047381}{0}{{\char 62\relax}}}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle C(P)$}}}}
. Indeed, by construction,
\vec{f}
is a face of an open cone of
\mathcal{F}^{J}
and, by Proposition
48
, the Weyl group
W(G,S)(k)
acts transitively on these open cones. If
w\in W(G,S)(k)
maps
\vec{f}
to a face of
\overset{\circ}{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{1536775}{0}{{\char 62\relax}}}\hbox{$\displaystyle C(P)$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{1536775}{0}{{\char 62\relax}}}\hbox{$\textstyle C(P)$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{1221835}{0}{{\char 62\relax}}}\hbox{$\scriptstyle C(P)$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{1047381}{0}{{\char 62\relax}}}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle C(P)$}}}}
, and if
\dot{w}\in N_{G}(S)(k)
is a lift of
w
, then
(\dot{w}x_{n})
and
(\dot{w}x^{\prime}_{n})
both converge and have the same limit
\dot{w}l
lying in
A(G,S)_{w\vec{f}}
, and
w\vec{f}
is a face of
\overset{\circ}{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{1536775}{0}{{\char 62\relax}}}\hbox{$\displaystyle C(P)$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{1536775}{0}{{\char 62\relax}}}\hbox{$\textstyle C(P)$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{1221835}{0}{{\char 62\relax}}}\hbox{$\scriptstyle C(P)$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{1047381}{0}{{\char 62\relax}}}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle C(P)$}}}}
. Furthermore,
(\vartheta_{P}(x_{n}))
and
(\vartheta_{P}(x^{\prime}_{n}))
converge and have the same limit in
(G/P)^{\an}
if and only if the same holds for
(\vartheta_{P}(\dot{w}x_{n}))
and
(\vartheta_{P}(\dot{w}x^{\prime}_{n}))
by
G(k)
-equivariance of
\vartheta_{P}
and continuity of the action of
G(k)
on
(G/P)^{\an}
.

Let us therefore assume that
\vec{f}
is a face of
\overset{\circ}{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{1536775}{0}{{\char 62\relax}}}\hbox{$\displaystyle C(P)$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{1536775}{0}{{\char 62\relax}}}\hbox{$\textstyle C(P)$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{1221835}{0}{{\char 62\relax}}}\hbox{$\scriptstyle C(P)$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{1047381}{0}{{\char 62\relax}}}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle C(P)$}}}}
. Each
a\in\Phi(\mathcal{R}_{u,k}(P),S)
, assumes only nonnegative values on
\vec{f}
. In fact, because
\vec{f}
is relatively open, each
a
is either identically zero or positive on
\vec{f}
. Therefore, by Proposition
46
, the limits

\lim_{n\to\infty}\langle a,x_{n}\rangle\text{ and }\lim_{n\to\infty}\langle a,x^{\prime}_{n}\rangle

both exist in
\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\}
and coincide for each
a\in\Phi(\mathcal{R}_{u,k}(P),S)
.

We now deduce that
(\vartheta_{P}(x_{n}))
and
(\vartheta_{P}(x^{\prime}_{n}))
converge in
(G/P)^{\an}
and have the same limit. We use Proposition
32
.
Let
T
be a maximal torus of
G
containing
S
and
K/k
be a finite Galois extension such that:

1.

The torus
T_{K}
is split.

2.

The apartment
A(G_{K},T_{K})
contains
p_{K/k}(A(G,S))
.

3.

The point
o_{K}\in A(G_{K},T_{K})
is a special vertex.

Identify
A(G_{K},T_{K})
with its direction
V(G_{K},T_{K})
by setting the origin at
o_{K}
. Let
\mu:\mathbb{G}_{m}\to S
such that
P=P_{G}(\mu)
and set
U^{-}=U_{G}(-\mu)
. By Proposition
32
, both
(\vartheta_{P}(x_{n}))
and
(\vartheta_{P}(x^{\prime}_{n}))
take their values in
(\Omega(T,P)/P)^{\an}
. Moreover, identifying
\Omega(T,P)/P
with
U^{-}
and choosing a system of coordinates
(\xi_{\alpha,i})_{(\alpha,i)\in\mathbf{\Psi}_{K}}
on
(U^{-})_{K}
adapted to
o_{K}
, we have, for each
f=\sum_{\nu\in\mathbb{N}^{J}}f_{\nu}\xi^{\nu}\in K[U^{-}]
:

|f(\vartheta_{P_{K}}((x_{n})_{K}))|=\max_{\nu\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbf{\Psi}_{K}}}|f_{\nu}|\prod_{(\alpha,i)\in\mathbf{\Psi}_{K}}e^{\nu(\alpha,i)\langle\alpha,(x_{n})_{K}\rangle},

which we may rewrite

|f(\vartheta_{P_{K}}((x_{n})_{K}))|=\max_{\nu\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbf{\Psi}_{K}}}|f_{\nu}|\prod_{a\in\Psi}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\alpha,i)\in\mathbf{\Psi}_{K}\\
\alpha_{|S}=a\end{subarray}}e^{\nu(\alpha,i)\langle a,x_{n}\rangle}.

Evidently, for each
f\in K[U^{-}]
, the quantities
|f(\vartheta_{P_{K}}((x_{n})_{K}))|
and
|f(\vartheta_{P_{K}}((x^{\prime}_{n})_{K}))|
converge in
\mathbb{R}
with the same limit, hence the sequences
(\vartheta_{P_{K}}((x_{n})_{K}))
and
(\vartheta_{P_{K}}((x^{\prime}_{n})_{K}))
converge in
(U^{-})^{\an}_{K}
and have the same limit. By continuity of
\pr_{K/k}^{\an}
, so do the sequences
(\vartheta_{P}(x_{n}))
and
(\vartheta_{P}(x^{\prime}_{n}))
, by commutativity of the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(G_{K},T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(U^{-})_{K}^{\an}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(G,S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(U^{-})^{\an}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vartheta_{P_{K}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pr_{K/k}^{\an}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{K/k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vartheta_{P}}

.

(Step 2: (ii)
\implies
(i))
Let
(x_{n})
and
(x^{\prime}_{n})
be two sequences of points of
A(G,S)
. Assume that
(\vartheta_{P}(x_{n}))
and
(\vartheta_{P}(x^{\prime}_{n}))
both converge in
(G/P)^{\an}
. We first prove that the common limit of the two sequences may be assumed to lie in
(\Omega(S,P)/P)^{\an}
.

Recall from Proposition
48
that

A(G,S)=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\text{ conjugate to }P\\
S\subset Q\end{subarray}}C(Q).

Because only finitely many subgroups
Q
conjugate to
P
contain
S
, there exists such a
Q
and a subsequence
(x_{n_{j}})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\vartheta_{P}(x_{n_{j}})\in C(Q)
for each
j\in\mathbb{N}
. In particular, we have
\lim_{n\to\infty}\vartheta_{P}(x_{n})\in\overline{\vartheta_{P}(C(Q))}
.
By Proposition
37
, for each pseudo-parabolic subgroup
Q
containing
S
conjugate to
P
, the image
\vartheta_{Q}(C(Q))
is relatively compact in
(\Omega(S,Q)/Q)^{\an}
, ie.
\overline{\vartheta_{Q}(C(Q))}\subset(\Omega(S,Q)/Q)^{\an}
.
Let
w\in W(G,S)(k)
be such that
wC(Q)=C(P)
and
\dot{w}
be a lift of
w
in
N_{G}(Q)(k)
. Then, we have
P=\dot{w}Q\dot{w}^{-1}
, and therefore, by Proposition
28
:

\overline{\vartheta_{P}(C(Q))}=\iota_{P,Q}^{\an}(\vartheta_{Q}(C(Q)))\subset\iota_{P,Q}^{\an}((\Omega(S,Q)/Q)^{\an})=\dot{w}(\Omega(S,P)/P)^{\an}.

Consequently, the sequences
(\vartheta_{P}(\dot{w}^{-1}x_{n}))
and
(\vartheta_{P}(\dot{w}^{-1}x^{\prime}_{n}))
converge to a point in
(\Omega(S,P)/P)^{\an}
. Moreover, the sequences
(x_{n})
and
(x^{\prime}_{n})
converge to the same limit if and only if the same holds for
(\dot{w}^{-1}x_{n})
and
(\dot{w}^{-1}x^{\prime}_{n})
.

Let us therefore reduce to the case when
(\vartheta_{P}(x_{n}))
and
(\vartheta_{P}(x^{\prime}_{n}))
both converge in
(\Omega(S,P)/P)^{\an}\simeq(U^{-})^{\an}
. Choose a special vertex
o
in
A(G,S)
, a maximal torus
T
of
G
containing
S
and a finite Galois extension
K/k
as in Step 1 and make the same choice of notations. Let
a\in-\Phi(\mathcal{R}_{u,k}(P),S)
and let
\phi=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\alpha,i)\in{\mathbf{\Psi}_{K}}\\
\alpha_{|S}=a\end{subarray}}\phi_{\alpha,i}\xi_{\alpha,i}\in k[U^{-}]\subset K[U^{-}]
be a
k
-linear form on the root group
U_{a}
. Then, we have

|\phi(\vartheta_{P}(x_{n}))|=|\phi_{K}(\vartheta_{P_{K}}((x_{n})_{K}))|=\left(\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\alpha,i)\in{\mathbf{\Psi}_{K}}\\
\alpha_{|S}=a\end{subarray}}|\phi_{\alpha,i}|e^{\langle\alpha,(x_{n})_{K}\rangle}\right),

which rewrites as

|\phi(\vartheta_{P}(x_{n}))|=\left(\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\alpha,i)\in{\mathbf{\Psi}_{K}}\\
\alpha_{|S}=a\end{subarray}}|\phi_{\alpha,i}|\right)e^{\langle a,x_{n}\rangle}.

Because the sequences
(|\phi(\vartheta_{P}(x_{n}))|)
and
(|\phi(\vartheta_{P}(x^{\prime}_{n}))|)
converge with the same limit, the sequences
(\langle a,x_{n}\rangle)
and
(\langle a,x^{\prime}_{n}\rangle)
must converge in
\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}
with the same limit.
We deduce that, for each
a\in\Phi(\mathcal{R}_{u,k}(P),S)
, we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}\langle a,x_{n}\rangle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\langle a,x^{\prime}_{n}\rangle\in\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\}.

Let

I_{c}=\left\{a\in\Phi(\mathcal{R}_{u,k}(P),S),\lim_{n\to\infty}\langle a,x_{n}\rangle\in\mathbb{R}\right\}

and

I_{d}=\left\{a\in\Phi(\mathcal{R}_{u,k}(P),S),\lim_{n\to\infty}\langle a,x_{n}\rangle=+\infty\right\}.

Then, denoting by
\vec{f}
the set

\vec{f}=\{x\in V(G,S),\forall a\in I_{c},\langle a,x\rangle=0\text{ and }\forall a\in I_{d},\langle a,x\rangle>0\},

the cone
\vec{f}
is a face of
C(P)
by Lemma
45
and it follows from Proposition
46
that
(x_{n})
and
(x^{\prime}_{n})
converge to a common limit lying in the façade
A(G,S)_{\vec{f}}
, which completes the proof of the equivalence.
∎

By the criterion
[
Cha10
, Proposition 7.4]
, the compactification
\overline{\vartheta_{P}(\mathcal{B}(G,k))}
is thus
G(k)
-equivariantly homeomorphic to the polyhedral compactification of
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
associated to the fan
\mathcal{F}^{J}
.

4.3
Identifying the strata

It now follows from
[
Cha10
, Corollaire 8.2.11]
that the compactification
\overline{\vartheta_{P}(\mathcal{B}(G,k))}
is stratified by affine buildings. Precisely, we have a
G(k)
-equivariant homeomorphism

\overline{\vartheta_{P}(\mathcal{B}(G,k))}=\bigcup_{F\in(\mathcal{F}^{J})_{\mathcal{B}(G,k)}}\mathcal{B}(G,k)_{F},

where the right-hand side is indexed by cones in
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
in the sense of
[
Cha10
, Sections 5.2]
. The stratum
\mathcal{B}(G,k)_{F}
corresponding to a cone
F
is in turn defined as the set of equivalence classes of cones parallel to
F
for the relation
[
Cha10
, Définition 5.4.1]
.

We begin by proving that these strata – which are affine buildings – can be interpreted as Bruhat-Tits buildings of certain Levi subgroups of pseudo-parabolic subgroups of
G
.

Theorem 51
.

Let
F
be a cone in
(\mathcal{F}^{J})_{\mathcal{B}(G,k)}
. There exists a unique pseudo-parabolic subgroup
Q
of
G
such that
\mathcal{B}(G,k)_{F}
is stable under the action of
Q(k)
. Moreover, this stratum identifies
Q(k)
-equivariantly to the Bruhat-Tits building of the quasi-reductive group
Q/\mathcal{R}_{us,k}(Q)
.

The proof of this theorem will take up most of this section and will therefore be broken down into several steps, namely Propositions
54
,
56
, and
57
. The outline goes as follows: We begin by associating to an apartment
A(G,S)
containing the core of
F
a pseudo-parabolic subgroup
Q
containing
S
. We prove that this pseudo-parabolic subgroup
Q
depends only on the parallelism class of
F
(thus neither on the specific representative
F
nor the specific apartment containing
\delta(F)
that we choose). We deduce from these observations that
Q(k)
stabilises the façade
\mathcal{B}(G,k)_{F}
and that the
k
-split unipotent radical
\mathcal{R}_{us,k}(Q)(k)
acts trivially on that same façade.
Lastly, we check that, given a Levi factor
L_{Q}\subset Q
, the projections
A(G,S)\to A(G,S)_{F}
with
S\subset L_{Q}
glue together to a
L_{Q}(k)
-equivariant map
L_{Q}(k)\cdot A(G,S)\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{B}(G,k)_{F}
that factors through an equivariant, simplicial homeomorphism
\mathcal{B}(L_{Q},k)\to\mathcal{B}(G,k)_{F}
, which will complete the proof.

Associating a pseudo-parabolic subgroup to a façade

Let us now carry out the first step, namely identifying the pseudo-parabolic subgroup
Q
and establishing its sole dependency on the parallelism class of
F
. The argument hinges on the following preliminary observation.

Proposition 52
.

Let
X
be a smooth irreducible variety over a field
k
complete with respect to a non-trivial absolute value. Then, any non-empty open subset of
X(k)
for its analytic topology is Zariski-dense in
X
.

Proof.

A proof is given in
[
Poo17
, Proposition 3.5.75 and Remark 3.5.76]
in the case of local fields, but a careful inspection shows this proof only to depend on the completeness of
k
(specifically in the use of the implicit function theorem over
k
in the proof of
[
Poo17
, Proposition 3.5.73 (i)]
).
∎

We take note of the following special case.

Corollary 53
.

Let
k
be a complete discretely valued field. Let
\mathfrak{X}
be a smooth irreducible variety over the valuation ring
k^{\circ}=\{x\in k,\omega(x)\geq 0\}
and let
X=\mathfrak{X}_{k}
. Then, the set of integral points
\mathfrak{X}(k^{\circ})
is either empty or Zariski-dense in
X
.

From this analytic observation, we draw the following consequence.

Proposition 54
.

Let
Q
be the Zariski closure in
G
of the pointwise stabiliser
P_{\delta(F)}
of
\delta(F)
in
G(k)
. Then
Q
is a pseudo-parabolic subgroup in
G
and, for any cone
F^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
parallel to
F
, the pointwise stabiliser
\hat{P}_{\delta(F^{\prime})}
is Zariski-dense in
Q
.

Proof.

We give an indirect proof, namely we first construct a suitable pseudo-parabolic subgroup
Q
then prove that, for any cone
F^{\prime}
parallel to
F
, the pointwise stabiliser

\hat{P}_{\delta(F^{\prime})}=\{g\in G(k),\forall x\in\delta(F^{\prime}),gx=x\}

is Zariski-dense in
Q
.

Let
S
be a maximal split torus of
G
such that the apartment
A(G,S)
contains the core
\delta(F)
of
F

[
Cha10
, Définition 3.3.2]
. Because the direction
\vec{\delta}(F)
is a Weyl facet in
V(G,S)
(Example
49
) , it corresponds to a unique parabolic subset
\Psi
of
\Phi(G,S)
, namely the intersection of the root system with the dual cone of
\vec{\delta}(F)

\Psi=\Phi(G,S)\cap\vec{\delta}(F)^{\vee}=\{\alpha\in\Phi(G,S)\ |\ \forall x\in\vec{\delta}(F),\langle\alpha,x\rangle\geq 0\}.

This subset is indeed parabolic as it is clear that, whenever
\alpha,\beta\in\Psi
and
\alpha+\beta\in\Phi(G,S)
, we have
\alpha+\beta\in\Psi
and, if
\alpha\in\Phi(G,S)
, then because the hyperplane
\{x\in V(G,S),\langle\alpha,x\rangle=0\}
is part of the hyperplane arrangement that defines
\Sigma(G,S)
, the root
\alpha
assumes a constant sign on each facet of
\Sigma(G,S)
, hence either
\alpha\in\Psi
or
-\alpha\in\Psi
. By
[
CGP15
, Theorem C.2.15]
, the subset
\Psi
then determines a unique pseudo-parabolic subgroup
Q
of
G
containing
S
such that
\Psi
is the set of
S
-weights in the Lie algebra of
Q
, namely
Q=P_{G}(\mu)
for any cocharacter
\mu\in\vec{\delta}(F)
.

We now prove that the pointwise stabiliser
\hat{P}_{\delta(F)}
is Zariski-dense in
Q
. Fix a special vertex
o
in
A(G,S)
and the associated valuation

(\varphi_{\alpha}:U_{\alpha}(k)\to\mathbb{R})_{\alpha\in\Phi(G,S)}

of the generating root datum
(Z_{G}(S)(k),(U_{\alpha}(k))_{\alpha\in\Phi(G,S)})
. Denote by
x
the vertex of the cone
\delta(F)
. By
[
BT72
, Définition 7.1.8 and Proposition 7.4.4]
, the pointwise stabiliser
\hat{P}_{\delta(F)}
is spanned by the subgroup
\hat{N}_{\delta(F)}=N_{G}(S)(k)\cap\hat{P}_{\delta(F)}
and the root subgroups
U_{\alpha,f_{\delta(F)}(\alpha)}
for
\alpha\in\Phi
, where

f_{\delta(F)}(\alpha)=\inf\{k\in\mathbb{R}\,|\,\forall z\in\delta(F),\langle\alpha,z-o\rangle+k\geq 0\}.

First of all, observe that if
n\in N_{\delta(F)}
, then
n
fixes the vertex
x
of
\delta(F)
as well as the point
x+\mu
. Hence, the linear part of
n
fixes
\mu
, that is
n
commutes with
\mu
. Hence, we have the inclusion
\hat{N}_{\delta(F)}\subset Z_{G}(\mu)(k)\subset Q(k)
. Next, recall from
[
BT72
, Proposition 7.4.5]
that, if
\alpha\in\Phi(G,S)
and
u\in U_{\alpha}(k)\setminus\{1\}
, then the set of points of
A(G,S)
that are fixed under
u
is the affine root

\{z\in A(G,S),\langle\alpha,z-o\rangle+\varphi_{\alpha}(u)\geq 0\}.

It follows that, if
\langle\alpha,\mu\rangle<0
and
u\in U_{\alpha}(k)\setminus\{1\}
, there exists
t>0
such that
x+t\mu
is not fixed under
u
and therefore
u
does not fix
\delta(F)
. Hence, if
\langle\alpha,\mu\rangle<0
, we have
U_{\alpha,\delta(F)}=\{1\}
. Therefore
\hat{P}_{\delta(F)}
is spanned by a subgroup of
Z_{G}(\mu)(k)
and subgroups of
U_{\alpha}(k)
for
\langle\alpha,\mu\rangle\geq 0
, hence
\hat{P}_{\delta(F)}\subset Q(k)
. Consequently, the Zariski closure of
\hat{P}_{\delta(F)}
lies in the Zariski closure of
Q(k)
, ie.
Q
by Proposition
52
.
On the other hand, if
\langle\alpha,\mu\rangle\geq 0
, we have
\langle\alpha,x-o\rangle=\inf\{\langle\alpha,z-o\rangle,z\in\delta(F)\}
and therefore
f_{\delta(F)}(\alpha)=\inf(\Gamma^{\prime}_{\alpha}\cap[-\langle\alpha,x-o\rangle,+\infty[)<\infty
. Hence, for each nondivisible
\alpha\in\Phi(Q,S)
, the stabiliser
\hat{P}_{\delta(F)}
contains the subgroup
U_{\alpha,f_{\delta(F)}(\alpha)}U_{2\alpha,f_{\delta(F)}(2\alpha)}
, which is the set of
k^{\circ}
-points of a smooth connected
k^{\circ}
-model of
U_{\alpha}
by
[
Lou21
, Lemme 4.4]
, and therefore Zariski-dense in
U_{\alpha}
by Corollary
53
. Moreover, because the maximal compact subgroup
Z_{G}(S)(k)_{b}
of
Z_{G}(S)(k)
acts trivially on the apartment
A(G,S)
, it fixes
\delta(F)
and is thus contained in
\hat{N}_{\delta(F)}
. Because
Z_{G}(S)(k)_{b}
is the set of integral points of a smooth connected
k^{\circ}
-model of
Z_{G}(S)
by
[
Lou21
, Proposition 4.3]
, it is Zariski-dense in
Z_{G}(S)
. Consequently, the Zariski closure of
\hat{P}_{\delta(F)}
contains
U_{\alpha}
for each nondivisible
\alpha\in\Phi(Q,S)
as well as
Z_{G}(S)
, hence it contains
Q
, which completes the proof.

It is clear that the above argument is independent of the specific vertex
x\in A(G,S)
of the affine cone
\delta(F)
. Hence, for any affine cone
F^{\prime}\subset A(G,S)
parallel to
F
, the pointwise stabiliser
\hat{P}_{\delta(F^{\prime})}
is Zariski-dense in
Q
.

Lastly, if
\mathfrak{F}\subset\mathcal{B}(G,k)
is an affine cone that is parallel to
F
, then there exist parallel subcones
F^{\prime}\subset F
and
\mathfrak{F}^{\prime}\subset\mathfrak{F}
and an apartment
A(G,S^{\prime})
that contains both
\delta(F^{\prime})
and
\delta(\mathfrak{F}^{\prime})
. Then, several applications of the above result yield that the Zariski closures of the pointwise stabilisers of
\delta(F),\delta(F^{\prime}),\delta(\mathfrak{F})
, and
\delta(\mathfrak{F}^{\prime})
are all equal to
Q
.

∎

Corollary 55
.

For any maximal split torus
S^{\prime}
in
G
such that
A(G,S^{\prime})
contains
\delta(F^{\prime})
for some cone
F^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
parallel to
F
, we have the inclusion
S^{\prime}\subset Q
.

Proof.

The pointwise stabiliser
\hat{P}_{\delta(F^{\prime})}
contains the maximal compact subgroup
Z_{G}(S^{\prime})(k)_{b}
of
Z_{G}(S^{\prime})
, which is the set of
k^{\circ}
-points of a smooth connected
k^{\circ}
-model of
Z_{G}(S^{\prime})
by
[
Lou21
, Lemme 4.4]
and therefore Zariski-dense in
Z_{G}(S^{\prime})
by Corollary
53
. It follows that the Zariski closure
Q
of
\hat{P}_{\delta(F^{\prime})}
contains
Z_{G}(S^{\prime})
, and therefore
S^{\prime}
.
∎

Action of the pseudo-parabolic subgroup on the façade

We now deduce that
Q(k)
acts on the façade
\mathcal{B}(G,k)_{F}
through its quotient
(Q/\mathcal{R}_{us,k}(Q))(k)=Q(k)/\mathcal{R}_{us,k}(Q)(k)
, the first step towards identifying the façade with the Bruhat-Tits building of
Q/\mathcal{R}_{us,k}(Q)
.

Proposition 56
.

The group
Q(k)
is the stabiliser of the façade
\mathcal{B}(G,k)_{F}
in
G(k)
. Moreover, the subgroup
\mathcal{R}_{us,k}(Q)(k)
fixes
\mathcal{B}(G,k)_{F}
pointwise.

Proof.

It follows from the previous proposition that, if
F^{\prime}
is an affine cone in an apartment
A(G,S^{\prime})
such that
\delta(F^{\prime})/\!/\delta(F)
, then we have
S^{\prime}\subset Q
. We check that
Q(k)
maps the façade
A(G,S^{\prime})_{F^{\prime}}
into
\mathcal{B}(G,k)_{F}
. To this end, it is sufficient to check that the centraliser
Z_{G}(S^{\prime})(k)
and the root groups
U_{\alpha}(k)
for
\alpha\in\Phi(Q,S^{\prime})
all map
A(G,S^{\prime})_{F^{\prime}}
into
\mathcal{B}(G,k)_{F}
. Let
\mu\in X_{*}(S^{\prime})
be such that
Q=P_{G}(\mu)
, so that

\Phi(Q,S^{\prime})=\{\alpha\in\Phi(G,S^{\prime})\,|\,\langle\alpha,\mu\rangle\geq 0\}.

First of all, the subgroup
Z_{G}(S^{\prime})(k)
acts on
A(G,S^{\prime})
by translations, and therefore permutes affine cones in
A(G,S^{\prime})
parallel to
\delta(F^{\prime})
, hence
Z_{G}(S^{\prime})(k)
maps
A(G,S^{\prime})_{F^{\prime}}
to itself. Next, we deal with root groups. Let
\alpha\in\Phi(G,S^{\prime})
such that
\langle\alpha,\mu\rangle\geq 0
and
u\in U_{\alpha}(k)
. By
[
BT72
, Proposition 7.4.5]
, there exists a half-space
H
in
A(G,S^{\prime})
with direction

\vec{H}=\{x\in A(G,S^{\prime})\,|\,\langle\alpha,x\rangle\geq 0\}

that
u
fixes pointwise. Denote by
x^{\prime}
the vertex of
F^{\prime}
. There exists
v\in X_{*}(S^{\prime})
such that
x+v\in H
and therefore
x+v+\vec{\delta}(F^{\prime})\subset H
. Then, because
u
induces an affine map from
A(G,S^{\prime})
to
A(uS^{\prime}u^{-1},k)
that fixes
H
, the cone
u(\delta(F^{\prime}))=\delta(u(F^{\prime}))
is parallel to
u(x+v+\vec{\delta}(F^{\prime}))=x+v+\vec{\delta}(F^{\prime})
, and therefore to
\delta(F^{\prime})
. Therefore,
u(F^{\prime})
is parallel to
F^{\prime}
and we have
u([F^{\prime}])\in\mathcal{B}(G,k)_{F}.
Moreover, if
\langle\alpha,\mu\rangle>0
, then we may choose the cocharacter
v
of the form
n\mu
for some sufficiently large
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then, the cone
x+n\mu+\vec{\delta}(F^{\prime})
is a parallel subcone of
F^{\prime}
that is fixed by
u
, hence, in that case,
u
fixes the equivalence class
[F^{\prime}]
.

We have thus proved that the group
Q(k)
leaves
\mathcal{B}(G,k)_{F}
globally invariant and that the subgroup
\mathcal{R}_{us,k}(Q)(k)
, which is spanned by the
U_{\alpha}(k)
for
\langle\alpha,\mu\rangle>0
, fixes
\mathcal{B}(G,k)_{F}
pointwise.

Assume that the stabiliser
\Stab_{G(k)}(\mathcal{B}(G,k)_{F})
properly contains
Q(k)
. Then, by
[
CGP15
, Theorem C.2.23]
, there exists a pseudo-parabolic subgroup
Q^{\prime}\supsetneq Q
such that

Q^{\prime}(k)=\Stab_{G(k)}(\mathcal{B}(G,k)_{F}).

Then, there exists a root
\alpha\in\Phi(Q^{\prime},S)
such that
\alpha\not\in\Phi(Q,S)
, ie.
\langle\alpha,\mu\rangle<0
. Then, we have
-\alpha\in\Phi(Q,S)\subset\Phi(Q^{\prime},S)
, hence
Q^{\prime}(k)
contains the root groups
U_{\alpha}(k)
and
U_{-\alpha}(k)
. Consequently, by
[
CGP15
, Proposition C.2.24]
, the subgroup
Q^{\prime}(k)
contains an element
m\in N_{G}(S)(k)
that lifts the reflection
s_{\alpha}\in W(G,S)(k)
with respect to the hyperplane
H_{\alpha}=\{x\in V(G,S)\,|\,\langle\alpha,x\rangle=0\}
. But the cone
\vec{\delta}(F)
is contained in the open half-space
\{x\in V(G,S)\,|\,\langle\alpha,x\rangle<0\}
and is thus not invariant under
s_{\alpha}
, hence
m
maps
\delta(F)
to a cone with direction
s_{\alpha}(\vec{\delta}(F))\neq\vec{\delta}(F)
, hence
m
maps the equivalence class
[F]\in\mathcal{B}(G,k)_{F}
outside of the façade
\mathcal{B}(G,k)_{F}
, a contradiction.
∎

Recovering the façade

Lastly, we identify the façade
\mathcal{B}(G,k)_{F}
with the Bruhat-Tits building of
Q/\mathcal{R}_{us,k}(Q)
(under the guise of a Levi subgroup
L_{Q}
of
Q
).

Proposition 57
.

Let
S
be a maximal split torus such that
\delta(F)\subset A(G,S)
and
L_{Q}
be the Levi subgroup of
Q
that contains
S
. Let
f=F\cap A(G,S)
.

1.

There exists a unique continuous
L_{Q}(k)
-equivariant surjection

p:L_{Q}(k)\cdot A(G,S)\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{B}(G,k)_{F}

that extends the projection
A(G,S)\twoheadrightarrow A(G,S)_{f}
.

2.

The map
p
factors through a
L_{Q}(k)
-equivariant simplicial homeomorphism

\bar{p}:\mathcal{B}(L_{Q},k)\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathcal{B}(G,k)_{F}.

Proof.

1.

Uniqueness follows directly from the
L_{Q}(k)
-equivariance of
p
. Our goal is now to prove that the
L_{Q}(k)
-equivariant map

\begin{array}[]{ccc}p:L_{Q}(k)\cdot A(G,S)&\longrightarrow&\mathcal{B}(G,k)_{F}\\
x&\longmapsto&\lim\limits_{t\to+\infty}\left(x+t\mu\right)\end{array}

is well-defined, where the operator
+
denotes the action of the vector space

V=\{x\in V(G,S)\,|\,\forall\alpha\in\Phi(G,S),\langle\alpha,\mu\rangle=0\implies\langle\alpha,x\rangle=0\}

on
L_{Q}(k)\cdot A(G,S)
defined in
[
BT72
, Proposition 7.6.4 (iii)]
and described in the Appendix.

Let
S^{\prime}
be a maximal split torus in
L_{Q}
and
x\in A(G,S^{\prime})
. For each root
\alpha\in\Phi(G,S^{\prime})
, we have

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\langle\alpha,x+t\mu\rangle\underset{t\to+\infty}{\longrightarrow}+\infty&\text{ if }&\langle\alpha,\mu\rangle>0\end{array}

and

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\langle\alpha,x+t\mu\rangle\underset{t\to+\infty}{\longrightarrow}\langle\alpha,x\rangle&\text{ if }&\langle\alpha,\mu\rangle=0\end{array}

hence, by
[
Cha10
, Proposition 3.2.4]
, the limit
p(x)=\lim_{t\to+\infty}(x+t\mu)
exists and lies in the façade
A(G,S^{\prime})_{\vec{f}^{\prime}}\subset\mathcal{B}(G,k)_{F}
, where
\vec{f}^{\prime}\subset X_{*}(S^{\prime})_{\mathbb{R}}
is the cone in the fan
\mathcal{F}^{P}
that contains
\vec{\delta}(F)
. More precisely, for each
S^{\prime}\subset L_{Q}
, the restriction of
p
to the apartment
A(G,S^{\prime})
is the projection
x\mapsto[x+\vec{f}^{\prime}]
of
A(G,S^{\prime})
onto its façade of type
\vec{f}^{\prime}

[
Cha10
, Définition 3.1.3]
.
In particular, the image of the map
p
is the union
\bigcup_{S^{\prime}\subset L_{Q}}A(G,S^{\prime})_{\vec{f}^{\prime}}
over maximal split tori
S^{\prime}\subset L_{Q}
. To prove that this union is the entire façade
\mathcal{B}(G,k)_{F}
, recall from
[
Cha10
, Proposition 8.1.5]
that the façade
\mathcal{B}(G,k)_{F}
is the union of the façades
A(G,S^{\prime})_{f^{\prime}}
for apartments
A(G,S^{\prime})
containing a cone
f^{\prime}
such that
\widetilde{f}^{\prime}/\!/F
. Let
A(G,S^{\prime})
be an apartment containing a cone
f^{\prime}
such that
\widetilde{f}^{\prime}/\!/F
. By Corollary
55
, there exists
q\in Q(k)
such that
A(G,S^{\prime})=qA(G,S)
. Then, because
Q(k)
maps
\mathcal{B}(G,k)_{F}
to itself, we must have
A(G,S^{\prime})_{f^{\prime}}=qA(G,S)_{f}
. Write
q=mu
with
m\in L_{Q}(k)
and
u\in\mathcal{R}_{u,k}(Q)(k)
. Because
\mathcal{R}_{u,k}(Q)(k)
acts trivially on the
\mathcal{B}(G,k)_{F}
by Proposition
56
, we then have
A(G,S^{\prime})_{f^{\prime}}=mA(G,S)_{f}
. Hence, we have

\mathcal{B}(G,k)_{F}=L_{Q}(k)\cdot A(G,S)_{f}

and the map
p
is therefore surjective.
Finally, the map
p
is
L_{Q}(k)
-equivariant because the
V
-action on
L_{Q}(k)\cdot A(G,S)
is continuous and commutes with the action of
L_{Q}(k)
.

2.

Recall from
[
BT72
, Proposition 7.6.4 (i)]
that we have a canonical
L_{Q}(k)
-equivariant map
\widetilde{\pi}:L_{Q}(k)\cdot A(G,S)\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{B}(L_{Q},k)
that extends the projection
A(G,S)\twoheadrightarrow A(L_{Q},S)
. Moreover, the map
\widetilde{\pi}
is invariant under the action of
V
on
L_{Q}(k)\cdot A(G,S)
and it follows from
[
BT72
, Proposition 7.6.4 (iii)]
that any
V
-invariant map with domain
L_{Q}(k)\cdot A(G,S)
factors through
\widetilde{\pi}
. Now, observe that, if
S^{\prime}\subset L_{Q}
is a maximal split torus,
x\in A(G,S^{\prime})
,
\mu\in X_{*}(S^{\prime})
such that
Q=P_{G}(\mu)
, and
v\in V
, then for each
\alpha\in\Phi(G,S^{\prime})
, we have

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\langle\alpha,x+v+t\mu\rangle\underset{t\to+\infty}{\longrightarrow}+\infty&\text{ if }&\langle\alpha,\mu\rangle>0\end{array}

and

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\langle\alpha,x+v+t\mu\rangle\underset{t\to+\infty}{\longrightarrow}\langle\alpha,x\rangle&\text{ if }&\langle\alpha,\mu\rangle=0\end{array},

and therefore
p(x+v)=p(x)
. The map
p
is thus invariant under
V
and, by the above discussion, there exists a map

\overline{p}:\mathcal{B}(L_{Q},k)\to\mathcal{B}(G,k)_{F}

such that
p
admits the factorisation
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{Q}(k)\cdot A(G,S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(G,k)_{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(L_{Q},k)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\pi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{p}}

.

The map
\overline{p}
is surjective because
p
is, and
L_{Q}(k)
-equivariant because
p
and
\widetilde{\pi}
are
L_{Q}(k)
-equivariant and surjective. The restriction of
\overline{p}
to the apartment
A(L_{Q},S)
is a surjective affine map between affine spaces of the same dimension, and therefore one-to-one. The
L_{Q}(k)
-equivariance of
\overline{p}
then implies that its restriction to each apartment is one-to-one, and therefore that
\overline{p}
is globally one-to-one – as any two points in
\mathcal{B}(L_{Q},k)
are contained in an apartment – and finally that
\overline{p}
is bijective.
To prove that
\overline{p}
is a homeomorphism, we first observe that the restriction of
\overline{p}
to
A(L_{Q},S)
, a bijective affine map, is a homeomorphism onto its image. Next, we recall that if
x\in A(L_{Q},S)
, then, by
[
Sol18
, Theorem 3.11 (d) and (e)]
, we have

\mathcal{B}(L_{Q},k)=L_{Q}(k)_{x}\cdot A(L_{Q},S).

Further, because
Z(L_{Q})(k)
acts trivially on the building
\mathcal{B}(L_{Q},k)
and is contained and cocompact in the stabiliser
L_{Q}(k)_{x}
by
[
Sol18
, Proposition 3.9 (a) and (b)]
, we may write

\mathcal{B}(L_{Q},k)=\underbrace{\frac{L_{Q}(k)_{x}}{Z(L_{Q})(k)}}_{\text{compact}}\cdot A(L_{Q},S).

Now, because
\bar{p}
is
L_{Q}(k)
-equivariant, the action of
Z(Q)(k)
on
\mathcal{A}(G,S)_{f}
is also trivial and we also have

\mathcal{B}(G,k)_{F}=\frac{L_{Q}(k)_{x}}{Z(L_{Q})(k)}\cdot A(G,S)_{f}.

Let
(u_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of points in
\mathcal{B}(L_{Q},k)
that converges to a point
u\in\mathcal{B}(L_{Q},k)
and, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, write
u_{n}=k_{n}x_{n}
with
k_{n}\in L_{Q}(k)_{x}/Z(L_{Q})(k)
and
x_{n}\in A(G,S)
. Let
\varphi:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
be an increasing function. Then, because
L_{Q}(k)_{x}/Z(L_{Q})(k)
is compact, we may extract from
k_{\varphi(n)}
a subsequence
k_{\varphi\circ\psi(n)}
that converges to a point
k\in L_{Q}(k)_{x}/Z(L_{Q})(k)
. Then, the sequence
x_{\varphi\circ\psi(n)}
converges to
x=k^{-1}u
and, by continuity of the
L_{Q}(k)
-action and of the restriction of
\overline{p}
to
A(G,S)
, we may write

\overline{p}(u_{\varphi\circ\psi(n)})=k_{\varphi\circ\psi(n)}\overline{p}(x_{\varphi\circ\psi(n)})\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}k\overline{p}(x)=\overline{p}(u).

We have thus proved that, from any subsequence of
(\overline{p}(u_{n}))
we may extract a subsubsequence that converges to
\overline{p}(u)
, hence
(\overline{p}(u_{n}))
converges to
\overline{p}(u)
. Hence, the map
\overline{p}
is continuous. Because the above argument only relied on
L_{Q}(k)
-equivariance and the continuity of the restriction to an apartment, it also applies to
(\overline{p})^{-1}
, hence
\overline{p}
is a homeomorphism.

Lastly, we check that
\overline{p}
is simplicial, which amounts to proving that it maps walls in
\mathcal{B}(L_{Q},k)
to walls in
\mathcal{B}(G,k)_{F}
. By
L_{Q}(k)
-equivariance, it is sufficient to check this property in our distinguished apartment
A(L_{Q},S)
. By
[
Cha10
, Section 3.3.4]
, the walls in
A(G,S)_{f}
are the images under
p
of the walls in
A(G,S)
whose direction contains the cone
\vec{f}
or, equivalently the subspace
