V=\Span_{\mathbb{R}}(\vec{f})
. Now, if
H\subset A(G,S)
is a wall, then we have
\overline{p}(H)=p(\widetilde{\pi}^{-1}(H))
. By
[
BT72
, Proposition 7.6.4 (ii)]
, the affine subspace
\widetilde{\pi}^{-1}(H)
is a wall of
A(G,S)
and its direction contains
V
, as the linear part of
\widetilde{\pi}
is the quotient map
V(G,S)\twoheadrightarrow V(L_{Q},S)=V(G,S)/V
. Hence, the affine subspace
\overline{p}(H)
is a wall in
A(G,S)_{f}
, which completes the proof.

∎

Relevant pseudo-parabolic subgroups

We have thus proved that the stratification of
\overline{\vartheta_{P}(\mathcal{B}(G,k))}
is indexed by pseudo-parabolic subgroups of
G
. In this last section, we characterise those pseudo-parabolic subgroups that give rise to a stratum in terms of
P
, thereby completing our description.

Fix a maximal split torus
S
of
G
and a minimal pseudo-parabolic subgroup
P_{0}
such that
S\subset P_{0}\subset P
. Let
\Phi=\Phi(G,S)
be the root system associated to
S
and
\Delta\subset\Phi
be the system of simple roots corresponding to
P_{0}
. We endow
\Delta
with its Dynkin diagram structure. All subsets of
\Delta
thus inherit an induced graph structure. Denote by
J
the type of
P
, that is the set of simple roots
J=\Delta\cap-\Phi(P,S)
. We have proved in Section
4.2
that
\overline{\vartheta_{P}(\mathcal{B}(G,k))}
is the polyhedral compactification in the sense of
[
Cha10
]
associated with the fan
\mathcal{F}^{J}
of
[
Cha10
, Définition 2.5.2]
.

In keeping with the terminology of
[
RTW10
]
, we give the following definition:

Definition 58
.

Let
Q
be a pseudo-parabolic subgroup in
G
. By
[
CP17
, Theorem 5.1.3 and Proposition 5.1.4 (iii)]
, there exists a unique pseudo-parabolic subgroup
Q
in the conjugacy class of
Q
such that
P_{0}\subset Q
. Then, we say that
Q
is
J
-relevant
if there exists
I\subset\Delta
, none of whose connected components is contained in
J
, such that the type of
Q^{\prime}
is
I\sqcup(J\cap I^{\perp})
.

Then, we have

Proposition 59
.

Let
Q
be a pseudo-parabolic subgroup in
G
. The following are equivalent:

1.

The subgroup
Q
is
J
-relevant.

2.

There exists a cone
F
in
(\mathcal{F}^{J})_{\mathcal{B}(G,k)}
such that
Q(k)
is the stabiliser in
G(k)
of the stratum
\mathcal{B}(G,k)_{F}
.

Proof.

Because both statements are invariant under
G(k)
-conjugacy, we may assume that
Q
contains
P_{0}
. Then, the equivalence essentially follows from the second example in
[
Cha10
, Section 3.3.2]
. Namely, the proof of Proposition
54
and Proposition
56
imply that
Q
is the stabiliser of a façade
\mathcal{B}(G,k)_{F}
for some
F\in(\mathcal{F}^{J})_{\mathcal{B}(G,k)}
if and only if, for any apartment
A(G,S^{\prime})
with
S^{\prime}\subset Q
, the Weyl facet

\vec{\delta}=\{x\in V(G,S^{\prime})\,|\,\forall\alpha\in\Phi(Q,S^{\prime}),\langle\alpha,x\rangle\geq 0\}

is the direction of the core of an affine cone in
(\mathcal{F}^{J})_{A(G,S^{\prime})}
.

Now, by
[
Cha10
, Section 3.3.2]
, the directions of the cores of the cones in
(\mathcal{F}^{J})_{A(G,S)}
are precisely the Weyl facets whose type is of the form
I\cup(J\cap I^{\perp})
for some
I\subset\Delta
none of whose connected component is contained in
J
, from which we deduce both implications.
∎

We have thus proved the following theorem.

Theorem 60
.

The compactification
\overline{\vartheta_{P}(\mathcal{B}(G,k))}
is
G(k)
-equivariantly homeomorphic to the disjoint union

\overline{\vartheta_{P}(\mathcal{B}(G,k))}=\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\subset G\\
Q\,J\text{-relevant pseudo-parabolic}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{B}(Q/\mathcal{R}_{us,k}(Q),k).

For each
J
-relevant pseudo-parabolic subgroup
Q
of
G
, the group
Q(k)
is the stabiliser of the stratum
\mathcal{B}(Q/\mathcal{R}_{us,k}(Q),k)
.

For the sake of completeness, we now include the comparison between our definition of
J
-relevant and the characterisation given in
[
RTW10
, Proposition 3.24 (ii)]
, thereby proving that the terminology is consistent in the reductive case.

Proposition 61
.

Let
Q
be a pseudo-parabolic subgroup of
G
containing
P_{0}
. Denote by
T\subset\Delta
its type. Endow
T
with the graph structure induced by the Dynkin diagram structure on
\Delta
. Let
\widetilde{T}
be the union of the connected components of
T
that meet
\Delta\setminus J
. The following are equivalent:

1.

Q
is
J
-relevant

2.

For any simple root
\alpha\in\Delta
, if
\alpha\in J
and
\alpha\perp\widetilde{T}
, then
\alpha\in T
.

Proof.

Assume that
Q
is
J
-relevant. Then, there exists
I
such that
T=I\sqcup(J\cap I^{\perp})
. Observe that
I
and
J\cap I^{\perp}
are disconnected for the graph structure on
T
, so that the connected components of
T
are precisely those of
I
and those of
J\cap I^{\perp}
. Then, because no connected component of
I
is contained in
J
, the connected components of
T
that meet
\Delta\setminus J
are precisely the connected components of
I
. Therefore, a simple root
\alpha
that is in
J
and orthogonal to
\widetilde{T}=I
is in
J\cap I^{\perp}
and thus in
T
.

Conversely, if
T
satisfies condition 2, then setting
I=\widetilde{T}
, no connected component of
I
is contained in
J
by construction, and we have
T=I\sqcup(J\cap I^{\perp})
. Indeed, condition 2 immediately implies that
J\cap I^{\perp}\subset T
. On the other hand, if
\alpha\in T\setminus I
then, because
I
is a union of connected components, we have
\alpha\in I^{\perp}
. Moreover, by construction,
T\setminus I
is the union of the connected components of
T
that are contained in
J
, hence
\alpha\in J
, hence
\alpha\in J\cap I^{\perp}
, hence
T\subset I\sqcup(J\cap I^{\perp})
, which completes the proof.
∎

5
Appendix: Levi subgroups and their inner façades

In the following, we fix a quasi-reductive group
G
over a discretely valued field
k
with a perfect residue field. Let
S
be a
k
-split torus and
G_{1}=Z_{G}(S)
be its centraliser.
In this section, we relate the Bruhat-Tits building of
G_{1}
to that of the ambient group
G
, which essentially amounts to rephrasing the results of
[
BT72
, §7.6]
in our particular context.

The inner façade

Observe that the apartments in the Bruhat-Tits building
\mathcal{B}(G_{1},k)
are in one-to-one correspondence with the maximal
k
-split tori of
G_{1}
, that is the maximal
k
-split tori of
G
that contain
S
. Therefore, a maximal
k
-split torus
T
of
G
containing
S
gives rise to two apartments, namely
A(G,T)
and
A(G_{1},T)
, and two root systems
\Phi=\Phi(G,T)
, and
\Phi_{1}=\Phi(G_{1},T)
. Moreover, these apartments are related by the map

\pi:A(G,T)\twoheadrightarrow A(G_{1},T)

that associates to each valuation
\varphi=(\varphi_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\Phi}
in
A(G,T)
the family
\varphi_{1}=(\varphi_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\Phi_{1}}
, which is established in
[
BT72
, Proposition 7.6.3]
to be a valuation of the generating root data
(Z_{G}(T)(k),(U_{\alpha}(k))_{\alpha\in\Phi_{1}})
in
G_{1}(k)
. The map
\pi
is well-defined as it preserves equipollence, affine, and its linear part is the quotient map

V(G,T)=\frac{X_{*}(T)_{\mathbb{R}}}{\Span_{\mathbb{R}}(\Phi)^{\perp}}\twoheadrightarrow V(G_{1},T)=\frac{X_{*}(T)_{\mathbb{R}}}{\Span_{\mathbb{R}}(\Phi_{1})^{\perp}}.

Further, if
T
is a maximal
k
-split torus in
G_{1}
, then the subspace
\mathcal{B}(G_{1},G,k)
defined as

\mathcal{B}(G_{1},G,k)=G_{1}(k)\cdot A(G,T)=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}T^{\prime}\text{ max. }k\text{-split torus of }G\\
S\subset T^{\prime}\end{subarray}}A(G,T^{\prime})

may be thought of as a Euclidean extension of the Bruhat-Tits building of
G_{1}
, in the following sense.
By
[
BT72
, Proposition 7.6.4]
, there exists a unique
G_{1}(k)
-equivariant map

\widetilde{\pi}:\mathcal{B}(G_{1},G,k)\to\mathcal{B}(G_{1},k)

that extends
\pi
. Moreover, it enjoys the following properties:

1.

We have
\widetilde{\pi}^{-1}(A(G_{1},T))=A(G,T)
, and the inverse image of each apartment of
\mathcal{B}(G_{1},k)
is an apartment in
\mathcal{B}(G,k)
.

2.

There exists a unique action of the vector space

V=\ker\big(V(G,T)\to V(G_{1},T)\big)=\frac{\Span_{\mathbb{R}}(\Phi_{1})^{\perp}}{\Span_{\mathbb{R}}(\Phi)^{\perp}}

on
\mathcal{B}(G_{1},G,k)
that extends the action of
V
on
A(G,T)
by translation and commutes with the action of
G_{1}(k)
in the sense that, for each
x\in\mathcal{B}(G_{1},G,k)
,
g\in G_{1}(k)
, and
v\in V
, we have

g\cdot(x+v)=g\cdot x+v.

Then, the map
\widetilde{\pi}
is constant on
V
-orbits and factors through a bijection
\mathcal{B}(G_{1},G,k)/V\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{B}(G_{1},k)
.

To understand the action of
V
more concretely, note that we have

V=\frac{X_{*}(Z(G_{1}))\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}}{X_{*}(Z(G))\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}}.

Hence,
V
naturally sits inside
V(G,T^{\prime})
for each maximal
k
-split torus
T^{\prime}
containing
S
. In particular, the vector space
V
acts on each
A(G,T^{\prime})
by translation. Then, the action of
V
on
\mathcal{B}(G_{1},G,k)
restricts to the translation action on
A(G,T^{\prime})
for each maximal
k
-split torus
T^{\prime}
in
G_{1}
.

Remark 62
.

With the terminology of
[
Rou20
]
, the subspace
\mathcal{B}(G_{1},G,k)
endowed with the polysimplicial structure given by the affine roots with direction in
\Phi_{1}
is an inessential affine building
[
Rou20
, 2.3.4]
. It is the
inner façade
associated to the sector face support
V

[
Rou20
, 3.3.14]
. Point 2 translates in that language to the statement that the map
\widetilde{\pi}
realises the essentialisation map of
\mathcal{B}(G_{1},G,k)

[
Rou20
, 2.1.12]
.

Inner façades and field extensions

For each discretely valued extension
K/k
with perfect residue field, we set

\mathcal{B}(G_{1},G,K):=\mathcal{B}((G_{1})_{K},G_{K},K)=G_{1}(K)\cdot A(G_{K},T)

for
T
any maximal
K
-split torus of
G_{K}
containing
S_{K}
.

In the case where
K/k
is Galois, we establish that the action of
\Gal(K/k)
on
\mathcal{B}(G,K)
stabilises
\mathcal{B}(G_{1},G,K)
, and that both the essentialisation map and the
V
-action are compatible with the Galois action.

Proposition 63
.

Let
K/k
be a Galois extension of discretely valued fields with perfect residue fields. Let
T
be a maximal
K
-split torus containing
S_{K}
. Let

V^{K}=\ker(V(G_{K},T)\to V((Z_{G}(S))_{K},T)).

Then

1.

The action of
\Gal(K/k)
on
\mathcal{B}(G,K)
stabilises
\mathcal{B}(G_{1},G,K)
.

2.

The map
\widetilde{\pi}_{K}:\mathcal{B}(G_{1},G,K)\to\mathcal{B}(G_{1},K)
is
\Gal(K/k)
-equivariant.

3.

The action of
V^{K}
on
\mathcal{B}(G_{1},G,K)
commutes with the action of
\Gal(K/k)
in the following sense: For each
v\in V^{K}
, each
x\in\mathcal{B}(G_{1},G,K)
, and each
\sigma\in\Gal(K/k)
, we have

\sigma(x+v)=\sigma(x)+\sigma(v).

Proof.

1.

This is clear as the action of
\Gal(K/k)
stabilises the set of maximal
K
-split tori containing
S_{K}
.

2.

Let
\sigma\in\Gal(K/k)
and consider the automorphisms

p_{\sigma}:\mathcal{B}(G,K)\to\mathcal{B}(G,K)

and

p^{G_{1}}_{\sigma}:\mathcal{B}(G_{1},K)\to\mathcal{B}(G_{1},K).

Then, the map
p_{\sigma}^{G_{1}}\circ\widetilde{\pi}^{K}\circ p_{\sigma}^{-1}
is
G(K)
-equivariant and restricts to an affine map from
A(G_{K},T)
to
A((G_{1})_{K},T)
. Moreover, its restriction to
A(G_{K},T)
is the map

\pi^{K}:A(G_{K},T)\to A((G_{1})_{K},T)

described in the previous section. Indeed, if we fix a valuation
(\varphi_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\Phi(G_{K},T)}
corresponding to a point in
A(G_{K},T)
, then it follows from Proposition
2
that
p_{\sigma}\circ\widetilde{\pi}^{K}\circ(p^{G_{1}}_{\sigma})^{-1}
maps
(\varphi_{\alpha})
to
(\varphi_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\Phi((G_{1})_{K},T)}
.
Moreover, it follows from Point 2 of Theorem 1 that the linear part of
p_{\sigma}\circ\widetilde{\pi}^{K}\circ(p^{G_{1}}_{\sigma})^{-1}
is the same as that of
\pi^{K}
, which completes the proof. We thus find that

p_{\sigma}^{G_{1}}\circ\widetilde{\pi}^{K}=\widetilde{\pi}^{K}\circ p_{\sigma}.

3.

This follows directly from the fact that the action of
\Gal(K/k)
on each apartment is affine and that
V^{K}
acts on each apartment by translation.

∎
